- Each sign-in gets its own session (12 hours, or 30 idle minutes: `ZSCALER_SSO_IDLE_MINUTES`); the cookie holds only a random session id. The page shows who is signed in, with **Sign out** (`POST /api/auth/logout`, audited). A restart of the app signs everyone out.
- The static UI token is refused in every form (header, Bearer, cookie, `?token=`, unlock), and is no longer printed or written to `ui_token.txt`: scripts that sent it must sign in through the browser instead. SSO on with `ZSCALER_UI_AUTH=0` now still requires sign-in.
- `ZSCALER_SSO_ALLOWED_GROUPS` limits who may sign in; `ZSCALER_SSO_WRITERS` (groups or e-mails) limits who may deploy, remove, import, undo, apply or roll back proposals, run fix recipes or migrations, or turn write mode on. Everyone else can still assess, read and preview. The claim name is `ZSCALER_SSO_GROUPS_CLAIM` (default `groups`); SECURITY.md says how to get Entra ID, Okta, Keycloak and Google to send it. Unset: everyone signed in may write, as before.

**For every install**
- **Who did what.** Every audit entry names the actor: the signed-in e-mail under SSO, otherwise "local UI token". Proposal decisions, applies and rollbacks take "by" from the server, not from what the page sends. A failed audit write is reported on stderr instead of being ignored.
- **What a deploy actually did.** Every background deploy, removal or Terraform job writes a `job_finished` entry when it ends (who, tenant, approved plan, succeeded / failed / cancelled, counts, activation, duration), and every object Zscaler Made Easy creates, updates or deletes on a tenant writes an `object_write` entry (type, id, name, result, ZIA's error). Runs started without a job add a `run_finished` summary. Secrets and request bodies are never recorded; an audit failure never fails the deploy.
- **The container decides from where it is published.** `docker-compose.yml`, the by-hand `docker run` and CI no longer set `ZSCALER_ALLOW_NONLOCAL_BIND`. Published on loopback (`DLP_CHECK_HOST_BIND`, the default) it starts as before; published wider without SSO it refuses to start and says both ways out. A local install bound beyond loopback with `ZSCALER_UI_AUTH=0` is always refused, override or not.
- **A warning banner on every page** while write mode is on ("this app can change your Zscaler tenant", with **Back to read-only**) or while the UI token gate is off. Deploy Studio shows write mode in amber instead of a green "ok", and Advanced settings flags "UI token gate: Off" as a warning.
- **Checked** in a container built from this change: healthy on the default loopback publish without the override, `sso:false`, the banner is on the page, turning write mode on is audited as "local UI token", and publishing on 0.0.0.0 without SSO refuses to start.

### The container image: from 70 vulnerabilities to 16, none critical
- **What you asked for.** "Docker is showing a lot of vulnerabilities in the container; scan and fix."
- **What the scan found.** Docker Scout on the image built from main: 70 vulnerabilities in 18 packages, including 2 critical and several high in perl, util-linux, openssl and zlib. Every one was in a Debian 12 (bookworm) package Debian has not fixed; none were in the app's own Python libraries.
- **The fix.** The image is built on Debian 13 (`python:3.11-slim-trixie`) and removes every operating-system package the app never uses: apt, perl, tar, diffutils, util-linux and mount, the login and password tools, the systemd libraries, sqlite and the openssl command. The app runs Python only, so nothing it does changes; the build stops if one of those packages is still there, and checks that Python, TLS and compression still load. Nothing can install packages into the running image any more.
- **What is left.** 16: one high in zlib (Python needs it; Debian has no fix yet), one medium and 14 low in glibc, dash, coreutils and OpenSSL's library. No fixable critical or high. Checked in the rebuilt container: healthy, the pages and API answer, the Log Analyzer reads a gzip log, and HTTPS through the Zscaler certificate works.
- **To get it.** Rebuild: `update_container.ps1 -Force` / `./update_container.sh --force`, or the Docker-only build line in INSTALL.md.

### The review of the rate-limit check and the CISA Secure by Design assessment: what they confirmed, fixed
- **What you asked for.** Finish last night's review of the rate-limit check and the assessment against CISA's *Secure by Design*, then fix what they confirm. 100 reviewers found 76 distinct issues; the top 30 were checked by someone trying to refute them, and 17 held up. Twenty assessors judged the app against 33 CISA practices; of the top 30 gaps, 18 held up. All 17 review findings and 12 of the 18 gaps are fixed here; the other 6 change how sign-in and roles work and wait for a decision.

**The rate-limit check**
- Every deploy, removal, import, undo and proposal change counts against its own tenant's ZIA limits, also when started straight from the API. One tenant's block no longer turns another tenant's check into "Likely to fail".
- A block in an old record that names no tenant (from before tenants were kept apart) is a warning that says so, never a failure, and no longer delays other tenants. An old state file's pause is no longer reported as "Resource Access Blocked".
- The check says "Likely to fail" when a `DLP_CHECK_ZIA_LIMIT_*` setting paces more loosely than ZIA allows. After a 429 it no longer promises a start time the run will not keep. A removal preview gives removal advice, and the sarcastic opener quotes the same block as the verdict.
- Blocks are recorded more reliably: a block right after a 429 is kept, every refusal during a write's retries is kept, and blocks saved by another process (the command-line assessment, say) show up in the next check without a restart.
- A pause set by another run while a write waits is also waited out, and the pause after a refusal covers ZIA's own window when the setting is shorter.
- The state file is written far less often (at most every 5 seconds for ZIA's rate headers, at once for a block), so busy assessments are no longer slowed by it. A stuck lock file can no longer hang the app, and a failed save leaves no temp files behind.

**Secure by Design**
- The pack library preview escapes everything it shows, and an uploaded pack whose proximity is not a whole number is refused: a shared pack file can no longer run script in the app.
- The Log Analyzer reads `.gz` files as a stream and stops at the size limit, so a small compressed file can no longer use gigabytes of memory; gzip counts toward the file limits like ZIP and tar. Analysis runs off the server's main loop, so a large upload no longer holds up other requests.
- Rejected UI tokens and failed SSO sign-ins go to the audit log (`auth_denied`, `sso_login_failed`); the token itself is never recorded.
- The Terraform hand-off creates every rule DISABLED, as Deploy Policy Packs does, even when an uploaded pack says ENABLED or gives no state.
- Error text no longer shows any part of a password, client secret or API key, nor its length: `[redacted <field>]`.
- Digest e-mail checks the mail server's certificate and refuses to send the SMTP password with TLS turned off; the Secure by Design page warns when it is off.
- The launcher, the env example and the TLS error text say to trust the proxy's root certificate (`REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE`, or `certs/` for the container); switching verification off is a last resort, with its risk spelled out.
- The optional MCP server works again with the pinned mcp 2.x (an incompatible release now fails the tests instead of being skipped), and no longer accepts a client ID or secret as a tool argument: credentials come only from the `ZSCALER_*` environment variables, so a secret never passes through the AI conversation.
- In-place updates cross-check the private repository with `DLP_CHECK_GITHUB_TOKEN`. When that cannot run, the update says "Verify against GitHub: skipped" and why, and the page no longer reloads over the warning.
- The Secure by Design score is truthful: it names the fix recipes and the rule migrator, which write to the tenant directly (still only in write mode), shows a control it cannot inspect as not scored, and counts plain-text SMTP against the TLS control.
- A check that crashed, returned nothing usable or timed out is "Could not be checked", not "Not configured", in the app and the command line; the command line also says how many checks were assessed and warns on partial coverage.

**Waiting for a decision** (they change sign-in and roles): the UI token still works beside SSO; the container's non-loopback override; the audit log's identity and self-typed approver; viewers and writers under SSO; durable logging of background job outcomes; an app-wide banner when auth is off, write mode is on or the app is exposed.

### Fresh container install: one set of steps for Windows, Mac and Linux
- **What you asked for.** "What do I type to pull down the docker image and load it for a fresh install like I have never done it before", and "the instructions should account for a fresh install of a container on mac windows or linux".
- **INSTALL.md, *Run it in a container instead*.** Opens with a short copy-paste version for each system and says plainly that there is no image to pull: Docker builds it from the code. Step 2 now clones with git on each system (with the GitHub sign-in each one asks for) instead of a ZIP, because the update script only works in a git clone. Step 3 builds with the update script (`-Force` / `--force`), which stamps the build commit so the update notice works from day one, and then `docker logs dlp-check` gives the token. Stop, start and *start over from scratch* (`docker compose down -v`, with the warning that it deletes saved data) are spelled out.
- **README.md, *Quick start (container)*.** The same commands for Windows and for Mac or Linux. Plus the Docker-only build.
- **Docker commands only, no git.** You asked: "I should be able to use the docker command line and put the command in there to download the files from GH and install". New section *Install with Docker commands only*: Docker fetches the private repository itself (`docker build … "https://github.com/berserkerjay/DLP-Check.git#main"`, with a read-only GitHub token passed as a build secret, so it never lands in the image or the shell history), then the same `docker volume create` and `docker run` as the by-hand section.
- **Dockerfile: TLS-inspection certificates for a build from GitHub.** A build from the URL has no local `certs/` folder, so the Zscaler root certificate had nowhere to go and pip failed with `CERTIFICATE_VERIFY_FAILED`. The certificates now come from a `certs` build stage that is the `certs/` folder by default and any folder with `--build-context certs=<folder>`. Builds from a clone behave as before.

### Every deploy and delete shows the rate-limit check in its popup, and the app stops crushing ZIA's read limit itself
- **What you asked for.**
  - "The popup warning didn't tell me my API plan and success rate, and my sarcastic comment if I was crushing Zscaler's API rates."
  - "I want to see the sample warning."
  - "Should apply for any deploy or delete."
- **The popup.** Every confirmation now opens with the rate-limit check:
  - the verdict, with its sarcastic opener when ZIA's limits bite;
  - the **API plan**: each kind of request with how many, ZIA's limit, how many were already sent this hour and how long it takes, starting with when the run can begin if there is a wait first;
  - the **success outlook**: good, good but slow, or poor. It comes with this tenant's **track record**: how many of its deploys and removals in the last week finished without ZIA refusing them for its rate.
- **Where.**
  - Pack deploy: every time, not only on a warning. The approve screen shows the check too.
  - Removal: packs, a deployment, or selected rules.
  - The Evaluate All Rules migration: post, delete-all and enable.
  - Both remediation paths, and applying or rolling back a proposal.

  A new endpoint, `/api/rate-check`, gives any of them the check without sending anything to ZIA.
- **What was really crushing ZIA's API.** The container's own record for the last hour: 66 writes, 14 Endpoint DLP rules and 223 reads. ZIA answered with 14 × "Rate Limit (2/SECOND) exceeded", 4 × "Rate Limit (1/SECOND) exceeded", then "Resource Access Blocked".
  - **Reads.** The assessment client spaced its requests 0.15 s apart, 6 to 7 a second, and nothing paced the SDK's reads. ZIA's read limit is 2 a second.
  - **Writes.** Writes paced at exactly one a second still reached ZIA two in one second.
- **Now:**
  - every ZIA request on a tenant's session, from the SDK or the assessment, is paced before it goes out: reads at 2 a second, and writes at their limits unless the caller has just paced them;
  - per-second limits keep a 25% margin (`DLP_CHECK_ZIA_SECOND_MARGIN`, default 1.25);
  - reads do not wait for a hold on writes, so previews still work.
- **Verified.**
  - Tests cover the API plan and track record (only this tenant's runs), the popups on every path, `/api/rate-check`, and reads paced at 2 per 1.25 s. A write paced by its caller is not paced twice, an unpaced write is paced at the session, and a read does not wait for a hold.
  - The sample popup was rendered by the app's own `zdRateText` from the container's real record.

### When a run is crushing Zscaler's API rate limits, the check says so, sarcastically
- **What you asked for.** "I want the wording to be sarcastic if we are crushing Zscaler's API rate limits."
- **Now**, when the rate-limit check is not a plain *Will succeed*, the verdict opens with a line that gives ZIA's limits the respect they have earned. The facts follow unchanged: what ZIA did, the traffic that led to it, and when to run. For example: *Zscaler's API, a global cloud platform, needs a lie-down after 110 writes. Likely to fail: ZIA blocked this API client…*
- **Which line.** Each case has its own lines: blocked, the remembered ceiling, still resetting, close to the ceiling, and long pauses. The line is picked by the hour, so a refresh does not change the wording.
- **The run log.** When ZIA refuses a write mid-run, the log gets a line too.
- **Plain wording.** A clean *Will succeed* stays plain, and `DLP_CHECK_RATE_TONE=plain` turns the sarcasm off everywhere.

### The preflight checks whether a run will succeed at ZIA's rate limits, from what the app actually executed
- **What you asked for.** "Preflight checks should be where I get the warning, based on previous API calls and the limits: you succeed or epically fail, based on Zscaler's weak infra." Then: "look at the app here and based on what was executed as well."
- **What the app executed tonight.** The container's own record for the deploy started at 04:40 UTC:
  - 110 writes in about 3 minutes, at about one a second, then ZIA answered "Resource Access Blocked" and the run went into a one-hour hold;
  - that is far below the documented 400 an hour;
  - an assessment and benchmarks were reading through the same API client at the same time.

  ZIA's real tolerance on this tenant is lower than its documentation.
- **Now** every preflight and removal preview opens with a **Rate-limit check**, right under the headline:
  - **Will succeed**: the run goes straight through, with how long it takes;
  - **Will succeed, with pauses**: the limit is still resetting, or the hourly limit is nearly used, with how long and why;
  - **Likely to fail**, in two cases:
    - ZIA refused this API client in the last hour: the check says when, what ZIA answered, how much traffic led to it, and when to run instead (an hour after the refusal);
    - ZIA once blocked this client at less traffic than this run sends in its busiest 5 minutes.

  When it is not *Will succeed*, the verdict is also the first warning.
- **What the check is based on.**
  - **Every ZIA request the app sends, reads included.** A hook on each tenant's session sees the SDK's and the assessment's requests alike: deploys, removals, preflights, assessments, benchmarks and continuous evaluation.
  - **ZIA's own answers.** Its `x-ratelimit-*` headers include other tools on the same client. Its rate refusals are kept for a week, each with the requests of the minute, 5 minutes and hour before it.
  - **Earlier records.** The deployment log's refused runs, and a hold recorded before refusal details were kept. Tonight's run counts, too.
- **Verified.**
  - Your container's state file, read without changing it, gives **Likely to fail** for every pack until 05:43 UTC, an hour after the refusal. Its detail reads: "In the last hour Zscaler Made Easy sent ZIA 110 writes (POST/PUT). Most traffic ZIA refused at: 110 requests in 5 minutes."
  - Tests cover that state, with the ceiling remembered after the hour. They also cover:
    - the tap, and observing reads, headers and refusals (a permission 403 is not a rate refusal);
    - the deployment-log history;
    - the preflight warning, with the forecast kept out of the plan hash.
- **Review, before release.** 10 reviewers and 2 independent checkers per finding confirmed 22 findings in this check. All are fixed:
  - **Nothing freezes.** Pacing no longer sleeps while holding the rate lock, so a paced write never holds up another thread's ZIA request or a preview's forecast. A hold set by another run while this one waits is also honoured.
  - **One record per tenant and API client.** The key is a hash of cloud, tenant and client ID. A refusal on one tenant no longer makes another tenant's preflight say *Likely to fail*. Answers are seen by a hook on that tenant's own session, the SDK's and the assessment's, instead of a process-wide wrapper around every HTTP request.
  - **Refusals are classified.** A client-wide "Resource Access Blocked" in the last hour is *Likely to fail*. A 429 on an endpoint this run uses is a pause, for as long as ZIA's reset timer runs. A 429 on a read never affects a deploy.
  - **A like-for-like ceiling.** What ZIA tolerated comes only from client-wide blocks and counts writes on both sides; the reads are still shown.
  - **The simulation.** Buckets are simulated in the order a run sends them. ZIA's header count adds to the app's own hourly record instead of replacing it. A pause is measured against the same run from a quiet hour at the documented limits.
  - **The state file.** Processes merge into it under a lock file, using a unique temp file. The record from before tenants were told apart keeps its inferred refusal after its hold ends, and it applies to every tenant.
  - **Times.** They are shown in UTC, and the card adds your browser's local time.
  - **No card on a blocked plan.**
  - **Tests.** They now use the documented limits instead of pacing turned off. The permission-403 check can fail when it should.

### Each pack shows how it measures against ZIA's rate limits, and every preview says whether the run will go straight through
- **What you asked for.** "Make a note on each pack how it measures against the rate limit for a deployment or delete, and give a notice to the user based on all recent transactions if it will succeed."
- **On each pack card**, a *Rate limits* line says, for a fresh hour, how many requests a full deploy sends and how long it takes at ZIA's limits, the same for a full removal, and whether any channel has more rules than ZIA allows in an hour. For example, ZBP Enterprise: *Full deploy: 77 requests, about 66s at ZIA's limits (Endpoint DLP rules go at 10/min and 40/hr). Removal: 76 deletes, about 75s.*
- **The preflight** has a *ZIA rate limits* card in *Impact on this tenant*. It counts what this deploy creates or changes per limit, plus its one activation, and checks that against the requests Zscaler Made Easy sent in the last hour. It then gives one of these verdicts:
  - *Goes straight through*, with the time it takes;
  - *Will succeed, but pauses for ZIA's hourly limit*, with how long and why (for example *Endpoint DLP rules: 38 of 40/hr already used in the last hour*);
  - the limit is still resetting from an earlier refusal, and when the run can start.
- **The removal preview** has the same card for its deletes and the one activation.
- **Recent requests survive a restart.** The requests of the last hour are kept in a small state file: `/data/zia_rate.json` in the container, or `zia_rate_state.json` next to the app, which you can set with `DLP_CHECK_ZIA_RATE_STATE`. A restart or a second run starts from the real count, not from zero. The forecast sees only what Zscaler Made Easy sent; another tool using the same API client is not visible, and the card says so.
- **Verified.** Tests cover:
  - the note on every pack;
  - a fresh hour going straight through;
  - 38 recent Endpoint rule writes turning the forecast into a pause with the reason;
  - more than an hourly limit flagged even from a fresh start;
  - an active hold;
  - the count surviving a restart, and requests older than an hour dropped;
  - the preflight and removal cards, with the forecast kept out of the plan hash.

  The full suite fails only the 8 tests that already fail on dev.

### A rate refusal pauses the whole run until the limit has completely reset
- **What you asked for.** "I wanted to create 565 objects, and after 5 minutes I started getting the 403 error. We need the timer on the limit to completely elapse before we reattempt, for every deployment or removal of a pack."
- **Before.** A 403 "Resource Access Blocked" was retried after 30 s, 1, 1.5, 2 and 2 minutes, and a 429 after a few seconds. Both are shorter than ZIA's windows (a minute or an hour), so the retries ran into the same limit again and added to the refusals.
- **Now**, during any deploy or removal, when ZIA refuses a write for its rate (429, or 403 "Resource Access Blocked"):
  - the **whole run pauses**, and nothing is sent, until that limit has **completely reset**;
  - the wait uses ZIA's reset timer when the answer carries one (`x-ratelimit-reset` or `Retry-After`). Otherwise it waits until every write this run counted has left the limit's window, an hour for the hourly limits;
  - the same write is then tried again, up to three full windows, after which the run stops with the reason;
  - the log says how long and why. Stop still ends the wait within seconds.
  - The run's one activation follows the same rule against the activation limit.
  - A plain permission 403 ("Forbidden") keeps its short retries, since time does not clear it.
- **Getting past the limit.** It cannot be reset or worked around from the app. The ways to go faster are a limit increase from Zscaler for the tenant, deploying one channel at a time, and reusing what already exists.
- **Verified.** Tests cover:
  - a blocked write waits one full window per refusal, never activates, and succeeds after the reset;
  - a limit that never clears stops the run after three windows, with the next object not sent;
  - a 429 after a block is waited out the same way;
  - Stop ends the wait at its first step;
  - the activation waits out its own limit.

  The all-pack regression test passes, and the full suite has no new failures.

### Policy packs activate once, and every write waits for ZIA's rate limits
- **What you asked for.**
  - "For the policy packs, don't activate until the entire deployment is complete or completely deleted."
  - Then: "these 403 errors are because there is a 10 POST per minute limit, and the activate equals a POST, and there is a 40 call limit per hour."
  - Then: "make sure there isn't a rate limit for dictionaries, as that is where I see common errors."
- **Why the errors happened.** ZIA's API Rate Limit Summary (https://help.zscaler.com/legacy-apis/api-rate-limit-summary) gives every endpoint its own limit:
  - DLP dictionaries, engines, Web DLP, SaaS and Email DLP rules, File Type, URL and SSL rules, and endpoint application groups: 1 write a second, 400 an hour.
  - **Endpoint DLP rules, Cloud App Control rules and activations: 10 a minute, 40 an hour.**

  The app sent a pack's writes back to back and activated on every 403 retry and between removal tiers, each activation being one more of the 40 an hour. On this tenant's container, one run's dictionary create answered "Resource Access Blocked" and the next 26 dictionaries were never attempted: the dictionaries are the first and biggest burst of a deploy. The earlier "one write a second" pacing is replaced by these limits.
- **Now**
  - **Activate once.** A deploy activates once, when every object is written. A removal deletes rules, engines and dictionaries, then activates once. The 403 retry and the refused-delete retry only wait; they never activate.
  - **In-use deletes.** An engine or dictionary ZIA refuses as still "in use" by a rule this removal deleted is not retried before that activation (a retry cannot succeed, and refused requests get the client blocked). It is reported with *run the removal again*, and the second run removes it.
  - **Rate limits.** Every create, update, delete and activation waits for its endpoint's limit (`zia_rate.py`). Dictionaries go at most one a second. Endpoint DLP rules and Cloud App Control rules go at most 10 a minute and 40 an hour. When a limit is reached the log says so, for example *ZIA allows 10 Endpoint DLP rule writes a minute; waiting 42s (next at 21:47)*, instead of the request being refused.
  - **Settings.** Each limit is a setting: `DLP_CHECK_ZIA_LIMIT_WRITE`, `_DELETE`, `_ENDPOINT_RULES`, `_CLOUD_APP`, `_ACTIVATE`.
- **Verified.** Tests cover:
  - one activation per deploy and per removal, and none inside a retry;
  - an in-use engine left for a second run, which removes it;
  - dictionaries held to one a second, and the 11th Endpoint DLP rule in a minute waiting for the minute to pass.

  The all-pack regression test passes. The limits were read from the rendered Zscaler page.

### "Resource Access Blocked" is waited out, not rushed
- **What you asked for.** Keep activating on a 403, but wait longer.
- **Why.** On a 403 the app activated, waited 15 seconds and tried again, five times: 75 seconds in all. The "Resource Access Blocked" block on this tenant lasted about five minutes, so the run gave up while the block was still on.
- **Now**, when ZIA answers "Resource Access Blocked", the app still activates before each retry but waits 30 seconds, then 1, 1.5, 2 and 2 minutes: about 7 minutes in all before it stops. This applies to deploys, removals and the rule-migration wizard's deletes. Any other 403 keeps the 15-second waits. The run's progress line and the stop message say so.
- **Verified.** The 403 tests now expect the longer schedule for "Resource Access Blocked" and 15 seconds for other 403s. The all-pack regression test passes.

### Deploys and removals pace their writes
- **What you asked for.** "Why am I getting the resource block when I try to deploy or remove too much?"
- **Why.** A deploy or removal sent its 80–160 writes back to back, as fast as ZIA answered. Seen on this tenant: after a burst of requests, especially refused ones, ZIA's API gateway answers every request with 403 "Resource Access Blocked" for several minutes (about five), then lets the client back in. Zscaler does not publish the threshold.
- **Now**
  - Every create, update and delete waits until about a second has passed since the previous write (`DLP_CHECK_WRITE_GAP`, default 1.0; 0 turns pacing off). Reads are not paced. A whole pack takes one to three minutes longer; one channel, well under a minute.
  - Together with sending no request ZIA would refuse (the email-tenant gap, and a single rank-0 attempt per run) and the one-channel-at-a-time note, a run stays well below the burst that gets the client blocked.
- **Verified.** A test checks that writes are spaced a second apart, that reads are not, and that the setting turns pacing off. The rank test now expects one refused rank-0 attempt per run instead of one per SaaS rule. The all-pack regression test passes.

### Every pack deploys the way ZBP Enterprise did, and a regression test holds them to it
- **What you asked for.** "This finally worked; every pack needs to deploy like that", and regression testing for all packs.
- **Now** `tests/test_pack_regression.py` deploys every shipped pack one channel at a time (Web, Endpoint, SaaS, Email) against a fake tenant that answers the way the real one did:
  - its SaaS tenant list holds only SharePoint;
  - the Exchange email tenant is known only from DLP_Rule_1;
  - it refuses SaaS rules at rank 0;
  - it refuses Email rules without an email tenant.

  For all six packs, every channel deploys in full with as many rules as the pack has Web rules (cjis 14, fed 17, banking 13, healthcare 12, ZBP Enterprise 9, ZBP Source Code 14). No rule fails, and every Email rule is scoped to the Exchange tenant.
- **One refused request per run, not one per SaaS rule.** Every SaaS rule used to try rank 0 first, get refused, and then retry at rank 7: 9 refusals for ZBP and 17 for the Fed pack, exactly the kind of burst that gets the client blocked. Once ZIA refuses rank 0 in a run, the rest of that run's SaaS rules now start at the rank it accepted, and the log says so. The test fails without this change.
- **Verified live, read-only.** The preflight of every pack against the real tenant: every channel of every pack is create or reuse, none blocked, and the email tenant was found on DLP_Rule_1. ZBP Enterprise shows Web and Endpoint already in place, 3 SaaS and 9 Email rules to go.
### The assessment checks follow current Zscaler guidance and read the fields the APIs return
- **What you asked for.** Review the assessment checks against current Zscaler best practice and update the ones that are out of date.
- **Why.** Each check was compared with Zscaler's help pages, deployment guides and the official SDK and Terraform provider. Three kinds of problem came up. Some checks read field names the APIs do not return, so they reported a gap that did not exist or crashed. Some texts had wrong numbers: 30-day log retention, a 15-minute ZPA idle timeout, and "Windows 10 support ends Oct 2026". Some pointed at the wrong control, for example ECH in SSL rules or OCR per rule. No API path the app already reads was changed.
- **Now**
  - **SSL checks read the action object.**
    - **What changed:** 4.1, 4.3, 4.5, 4.13, 7.3 and E.2, plus AI.5 and AI.6, now read `action.type` (`DECRYPT` / `DO_NOT_DECRYPT` / `BLOCK`). They count enabled rules only, and 4.3 treats a rule scoped to cloud apps or users as not broad.
    - **Why:** an SSL Inspection rule's action is an object in the ZIA API, and reading it as a string crashed these checks ("Could not complete check").
    - **Source:** zscaler-sdk-python 1.11.0 `zia/models/ssl_inspection_rules.py`; https://github.com/zscaler/terraform-provider-zia/blob/master/docs/resources/zia_ssl_inspection_rules.md
  - **New check 4.14, Decrypt rule hardening.** Every enabled Decrypt rule should:
    - block untrusted server certificates;
    - run the OCSP revocation check;
    - block undecryptable traffic;
    - require at least TLS 1.2 for client and server.
    - A sub-action the API does not return is not held against the rule.
    - **Source:** https://help.zscaler.com/zscaler-deployments-operations/zia-ssl-inspection-leading-practices-guide ("Zscaler recommends blocking undecryptable traffic …", "… blocking all TLS versions earlier than 1.2").
  - **4.12, ECH handling.**
    - **What changed:** it now reads DNS Control rules and passes when a Block rule covers the HTTPS and SVCB record types. The old check passed any SSL rule containing the letters "ech".
    - **Source:** https://help.zscaler.com/zia/best-practices-dns-control-rules
  - **Client Connector app profiles are read with their policyExtension and platform policies folded in.** This is the same reading the strict Client Connector checks already use.
    - **What changed:** 3.2 (Install Endpoint DLP, also 5.1 and B.1) reads `enableZdpService`, and 3.3 reads `installCerts`. 1.7 reads `prioritizeDnsExclusions` from the app profile. 1.8, 1.9 and 1.10 read the folded profile. A setting the profiles do not report is now a review item, not a failure.
    - **Source:** zscaler-sdk-python 1.11.0 `zcc/models/webpolicy.py`
  - **1.6 (and 3.1, 3.6) finds Z-Tunnel 2.0.**
    - **What changed:** it reads the forwarding profile's `forwardingProfileActions`, where the mode actually is.
    - **Source:** zscaler-sdk-python 1.11.0 `zcc/models/forwardingprofile.py`; https://help.zscaler.com/zscaler-client-connector/best-practices-deploying-z-tunnel-2.0
  - **6.1 reads the device's `agentVersion`.**
    - **Why:** the old check read a field the device list does not have, so it always passed.
    - **Now:** it flags 3.x and older on Windows and macOS, while Linux 3.7.2 is still an LTS.
    - **Source:** zscaler-sdk-python 1.11.0 `zcc/models/devices.py`; https://help.zscaler.com/product-usage-terms/supported-versions
  - **6.7 no longer counts Windows 11 as Windows 10.**
    - **Why:** both report version 10.0, and Windows 11 is build 22000 or later.
    - **Now:** the text gives the real end of support, 14 October 2025, and Zscaler's limited Windows 10 support.
    - **Source:** https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/lifecycle/products/windows-10-home-and-pro; https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/release-health/windows11-release-information
  - **5.19, OCR.**
    - **What changed:** it reads the organisation-wide OCR switch in DLP Advanced Settings (`/webDlpGlobalOptions`, `enableInlineDlpOcr`, plus the SaaS Security API and Email switches).
    - **Why:** Zscaler deprecated the per-rule `ocrEnabled` flag. "If OCR options are not enabled, DLP rules don't apply to image files."
    - **Now:** when the setting is not readable, the check is informational rather than a per-rule finding.
    - **Source:** https://help.zscaler.com/zia/configuring-dlp-advanced-settings; terraform-provider-zia CHANGELOG 2.9.0; zscaler-sdk-python 1.11.0 `zia/web_dlp_global_options.py`
  - **5.20, Evaluate All Rules.**
    - **What changed:** the probe first reads `/webDlpGlobalOptions` (`enableEvaluateAllDlpRules`).
    - **Now:** the text says what Zscaler says. The most restrictive matching action wins. Changing the mode requires deleting the existing inline DLP rules. The account team enables it. The setting is at Data Security > Common Resources > DLP Advanced Settings.
    - **Source:** https://help.zscaler.com/zia/configuring-dlp-advanced-settings; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/configuring-dlp-policy-rules-evaluate-all-rules-mode-enabled
  - **5.12, 5.14 and 5.15 recognise EDM and IDM dictionaries.**
    - **What changed:** they read `dictionaryType` (`EXACT_DATA_MATCH`, `INDEXED_DATA_MATCH`) and `idmProfileMatchAccuracyDetails`. The IDM check read a field that does not exist. 5.12 now also lists IDM as a detection method.
    - **Source:** https://github.com/zscaler/terraform-provider-zia/blob/master/docs/resources/zia_dlp_dictionaries.md; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/about-exact-data-match; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/about-indexed-document-match
  - **8.1–8.4 (and B.6–B.9) read Endpoint DLP rules.**
    - **Why:** the Printing, Removable Storage, Network Share and Personal Cloud Storage channels are Endpoint DLP rules, which web DLP rules cannot cover. 8.4 also passed on any web rule, because the key `cloudApplications` contains "cloud".
    - **Now:** rule values are matched, not keys.
    - **Source:** https://help.zscaler.com/zia/about-endpoint-dlp
  - **12.9 accepts `%s{trig_dlprulename}` for the DLP rule name and asks for it.**
    - **Why:** `%s{dlprulename}` is filled only for Allow rules.
    - **Source:** https://help.zscaler.com/zia/nss-feed-output-format-web-logs
  - **Log retention texts.**
    - **What changed:** 12.11, E.15, AI.16 and USR.5 now say ZIA keeps transaction and audit logs for six months (180 days), not 30 days. ZPA.14 says ZPA keeps activity logs for 14 days without the Log Streaming Service.
    - **Source:** https://help.zscaler.com/zia/about-insights-logs; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/about-audit-logs; https://help.zscaler.com/zpa/about-log-streaming-service
  - **ZPA.7:** the default timeout rule is re-authenticate every 7 days with no idle timeout, not 15 minutes. **Source:** https://help.zscaler.com/zpa/about-timeout-policy
  - **Texts and names.**
    - 4.8 names the web DLP Confirm action and its User Confirmation Notification templates (https://help.zscaler.com/zia/about-user-confirmation-notification-templates).
    - FW.2 and FW.8 follow Zscaler's firewall guidance: Default Firewall Filtering Rule on Block at the lowest order, and Allow rules built from three or more conditions (https://help.zscaler.com/zia/best-practices-firewall-control-policy).
    - ZCC.18 recommends static trusted-network criteria (https://help.zscaler.com/zscaler-client-connector/configuring-trusted-networks-zscaler-client-connector).
    - AI.1 and AI.7 use the category's current name, "Generative AI and ML Applications" (https://www.zscaler.com/url-categorization/Generative-AI-and-ML-Applications).
    - 3.7 names Zero Trust Browser, formerly Browser Isolation (https://help.zscaler.com/zero-trust-browser/configuring-internet-and-saas-for-zero-trust-browser).
    - 5.22 and B.14 name Endpoint Data Scan (https://help.zscaler.com/zia/about-endpoint-data-scan), and MC.11 names Zscaler DSPM.
  - **The NSS best-practice link** in the library, `best-practices-deploying-nss-servers`, answers "page not found". It now points to https://help.zscaler.com/zia/deploying-nss-virtual-appliances.
  - **4.14 is registered in section 4.** It is listed in `DLP_Checks_Export.csv` and `.md`, and the SSL guidance topic and compliance mapping pick it up by prefix. No existing check id changed.
  - **Second review.** Twenty reviewers checked the checks again against the same kind of sources. What they found, and what changed:
    - **1.7 and 2.4 were the wrong way round (corrects the change above).** Zscaler says Prioritize DNS Exclusions over Z-Tunnel 2.0 "is enabled by default in new app profiles and is recommended". 1.7 now passes when it is on, raises a review item when it is off, and skips non-Windows profiles, since it applies only to Client Connector 4.6+ for Windows. 1.23 no longer warns about it. Source: https://help.zscaler.com/zscaler-client-connector/configuring-zscaler-client-connector-app-profiles
    - **Client Connector fields.**
      - 1.2–1.5 and 2.5–2.7 read the real bypass fields: `vpnGateways`, `packetTunnelIncludeList` / `ExcludeList`, `packetTunnelDnsExcludeList`, `sourcePortBasedBypasses`, `bypassAppIds`. 2.6 matches hostnames against the VPN gateway and domain exclusion lists. Sources: zscaler-sdk-python `zcc/models/webpolicy.py`; https://help.zscaler.com/zscaler-client-connector/best-practices-adding-bypasses-z-tunnel-2.0
      - 1.9 and 1.10 read the Windows `disable_password` / `uninstall_password` and skip inactive profiles. 1.9 reads strict enforcement from the devices. 1.8 says Strict Enforcement is an install option (STRICTENFORCEMENT=1). Sources: zscaler-sdk-python `zcc/_serialize.py`; https://help.zscaler.com/zscaler-client-connector/customizing-zscaler-client-connector-install-options-msi
      - 1.12 reads telemetry from the company settings (`enableGlobalZCCTelemetry`). 1.27 reads rollout, cleanup and FIPS from `webAppConfig`. Source: zscaler-sdk-python `zcc/models/company_info.py`
      - 3.3 reads `install_ssl_certs` on Windows and leaves out macOS, where the option does not work on Big Sur and later; 10.2 says so too. Source: https://help.zscaler.com/zscaler-client-connector/best-practices-zscaler-client-connector-deployment
      - 1.15 assesses anti-tampering on Windows profiles only. 1.16 no longer asks for a `ziaDisablePassword` field that does not exist. Sources: the app-profile help page above; zscaler-sdk-python `zcc/models/webpolicy.py`
      - 1.18: Drop QUIC Traffic is checked on iOS and Android (Z-Tunnel 1.0). For Windows and macOS the check looks for a Firewall Filtering rule that blocks QUIC or a DNS Control rule that blocks HTTPS records, and gives a review item, not a failure. Source: https://help.zscaler.com/zia/managing-quic-protocol
      - 1.24 and 1.25 read transport, TLS fallback, IPv6, PAC and private-IP settings per network from `forwardingProfileActions` and `systemProxyData`. 1.26 counts trusted-network criteria defined on the forwarding profile itself. Source: zscaler-sdk-python `zcc/models/forwardingprofile.py`
      - 6.1 and 6.9 compare versions per platform against the supported minimums: Windows 4.7, macOS 4.5.2, Linux 3.7.2 or 4.2+, iOS 4.4.4 and Android 4.0. Versions are compared as numbers, and builds of one release are grouped. Source: https://help.zscaler.com/product-usage-terms/supported-versions
      - ZCC.3 reads the fail-open list and its real fields, and no longer claims that Zscaler recommends fail-closed. ZCC.6 reads the per-network forwarding actions. ZCC.1 and ZCC.18 name the documented trusted-network criteria. Sources: https://help.zscaler.com/zscaler-client-connector/configuring-fail-open-settings-zscaler-client-connector; https://help.zscaler.com/zscaler-client-connector/configuring-trusted-networks-zscaler-client-connector
    - **SSL Inspection.**
      - 4.1 (and 9.3, A.2, 6.4) passes only with a catch-all Inspect rule. 4.3 sorts rules by `order` and treats source-scoped exemptions as scoped. 4.4 and 7.2 require the Recommended Exemptions rule to be enabled.
      - 4.5 and 5.5 leave out Zscaler's predefined and default rules. 4.5 warns only on a custom Do Not Decrypt rule with no criteria. 5.5 warns on exemptions of data-loss destinations.
      - 4.14 holds only the catch-all Decrypt rule to the full hardening set and lists narrow exception rules for review.
      - Sources: https://help.zscaler.com/zscaler-deployments-operations/zia-ssl-inspection-leading-practices-guide; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/about-ssltls-inspection-policy
    - **Inline web DLP.**
      - 5.9 reports the engine split as information: an empty engine list means Any. A.4 checks `fileTypes`. 5.13 reads the `FTCATEGORY_*` enums. Sources: https://help.zscaler.com/zia/configuring-dlp-policy-rules-content-inspection; https://github.com/zscaler/terraform-provider-zia/blob/master/zia/validator.go
      - 5.6 no longer says URL Allow rules bypass DLP; it reports Block rules on data-loss destinations as information. Source: https://help.zscaler.com/zscaler-deployments-operations/zia-policy-leading-practices-guide
      - 5.17 also reports the tenant-wide DLP Advanced Settings exemptions. 5.19 and 8.12 are informational when the setting is not readable, whatever the legacy per-rule flag says. Source: https://help.zscaler.com/zia/configuring-dlp-advanced-settings
      - 5.20 and A.9 drop the order-safety verdict. The texts, the wizard's recommendation and its checklist say rule order matters only between rules with the same action, that the mode can change only after all inline DLP rules are deleted, and that the Account team enables it. They also give the Data Security > Common Resources path, and the wizard reference link no longer returns 404. Sources: https://help.zscaler.com/zia/configuring-dlp-advanced-settings; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/configuring-dlp-policy-rules-evaluate-all-rules-mode-enabled

      - A.3, A.11, A.12, 5.4, 5.22 and B.14 always gave the same result, so they are now informational. 5.4 no longer names unsourced hostnames.

AI.8 counts Caution on the AI & ML rule and on URL Filtering. AI.14 reads `appType`. AI.16 needs an enabled web-log feed. AI.20 reads real trust levels. AI.15 gives the WebSocket and Copilot Chat limits. AI.4 and AI.19 say Zero Trust Browser. Sources: https://help.zscaler.com/zia/adding-ai-ml-applications-rule-cloud-app-control; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/about-url-categories; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/configuring-advanced-policy-settings
    - **Endpoint DLP.**

Sources: https://help.zscaler.com/zia/about-zscaler-incident-receiver; https://github.com/zscaler/terraform-provider-zia/blob/master/docs/resources/zia_dlp_web_rules.md

      - USR.5 and AUTO-ANS-02 look for an Admin Audit NSS feed. Source: https://help.zscaler.com/zia/about-audit-logs
    - **SaaS Security API and Email DLP.**
      - C.3, C.4, C.5, C.7 and C.8 count enabled rules only. C.7 treats `OFLCASB_DLP_ALLOW` and Notify as monitoring. C.8 reads the `COLLABORATION_SCOPE_*` enums. Source: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/zscaler/terraform-provider-zia/master/docs/resources/zia_casb_dlp_rules.md
      - D.1 names Data Security > Email DLP > Email Tenants. D.3 accepts filter rules without content inspection. D.5 counts Custom Header Insertion and says the mail server enforces the X-Zscaler headers. D.7 needs an auditor and a template, or an Incident Receiver. D.8 is reworded. Sources: https://help.zscaler.com/zia/adding-email-tenants; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/configuring-outbound-email-policy-rules; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/understanding-outbound-email-policy-enforcement
      - MC.4 and 8.9 read the response code and enabled rules, and 8.9 describes the pilot.
    - **Platform checks.**
      - FW.4 checks that the Default Cloud IPS Rule blocks. FW.7 counts custom services. FW.8 checks the three-condition rule on enabled custom Allow rules in order. Sources: https://help.zscaler.com/zia/configuring-ips-control-policy; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/best-practices-firewall-control-policy
      - SEC.3 needs every malware category blocked. SEC.4 compares with the recommended ATP policy. SEC.5 compares with the recommended File Type Control policy. Sources: https://help.zscaler.com/zia/configuring-malware-protection-policy; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/recommended-advanced-threat-protection-policy; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/recommended-file-type-control-policy
      - UF.1–UF.3 count enabled rules, need custom categories and look for a Security-category Block rule. BW.1 checks the location bandwidth limits. Sources: the ZIA Policy Leading Practices Guide; https://help.zscaler.com/zia/about-bandwidth-control
      - ZPA.4 and ZPA.8 read `controlChannelStatus`. ZPA.1 flags port 53, wide IP ranges and None/Continuous health reporting. ZPA.3 flags manual server lists. ZPA.4 and ZPA.5 describe N+1 sizing. ZPA.10 is about web server certificates. ZPA.15 is informational. Sources: https://help.zscaler.com/zscaler-deployments-operations/zpa-leading-practices-guide; https://help.zscaler.com/zpa/about-browser-access-certificates; https://help.zscaler.com/zpa/about-machine-groups
      - ZDX.5, ZDX.6 and ZDX.8 unwrap the `alerts`, `software` and `devices` lists. ZDX.1–3 and ZDX.8 ask for 24 hours instead of the default 2. ZDX.8 is renamed "ZDX device telemetry reachable". Source: zscaler-sdk-python 1.11.0 `zdx/`
      - RBAC.3 reads enabled admins. RBAC.4 reads `orgAuthType`. RBAC.5 points to Authentication Service MFA. RBAC.8 reads the sync fields. RBAC.9 counts SCIM_GROUP operands only. RBAC.10 and ZID.5 read `adminEntitlementEnabled`. The user-facing texts say "Authentication Service (formerly ZIdentity)". Sources: https://help.zscaler.com/authentication-service/configuring-multi-factor-authentication; zscaler-sdk-python `zid/models/users.py`, `zpa/policies.py`
      - AUTO-IAC-01 reads rank 0 as the super admin. AUTO-TF-05 no longer claims import collisions. Sources: https://help.zscaler.com/zia/understanding-admin-rank; https://github.com/zscaler/zscaler-terraformer/blob/master/cmd/generate.go
    - **New checks:**
      - D.9: images in email are scanned (Outbound Email DLP OCR), https://help.zscaler.com/zia/configuring-dlp-advanced-settings
      - FW.9: recommended block rules (Block Malicious IPs and Domains, QUIC above Allow rules)
      - LOC.6: firewall and IPS per location, from the ZIA Policy Leading Practices Guide
      - CA.4: DNS over HTTPS blocked
      - CA.5: Cascading to URL Filtering off
      - D.9 is in `DLP_Checks_Export.csv` and `.md`; the platform checks are not in the export.
    - **Not changed:**
      - The section labels ("ZIdentity — Directory", "RBAC — ZIdentity") stay, because permissions, benchmarks and saved scores key on them.
      - The wizard still returns `order_already_safe_for_eval_all` for the page that shows it, but nothing treats it as readiness any more.
- **Verified.** `tests/test_best_practice_review.py` builds each case from the SDK field names. It checks both action shapes and every sub-action, the folded app profiles, the Windows 10 and 11 builds, the OCR and Evaluate All settings, EDM and IDM, the endpoint channels, the NSS rule-name field, the version check, and the retention and timeout texts. The second review adds a case for each change in the same file, built from the SDK and Terraform field names and enums. The existing tests that pinned the old behaviour were updated. The full suite has no new failures against `dev`.

### The one-channel note is emphasised, not shouted
- **What you asked for.** "Make the bold note less bold; it's abrasively bold and large."
- **Now** the note on every pack card is in sentence case ("Install one channel at a time. …"), at body size (1rem, was 1.15rem) and semi-bold (600, was 800). It keeps its highlighted box, so it still stands out.

### Email DLP finds the Exchange tenant the SaaS tenant list leaves out
- **What you reported.** The preflight said "No Exchange, Gmail or secure email gateway tenant in the SaaS tenant list (listed: SMOES (SHAREPOINT))", but Exchange is integrated.
- **Why.** The app found email tenants only in ZIA's SaaS tenant list (`casbTenant/lite`). A read-only look at the tenant showed that list returns only the SharePoint tenant (id 177648211): not with active-only, not with include-deleted, and not filtered to email. The Exchange email tenant is a separate object with the same name, "SMOES" (id 171656574). The Email DLP rule already on the tenant, DLP_Rule_1, is scoped to it. The dependency row also still said the tenant was "optional", which the preflight change earlier today had missed.
- **Now**
  - When the preflight reads the Email DLP rules, it records the email tenants they are scoped to, and uses them alongside any the SaaS list returns.
  - New Email rules are scoped to your Exchange tenant (id 171656574), not the SharePoint tenant with the same name.
  - A pack that pins `"tenants": ["SMOES"]` on an Email rule gets the email tenant too.
  - The dependency row says an email tenant is required, and names the rule it was found on.
  - With no email tenant anywhere, the row is *missing* and says what to onboard.
- **Verified.** Tests model this tenant (SharePoint-only list, DLP_Rule_1 on the Exchange tenant). Email rules are created scoped to id 171656574, a pinned "SMOES" resolves to the email tenant, and with no email tenant the row is missing and no request is sent.

### SaaS Security and Email DLP are scored in the assessment
- **What you reported.** "In the assessment I don't see scores for SaaS and Email."
- **Why.** Inline Web, Endpoint and GenAI were sections of their own with a score; SaaS and Email only had one presence check each inside Multi-Channel.
- **Now** two new sections, **Ch.C — SaaS Security** and **Ch.D — Email DLP**, eight checks each, read from the tenant (read-only): SaaS applications onboarded, rules present, every onboarded application covered, files at rest scanned, rules carry engines, rules enabled, remediation beyond reporting, external and public sharing covered; Email tenant (Exchange, Gmail or secure email gateway) onboarded, which ZIA requires for every Email DLP rule, rules present, rules carry engines, rules enabled, enforcement beyond monitoring, rules scoped to the mail tenant, incidents reach an auditor, recipient scoping. They appear in Score by section and the report, count toward the overall score, and follow the Scope page's channel ownership (a channel marked covered by another product is left out). A channel the tenant has not licensed says so instead of failing.

### Every pack says: install one channel at a time
- **What you asked for.** "Make a note for each pack to only install one channel at a time … in large bold letters … this applies for every pack."
- **Now** every pack card in Deploy policy packs opens with, in large bold type: **INSTALL ONE CHANNEL AT A TIME.** Deploy Web DLP first, check it, then Endpoint DLP, then SaaS, then Email, one channel per run. A small run is easy to verify, and it avoids the burst of requests that makes ZIA block the API client (Resource Access Blocked).
  - Each shipped pack carries the note in its `pack.json` (`deploy_note`).
  - Any other pack, uploaded ones included, shows the same note.
- **Verified.** A test checks that every built-in pack has the note and that every card shows it (with the fallback), in type at least 1.1rem and weight 800.

### Email DLP rules wait for an email tenant instead of being refused
- **What you reported.** A ZBP Enterprise deploy ran into five failed Email DLP rules ("One or more Email tenants is required", HTTP 400), then "Resource Access Blocked" (HTTP 403). It stopped with 10 rules never attempted and the tenant unreadable for the read-back.
- **Why.** The 2026-09-30 change *Email DLP rules deploy in every pack* created an Email rule without an email tenant when none was onboarded, trusting the SDK's example. ZIA refuses such a rule. Five refused writes in a row then made ZIA block the API client for several minutes, longer than the five 15-second retries, so everything after them was lost.
- **Now** the preflight checks for an email tenant. With none onboarded (this tenant has only SharePoint), every Email rule is a gap with what to do: *ZIA requires at least one email tenant on an Email DLP rule … onboard one under Administration > SaaS Application Tenants, then deploy the Email channel.* No request ZIA would refuse is sent, so the other channels deploy in full. With an Exchange, Gmail or secure email gateway tenant onboarded, Email rules are scoped to it as before.
- **Verified.** The pack tests now expect the gap, with no Email request sent, on a tenant without an email tenant and on a SharePoint-only tenant, in every pack. Scoping to Exchange, Gmail and a SEG is unchanged.

### Whatever works on a local install works in the container
- **What you asked for.** "Whatever works on the local install should work in the container without fault", then "please check".
- **How it was checked.** Every place the app writes a file, every external program it runs and every localhost-only rule was listed from the code. The running container was then asked whether each location is writable: `/app` is read-only there, and `/data` is the only place it can write.
- **What was broken in the container, and now works:**
  - **Uploading a pack** (Advanced settings) failed, and packs on the volume were never listed. Discovery and the upload both passed the app's own folder, so `DLP_CHECK_USER_PACKS` was ignored and both used read-only `/app/user_packs`. The setting now applies to the app's own folder, however it is passed.
  - **Approving guidance as a check reference** could not be saved, because the shipped `checks_config.json` is read-only. Approvals now go to `/data/checks_config.json` (`DLP_CHECK_CHECKS_CONFIG`), and the app reads the shipped file and the approvals together. A local install keeps using the one file.
  - **The SDK & docs update check, the best-practice crawler and its discoveries** lost everything they learned on every run. Their caches sat in the read-only home folder, and the write failures were silent. `XDG_CACHE_HOME=/data/cache` keeps them on the volume.
  - **Beacon's SDK diff** called `pip download`, and the image ships without pip. The SDK wheel is now fetched straight from PyPI (`files.pythonhosted.org`, added to the allowlist) and checked against PyPI's published SHA-256 before it is unpacked. This works the same everywhere.
  - **Rebuild pack from tenant** was refused ("only allowed when the server is bound to localhost") and could not have written the pack anyway. It now follows the same rule as write mode: nobody but this computer can reach the server. A built-in pack whose folder is read-only is rebuilt as a copy in the pack library under the same id, and that copy replaces the shipped one. Removing it there restores the shipped pack. Its Terraform is regenerated beside it.
  - **Secure by Design** scored the container down: "reachable from the network" for listening on 0.0.0.0 inside it. A container published on the host's loopback now passes, as a local install does.
- **Left as they are, with the container's own path:**
  - **Update app:** the container cannot replace its own files. It shows the `update_container` commands instead.
  - **Opening the browser at start**, and **Terraform** when it is not on PATH: the same as on a local install.
- **Verified.** A container built from this change (read-only, `/data` volume, published on loopback) ran every fixed path for real: the write switch, Secure by Design, an approval, the three caches, the SDK download without pip, and a rebuild into the library that replaced the shipped pack and regenerated its Terraform. All passed. `tests/test_container_parity.py` pins each fix, and the full suite has no new failures.

### Report details are part of the tenant configuration
- **What you asked for.** "Make the report details section part of the tenant config."
- **Now** the organisation and assessor fields are a Report details block in the tenant configuration on Connect to the tenant, above Authenticate, instead of a section of Scope assessment. They are saved with the tenant profile, as before, so choosing a profile fills them. Running an assessment with either one blank opens Connect on that field.

### Windows: the container update no longer stops when the build starts
- **What you reported.** Enable write mode still did not appear in the container.
- **Why.** The container was still running the old build. `update_container.ps1` pulled the new code, then stopped on docker's first build line: `Image dlp-check:local Building ... NativeCommandError`. Docker writes its build progress to stderr, and Windows PowerShell 5.1 treats each stderr line as an error record, which the script's `Stop` setting made fatal. The rebuild finished only because docker carried on in the background; the script said nothing about it and never reached *Done*.
- **Now** the script relaxes that setting for the docker call alone and judges the build by docker's exit code. The progress lines go to the window and `update_container.log`.
- **Verified.** A fake docker that writes its progress to stderr: exit 0 ends in *Done*, and exit 1 ends in *The build failed; the previous container keeps running*. On this machine the container now runs `a33c043` with `DLP_CHECK_HOST_BIND=127.0.0.1`, and write mode reports `can_toggle: true`.

### ZPA and ZDX checks read the paths the tenant serves
- **What you asked for.** "Try ZPA and ZDX again", once the API client had its ZPA and ZDX roles.
- **What the probe found.** The 401s were gone, but 16 of the 25 ZPA reads and 4 of the 8 ZDX reads still failed:
  - ZPA IdPs, trusted networks, posture profiles, SAML attributes and log streaming live on the `v2` customer base, not `v1`.
  - ZPA policy rules are listed per policy type (`/policySet/rules/policyType/ACCESS_POLICY`), and there is no `GLOBAL_POLICY` type.
  - ZDX calls its applications `/apps` and its device list `/devices`.
  - The ZDX API has no list of alert rules and no reports.
- **Now**
  - The resolver maps those short paths to the real ones, as it already did for ZCC. The access-policy check reads the access policy type, and dead fallbacks are gone.
  - ZDX alert rules and reports report INFO and point at the ZDX Admin Portal, without spending a request.
- **Verified.** On the tenant, every ZPA and ZDX check ran through the app's own client: all 22 endpoints answered 200 and every check gave a real result. `tests/test_api_paths.py` pins the mappings, the access-policy read and the no-API checks. Full suite: no new failures.

### The container updates from main, qa or dev
- **What you asked for.** "The container should also have the option to update from dev, qa or main branch."
- **Before.** `update_container.ps1` and `update_container.sh` only pulled the branch the folder already had checked out. The Branch list in Update app changed only which branch the container was compared with.
- **Now** both scripts take a branch: `-Branch main|qa|dev` on Windows, `--branch main|qa|dev` on Mac and Linux.
  - The script switches the folder to that branch and rebuilds. Later runs and the daily schedule keep updating from it.
  - Any other name is refused. `DLP-CHECK-QA`, the QA branch's old name, is taken as `qa`.
  - In the container, Update app shows both commands for the branch picked in its Branch list, and so does the update notice's **Show how**.
  - `INSTALL.md` explains the choice: main is what customers run; qa and dev carry changes before they are promoted.
- **Verified.** A new test runs `update_container.sh` against a scratch git remote that has main, qa and dev, with `docker` replaced by a stub. It covers switching to qa, staying on qa on the next run, `--branch=dev`, the old QA name, and refusing `prod`; each switch rebuilds and an up-to-date run does not. The PowerShell script was run the same way by hand (-Branch qa, a plain run, DEV, DLP-CHECK-QA, main), with the same results.

### Enable write mode in the container, when only this computer can reach it
- **What you asked for.** "No option to enable write mode in the container", then: yes to a switch there.
- **Why it was missing.** The switch is refused unless the server listens on loopback. Inside the container it listens on 0.0.0.0, even though `docker-compose.yml` publishes it on the host's 127.0.0.1 only. The container could not see that, so it refused.
- **Now** `docker-compose.yml` takes the host address from one setting, `DLP_CHECK_HOST_BIND` (default `127.0.0.1`), for both its `ports:` line and the app itself.
  - Published on loopback (the default), Deploy policy packs offers **Enable write mode** as on a laptop: you type `YES_I_UNDERSTAND`, it lasts until the container restarts, and the UI token still guards it.
  - Published beyond loopback, the switch is refused and the message points at `ENABLE_WRITE_ACTIONS=YES_I_UNDERSTAND` in `.env`.
  - A container that does not pass the setting (an older compose file, or a `docker run` without `-e DLP_CHECK_HOST_BIND=127.0.0.1`) stays refused, as before.
  - `INSTALL.md` (the compose and by-hand runs) and `docs/OVERVIEW.md` say so.
- **Verified.** A new test covers the switch in the container on loopback: offered, the phrase still required, and turning writes off always allowed. The same test covers publishing on 0.0.0.0, on a LAN address or unset (refused), and the compose file passing the same address its `ports:` line uses. The tests that pinned the `ports:` line now pin the variable with its `127.0.0.1` default.

### Beacon: SDK, API and Terraform changes, on a timer, with what changed since the last run

- **What you asked for.** A Beacon that watches for SDK changes, Zscaler API changes and Terraform changes, on demand and on a timer, and shows what changed since the last run.

- **Before.** The upstream contract check ran online only in GitHub Actions (06:23 UTC daily), whose result this server cannot read; the app itself ran the offline half (the installed SDK and the committed Terraform) on the Advanced page, and the *Zscaler SDK & docs updates* tab hashed the help-center pages without the SDK diff, the service definition or the provider. Nothing kept a history, and nothing said whether a tenant still answered the endpoints the checks read.

- **Now** **Beacon** (Reference → *Beacon: SDK, API & Terraform changes*, `/report/beacon`, `beacon.py`) runs the upstream check's watchers on this server and keeps the last 30 runs (`beacon_state.json` beside the update-check cache, `%LOCALAPPDATA%\ZscalerMadeEasy` on Windows; `DLP_CHECK_BEACON_STATE` moves it, the container keeps it on `/data`). **SDK:** the installed `zscaler-sdk-python` against PyPI's latest release and prerelease, whether the latest changes a module the app calls, the SDK's newest changelog entry, the SDK findings. **API:** the 2.x SDK's ZIA service definition against every endpoint the app writes to, whether Zscaler's API reference answers, and the help-center and OneAPI pages, hashed as the updates tab hashes them but kept separately, so running Beacon never changes what that tab reports. **Terraform:** the `zscaler/zia` pin against the registry's latest, attribute drift against the provider's schema and docs, the provider's newest changelog entry. *Changed since last run* lists, in words, versions that moved, drift that is new or cleared, pages whose content changed and tenant endpoints whose answer changed; an online run is compared with the last online run and an offline one with the last offline one, so a run without a network never reports facts as "cleared". A timer runs the online watchers every `DLP_CHECK_BEACON_HOURS` (default 24; 0 turns it off; documented in both `local_settings` examples) while the server runs; the first run waits until the newest online run is that old, so a restart does not re-fetch everything, and a failed run is recorded, never raised. **Tenant API watch** (optional, from the tab only, never the timer): the tenant chosen as on the Live DLP feed (the assessment tab, a saved profile, or this computer's settings) is signed in, then every endpoint the app's checks and features read (found in their code; not `discover_endpoints.py`'s guesses or the `scope_permissions` catalogue) is read with GET, about one a second, recording OK / 403 / 404 / 429 / timeout / error and the item count; the next watch of the same tenant names each endpoint that answers differently, with the permission a 403 needs. The watch's client refuses every method but GET before a request is built. `GET /api/beacon` (latest run, what changed, history, timer), `GET /api/beacon/status` (the current drift and a key that changes with it, for a notice; `?refresh=1` starts an online run at most once an hour), `POST /api/beacon/run` (`online`, `tenant_watch`, the usual tenant fields; one run at a time, 409 otherwise; credentials validated and never echoed). The Advanced page's upstream card and the README link to it.

- **In the update notice.** Beside the existing *Update available* bar, a second bar in the same stack at the top of every page shows Beacon's current upstream drift (for example *Upstream change: api. zscaler-sdk-python 2.0.0b2: no POST operation for /endPointDlpRules… (and 1 more)*) with **Details** (the Beacon tab) and its own **Later**, which hides that set of findings until it changes. It reads `/api/beacon/status` on the notice's schedule and never starts a fetch itself.
- **Tidy-up.** `upstream_check.WATCHED_SDK_FILES` listed `zscaler_client.py` and `legacy.py` at the package root, which no 1.x release has (the diff skipped them, so they did nothing); they are replaced by `zia/legacy.py`, where the classic ZIA API client the app signs in with lives. `ZscalerClient` is in `oneapi_client.py`, already watched.

- **Verified.** `tests/test_beacon.py` (25 tests: the snapshot, the diff, history and its cap, offline and online kept apart, the GET-only guard, the timer loop with a fake sleep, the endpoints, the page and the notice). The existing notice tests still pass; full suite has no new failures. Not verified here: a real browser pass of the tab and the stacked bars, a live online run, a live tenant watch, and the timer under a running server.

### Every API the checks read is one the tenant serves
- **What you asked for.** An inventory of every Zscaler API the app uses, called read-only against the tenant, then "update all the APIs".
- **What the probe found.** With zscaler-sdk-python 1.11.0, many reads went to paths Zscaler does not serve (404, 400 or 405), and some checks turned that refusal into a finding. The affected reads were:
  - firewall, DNS and IPS rules, and static IPs;
  - ZIA admin users;
  - ATP and malware settings;
  - Cloud App Control rules (the API lists them per rule type);
  - File Type Control, and Endpoint DLP rules (`endPointDlpRules`);
  - Browser Isolation profiles, incident receivers and NSS feeds;
  - SaaS Security API rules (they need `/all`);
  - the security blacklist (a field of `/security/advanced`);
  - ZIdentity (served at `/ziam/admin/api/v1`, not `/zidentity/v1`);
  - ZCC admin users (they need `userType`) and ZCC device cleanup.
- **Now** every one of those reads the real path, checked on a live tenant, where every fixed path answered 200.
  - Cloud App Control rules and ZCC admin users are read in parts and returned as one list, each item tagged with its rule type or user type. The SDK client now reads Cloud App Control for every type, not only STREAMING_MEDIA.
  - Some things no public API lists: endpoint DLP resources, ZCC posture profiles, Workflow Automation (its own ZWA API) and ZIdentity entitlements (listed per user only). Their checks now report INFO saying so, instead of reporting a gap.
  - "Group-only entitlement binding is in force" is no longer reported when the list could not be read.
  - Malware protection is read from the per-category `Blocked` flags.
  - Dead fallbacks are gone, and so are four evaluate-all-rules probes that could never answer (two duplicates, two with no product prefix).
  - The SdkClient no longer maps endpoint DLP resources to the ICAP server list, or posture profiles to device groups.
- **Not changed.**
  - ZPA and ZDX answered 401 to this tenant's API client. That is a role to grant in ZIdentity, not a path.
  - Cloud Sandbox's quota 403 is a license.
  - rule_migrator's remaining probes for the evaluate-all setting stay, since one tenant exposed it through a feature flag.
- **Verified.**
  - `tests/test_api_paths.py` covers the resolver, the per-type and per-userType reads, the corrected checks, and that no check reads a path the API does not serve.
  - Test fakes keyed on the old paths now use the real ones.
  - Full suite: the same failures as `dev` had before this change, none new.

### Read-only in the container: the message says how to turn writes on there
- **What you reported.** In the container, Deploy policy packs showed no Enable write mode switch and told you to start `START_Zscaler_Assessment_WRITES_ENABLED.bat`, which does not exist there.
- **Why.** Inside the container the server listens on 0.0.0.0, and the in-browser switch is refused beyond loopback. That safeguard is unchanged.
- **Now** every read-only message says how to turn writes on where the app runs: in the container, put `ENABLE_WRITE_ACTIONS=YES_I_UNDERSTAND` in `.env` next to `docker-compose.yml` and run `docker compose up -d`; on Windows the start script; on Mac and Linux `ENABLE_WRITE_ACTIONS=YES_I_UNDERSTAND ./start.sh`.

### Email DLP rules deploy like the Fed pack's in every pack; Exchange, Gmail and secure email gateway tenants recognised
- **What you reported.** ZBP's Email rules were skipped with "none of the onboarded SaaS tenants match email (onboarded: SMOES (SHAREPOINT))", and every pack should follow the Fed pack's methodology.
- **Why.** Every pack goes through the same deploy path, and it required a Microsoft 365 or Gmail tenant before creating any Email DLP rule. The Fed pack is skipped the same way on this tenant. The API does not need one: email tenants are optional on an Email DLP rule (the SDK's own example and the zscaler/zia provider create one without them).
- **Now** an Email DLP rule is scoped to the tenant's Exchange, Gmail or secure email gateway tenants when there are some, found through ZIA's own email-tenant filter (`app_type=EMAIL`) and by name. With none, it is created without an email tenant filter and the preview says so, instead of being skipped. SaaS rules still need a matching SaaS tenant (SharePoint scopes the file rules). The Terraform hand-off writes the email tenant block only when ids are set. A test checks that every pack's SaaS, Email and Endpoint rules have exactly the Fed pack's shape, and that every pack's Email rules deploy on a SharePoint-only tenant.

### Sidebar: Connect to the tenant above the Dashboard
- **What you asked for.** "Move connect to the tenant above the dashboard."
- **Now** the Workflow group reads in the order the work is done, like the step strip: Connect to the tenant, Scope assessment, Dashboard. The Dashboard is still the page that opens first.

### Container updates itself on a schedule; an update notice in the app
- **What you asked for.** "The container needs an auto update" (Update app showed the ZIP message inside the container) and "there should be a beacon that looks for updates and notifies the user".
- **Now** `update_container.ps1` (Windows) and `update_container.sh` (Mac, Linux), run on the computer that runs Docker, pull the latest code from GitHub and rebuild the container only when there is something new; `-Schedule` / `--schedule` once makes it run every day at 03:17 (Windows Task Scheduler, cron). They stop without changing anything if a file was edited by hand, and log to `update_container.log`. The image records the commit it was built from.
- **Update notice.** A minute after the page opens and every 6 hours after, the app checks GitHub and shows a notice at the top when a newer build is out, with **Update app** (local install) or **Show how** (container); **Later** hides it until the next build. Inside the container, Update app now explains the host update instead of the ZIP message. Without git (the container, a ZIP copy) the check uses GitHub's API; the repository is private, so the container needs a read-only `DLP_CHECK_GITHUB_TOKEN` in `.env` for the notice (never logged or shown).
- **Verified.** The shell script against a scratch GitHub-like remote: behind (pulled, rebuilt with the new commit), up to date (nothing done), hand edit (stopped). Tests pin the scripts, the build stamp, the container status, the beacon and the token handling. Full suite green. The PowerShell script was not run here (no PowerShell in the test environment).

## 2026-09-29

### The container publishes on port 8001 by default
- **What you asked for.** "Change it in the yml file so when I deploy the container it works" beside the local install on 8000.
- **Now** `docker-compose.yml` publishes the container on `127.0.0.1:8001` with no `.env` needed, and the container prints `http://localhost:8001/?token=…`. The local install stays on 8000. `DLP_CHECK_HOST_PORT` in `.env` still picks another port. `INSTALL.md`, `README.md` and the `docker run` example use 8001.

### Run the container beside a local install: choose the host port
- **What you asked for.** "I am running the local install and docker and it won't let both run on 8000. Can I run the docker container on 8001?"
- **Now** `DLP_CHECK_HOST_PORT=8001` in a `.env` file next to `docker-compose.yml` publishes the container on `127.0.0.1:8001` (still loopback only; 8000 when unset), and the container prints `http://localhost:8001/?token=…` so the link it gives works. The app inside the container stays on 8000. `INSTALL.md` says how, including for `docker run`.

### Container build behind TLS inspection: trust the company root certificate
- **What you reported.** `docker compose up -d --build` on Windows stopped at `[6/8] pip install` with `CERTIFICATE_VERIFY_FAILED ... unable to get local issuer certificate` for `pypi.org`.
- **Why.** TLS inspection (Zscaler) re-signs HTTPS with the company's root certificate, which the Python base image does not trust.
- **Now** the build adds any Base-64 root certificate found in the new `certs/` folder to the image's trust store before pip runs, and pip, `requests` and Python's `ssl` use that store, so the running app trusts the same certificate on the Zscaler API. With `certs/` empty nothing changes. `INSTALL.md` has a one-line PowerShell export of the Zscaler Root CA into `certs\zscaler-root.crt` (and the Mac and Linux equivalents); certificate files there are git-ignored.
- **Verified.** Built through a proxy with the certificate in `certs/`: "extra root certificates added: 1", every locked package installed, the container answered `/api/ping` and reached `https://pypi.org` from inside. A test pins the order (certificates before pip), the trust-store variables and the ignore rules.

### Install guide: build and run the container by hand
- **What you asked for.** Instructions to run the container manually, mounting its storage, without Docker Compose.
- **Now** `INSTALL.md` has *Build and run it by hand*: `docker build`, `docker volume create dlpcheck-data`, and a `docker run` with the same settings as `docker-compose.yml` (loopback-only port, the `/data` volume mount, read-only filesystem, no extra privileges, restart policy), for Mac/Linux and on one line for Windows, plus how to read the UI token, check health, update and remove it. A test keeps the commands in step with the compose file.

### Connect step counts Web, Endpoint, SaaS and Email DLP rules
- **What you asked for.** "Show how many SaaS, Email, Endpoint DLP and Web DLP rules exist" on the Connect to the tenant step.
- **Now** the connection check reads every DLP channel and the step says, for example, "0 Web DLP rules · 2 Endpoint DLP rules · 1 SaaS DLP rules · 3 Email DLP rules". A channel the API client cannot read shows "unreadable" with a line saying why, instead of 0. Read-only, like the rest of the check.

### Dictionary API check; SaaS and Email rules counted under their own channel
- **What you asked for.** "Check that the API for DLP dictionaries is correct", and "when trying to deploy all channels with zbp pack it is not recognizing the channels for email and saas correctly".
- **Dictionary API.** Every call matches Zscaler's SDK and API: `GET/POST /dlpDictionaries`, `PUT` and `DELETE /dlpDictionaries/{id}`, field names converted to the API's camelCase, phrases and patterns sent as (action, text) pairs with the `PHRASE_COUNT_TYPE_*` / `PATTERN_COUNT_TYPE_*` and `MATCH_*_CUSTOM_PHRASE_PATTERN_DICTIONARY` values. One call was wrong: the confidence-level change on an existing dictionary (the CJIS pack's Medical → Low) sent only four fields, and a PUT replaces the dictionary, so it could reset proximity, identifiers and other settings. It now sends the whole dictionary as the tenant has it with only the confidence level changed.
- **Channels.** A SaaS or Email rule that was reused or skipped was counted under Web in the deploy result's *Rules per channel*, so the result read as if those channels were missing. Every rule is now counted under its own channel.
- **Verified.** Tests pin the whole-object dictionary update and the per-channel count; a fake-tenant deploy of the ZBP and Fed packs shows Web, Endpoint, SaaS and Email each under its own channel. Full suite green. No tenant contacted.

### Every conversion to Terraform follows Terraform's own best practices
- **What you asked for.** "When converting from one file type to terraform ensure you use best practices from terraform."
