- **Before.** The pack workspaces (the committed `terraform/<pack>/`, the Terraform hand-off, the pack library's transcription) failed `terraform fmt -check` in 24 files (a fixed 28-column padding); used the older file names; asked for one required variable per SaaS and email rule (34 in the Fed pack, so `terraform plan` prompted 34 times); had no validation on the rank or the starting order; outputs without descriptions; lifecycle blocks in the middle of rules; engine descriptions up to 352 characters (ZIA refuses over 255, so apply would fail); duplicate lookups of the same predefined dictionary; a thin `.gitignore` (saved plans, which carry variable values, were not ignored); and CI missed a file the generator started or stopped emitting. The vendor migration emitted one `main.tf.json` with an exact provider pin and no `required_version`, doubled labels (`sym_sym_101`), no provenance, and an email action (`CUSTOMHEADERINSERTION`) the zscaler/zia provider 4.8 refuses, so a migration with a custom-header email rule failed `terraform validate`.
- **Now** every generated file is written the way `terraform fmt` lays it out (`pack_terraform.hcl_fmt`) and says what generated it. The workspace uses the style guide's names (`terraform.tf`, `providers.tf`, `variables.tf`, `locals.tf`, `outputs.tf`); credentials come from sensitive variables or `ZSCALER_*` environment variables, never files; `rule_starting_order` and `policy_rank` are validated and non-nullable; SaaS and email tenant ids are one map variable per channel (rule name to ids) with a default list, and a rule left without ids fails at plan with a sentence naming it; each resource has one lifecycle block, last; outputs are described and read `locals`; engine descriptions are clamped to 255; a predefined dictionary is looked up once; the README carries requirements, inputs, outputs, state and lock-file guidance, verify-and-review steps and how to rename with a `moved` block; `.gitignore` covers saved plans (including the plain `tfplan` the README saves), override files and tfvars JSON and keeps the lock file. Each committed `.terraform.lock.hcl` now records the provider's checksums for Windows, macOS (Intel and Apple silicon) and Linux, plus the release's `zh:` checksums, so `terraform init` accepts it on any of those machines; before, it held the Linux x86 checksum only and init refused the provider elsewhere. Resource addresses are unchanged, so existing state carries over; a workspace generated earlier needs its tfvars moved (per-rule `saas_tenant_ids_<label>` values into `saas_tenant_ids["<rule name>"]`, likewise for email) and its old `versions.tf` and `provider.tf` deleted, as the README's *Upgrading* section says. Only files the generator itself wrote are ever removed from a workspace folder: the team's own `.tf` files, `terraform.tfvars`, state and the lock file are never touched. The vendor migration uses the same constraints (`~> 4.8`, Terraform `>= 1.5.0`), keeps its rule labels exactly as before (so an applied migration keeps its addresses), writes provenance into descriptions and a `//` comment, records an email custom header as the designed action (set in the portal) instead of emitting an action the provider refuses, uses the same tenant-id variables, and is also handed over as the same workspace layout: **Download Terraform workspace (.zip)** on the page, `/api/vendor-migrate/workspace`, and `terraform/` beside `main.tf.json` from the command line. CI regenerates the workspaces and fails on any new, changed or deleted file, runs `terraform fmt -check`, and validates the vendor migration's HCL and JSON and a sample of the containment rules (Cloud App Control, File Type, URL Filtering, SSL Inspection) that no shipped pack uses, beside the six packs (`tools/terraform_samples.py`).
- **Verified.** Every workspace (six packs, the vendor HCL, the vendor JSON, the containment sample) passes `terraform fmt -check` and `terraform validate` against the real zscaler/zia 4.8.8 provider schema, run locally; resource addresses compared before and after for every pack (unchanged); tests pin the fmt layout, the file names, the variables and validation, the one-lifecycle-last rule, the outputs and locals, the README sections, the provenance headers (one line whatever the pack metadata holds), the engine-description clamp on a description longer than 255 bytes, the lock files' platforms, that only generated files are removed (a blocked compile leaves state, tfvars and the team's files alone), the quote-aware unwrapping of a whole interpolation, the vendor workspace download and its 409 when blocked, the email designed action, the unchanged vendor labels, and the CI checks. Full suite green. No tenant contacted.

### The install guide says how to install Docker, step by step
- **What you asked for.** "Install instructions: you forgot the steps to install Docker."
- **Before.** The container option said only "download Docker Desktop, install it, open it once" for Windows and Mac, and gave Ubuntu's `docker.io` package, which on Ubuntu 22.04 is older than the Compose 2.24 this app's `docker-compose.yml` needs.
- **Now** container Step 1 walks through Docker on each platform with a check that must pass before going on: **Windows** (turning on WSL 2 in an administrator PowerShell, the right installer for x86 or ARM, the installer's WSL 2 option, the restart, the first start to **Engine running**, and the docker-users group message); **Mac** (Apple silicon or Intel, the matching download, the drag to Applications, the first start); **Linux** (Docker Engine and the Compose plugin from Docker's own repository, the service, the docker group and the re-login, with Debian, Fedora and RHEL pointers). Each ends with `docker --version`, `docker compose version` (2.24 or newer) and `docker run --rm hello-world`. It also carries the Docker Desktop licence note for companies of 250 or more people, what TLS inspection does to the image build, where to set a proxy, and six new troubleshooting rows in Docker's own words.
- **Verified.** `tests/test_install_guide.py` pins every step, the checks, the licence and TLS notes and the troubleshooting rows. No tenant contacted.

### An install guide a new IT person can follow, and an overview of what the app does
- **What you asked for.** "Ensure there are clear install instructions that are so easy to follow that a new IT guy can follow the instructions in the repo"; "prerequisites: it also supports legacy API"; "provide an overview of what the app can do (or is supposed to do)… that overview should be in the repo."
- **Before.** `INSTALL.md` installed from a ZIP and then promised updates that only a git clone can do; it called the UI token a "one-time sign-in code", quoted messages the scripts never print, sent a Mac reader to Windows-only settings, kept the reader in a Terminal window that cannot see a freshly installed Python, and gave no way to know it worked or whom to ask. The Windows installer printed "Installation complete!" after a failed dependency install, never checked the Python version, and ended by describing a console window the silent launcher never opens; the uninstaller could not stop the server it was about to delete; `STOP` ignored a changed port; the launcher opened the browser four seconds after starting, token or no token. The three documents told three different install stories, and `README.md` called the repository public.
- **Now** `INSTALL.md` opens with a checklist (what you need, that the repository is private, and both sign-in methods with what to collect for each: OneAPI's cloud, vanity domain, client ID and secret, or the classic ZIA API's cloud, admin username, password and API key), offers Git or ZIP with what each means for updating, and walks Windows, Mac, Linux and the container through with the real button labels, the real messages, what success looks like, what failure looks like, a work-laptop box for TLS inspection and proxies on every platform, a shared-server recipe for the container, a "how you know it worked" section, an everyday-use table, a troubleshooting table quoting the scripts' own words, and where to get help. `docs/OVERVIEW.md` is the five-minute tour: what the app is for, the shape of an engagement, every function by toolbar group, the packs, what it never does (read-only start, write mode, nothing overwritten, every rule disabled), both sign-ins, the command line, and its limits. `README.md` and `GETTING_STARTED.md` point at both and tell the same story (section 1 defers to `INSTALL.md`; 1a/1b/1c in order; the Mac/Linux settings file covered).
- **Scripts made true to the guide.** The Windows installer reads `local_settings.bat` so the CA bundle and proxy apply to pip, refuses a Python older than 3.11 naming the one it found, stays open on a failed install with `INSTALL FAILED` and makes no shortcut, and ends by describing the silent launcher; it exits with `exit /b 0` so a calling Command Prompt survives. `UNINSTALL` stops the server by its port like `STOP`; `STOP` honours `ZSCALER_PORT` from `local_settings.bat`. The launcher waits for the server to answer (up to 30 s) before reading the token file, so a slow first start never opens the browser without the token. `install.sh` reads `local_settings.env` before pip and says how to stop; `start.sh --background` prints the address and keeps the token in `ui_token.txt`; an in-app update on Mac or Linux records `server.pid` so `./stop.sh` works afterwards, and `app_update.py` from a terminal installs into the app's `.venv`. The update card's help and the updater's refusals speak Mac and Linux as well as Windows; the unlock dialog points at `server.log` or the terminal, not a console. The container prints `localhost` and a one-line note instead of a network-bind warning that docker-compose has already contained. A second round of readers added: Git over the Windows Secure Channel backend behind TLS inspection; the old-Python refusal as a `goto` (a path with `)` closed the block); a note when a locked-down PowerShell makes no shortcut; an in-app restart on Mac/Linux that records the server's own pid (bash forks inside a backgrounded `cd && nohup`); a half-made `.venv` rebuilt; `--background` never offering the previous run's token; ASCII-only Windows scripts (cmd reads them in the OEM code page); the container keeping digests, proposals, the guidance state and the SaaS matrix on its volume; and an overview that names its two overwrite exceptions (the rule migration wizard's Step 3b and the API-only converge option), write mode in the container, every host the app reaches, the command line per platform, the MCP server, and the packs by the names the app shows.
- **Verified.** `tests/test_install_guide.py`: every file and link the guide names exists and every in-page link resolves; the sections a first-timer needs; both sign-in methods with their fields; the app's own words (UI token, the installers' messages, the Branch labels); the Windows installer, `STOP`, `UNINSTALL`, the launcher and `install.sh` doing what the guide says; the overview naming every function the app explains, every pack directory present; README and GETTING_STARTED agreeing with the guide; no Windows-only wording in the unlock dialog or the update help. A fresh-copy Linux install (`./install.sh`, `./start.sh --background`, `./stop.sh`) run end to end. Full suite green. No tenant contacted.

### A 403 on a deploy or a delete: activate, wait 15 seconds, try again, up to five times
- **Before.** A 403 from ZIA on a create, update or delete ("Resource Access Blocked" after a burst of requests, "Forbidden" while a pending change is applied) failed the object at once, and a "Forbidden" stopped the run as a client without the DLP write permission.
- **Now** every write a deploy, removal or undo sends through the shared path (`pack_ops.call`) answers a 403 the same way: activate the pending configuration, wait 15 seconds, try again, up to five more times, and only then record the failure; the log says so at each try (`ZIA answered 'Resource Access Blocked' (HTTP 403); activating, waiting 15s and trying again (1 of 5)`), names each activation and its status (every pending change in the tenant goes live with it), and says when the retry succeeds. A retry that meets a rate limit or a busy tenant is waited out like any other write. A 403 about the rule's rank is not retried, because activating changes nothing about it; a 401 is not (the credentials are refused); a read is not; a stopped run tries nothing more, and Stop clicked during the 15-second wait sends nothing more. A 403 still there after the five activations stops the run at once with a sentence that says what to do (`ZIA refused a write with a 403 and kept refusing it after 5 activations …`), because the tenant is refusing the client, not the object — without that, every later object would cost five more activations and 75 seconds against the same answer. A proposal's single field change, which runs outside a deploy, activates nothing, as its note promises. The migration wizard's *Delete all rules* step follows the same rule through the raw client. The Deploy Studio's run screen names the wait; the shared-path contract and the deployment flow page describe the rule.
- **Verified.** Tests: a 403 that clears on the fourth try (three activations, three 15-second waits, success logged), one that never clears (five more tries, then failed, the run stopped with the 403 sentence and the next write not sent), a 403 followed by a 429 (waited out, then succeeds), a 401 (no activation), Stop during the wait (no further write), an activation that cannot run (said, the wait and the try still happen), the rank refusal, a read, a call without the client, a call outside a run, the repository's `pc.activate` patch seeing the activations, every create / update / delete entry point handing the client to `call()`; end to end on the strict tenant, a deploy and a removal whose first writes are refused 403 three times finish clean with three 15-second waits, and a deploy refused 403 throughout stops after one object and 75 seconds with everything else *not attempted*; the wizard's delete with a 403 that clears and one that does not. Full suite green. No tenant contacted.

### The industry on the tenant profile pre-selects its policy packs
- **Before.** The industry chosen on the tenant profile marked its packs *For <industry>* in Deploy policy packs and folded the others away, but nothing was ticked: the operator selected the same packs by hand every time, and the scope only followed the industry after the profile was saved.
- **Now** choosing an industry on the profile form ticks the industry's packs on the form at once (more can be ticked, any can be unticked; unticking all falls back to the industry's list) and refreshes the scope without a save. Deploy policy packs opens with the scope's packs selected, says so in a card (*Pre-selected for Healthcare: …*; what the operator added or dropped is named) and in the footer, and re-selects only when the scope itself changes (another industry, packs ticked or unticked on the profile). Whatever the operator changes by hand after that stands, across closing and reopening the sheet. The scope API now also returns the industry's own packs beside the profile's choice. The profile form now lists every installed pack (baselines first), not only the baseline packs: the Fed and CJIS packs the Federal, State & local and Law enforcement industries call for were not tickable there before.
- **Verified.** A browser pass on the real server (no tenant): Healthcare ticks the PHI and Enterprise packs on the form and in the sheet, a pack added and one dropped by hand survive a reopen, Federal re-picks the Fed pack, ticking CJIS on the profile widens the selection; no page errors. Tests pin the API field, the form wiring and the studio's pre-selection. Full suite green.

### Remove rules: the sheet says what it is doing, top and bottom
- **Before.** Choosing *Remove rules…* (or removing a pack, or Undo) replaced the sheet's body and step strip but left its title reading *Deploy policy packs* and its footer showing the deploy step's *Select at least one pack to continue* with *Cancel* and *Run preflight →*, while the removal's own buttons sat inside the scrolling body above it. Two footers, one of them wrong.
- **Now** a removal renames the sheet (*Remove rules*, *Remove <pack>*, *Undo a deployment*), labels the step strip as removal steps, and writes every step's buttons into the sheet's footer with a one-line summary: how many items are ticked, how many objects will be removed and kept, the write-mode or consent state before *Remove now*, *Stop* while it runs, the verification result at the end. Nothing is left in the body. Leaving the removal (Cancel, Back to packs) restores the deploy title, step strip and footer.
- **Verified.** A browser pass with the tenant calls answered by fakes: the deploy footer, then Remove rules → Select (title, step strip, footer with *Preview removal*, no *Run preflight*), a ticked rule enabling the button, Preview (*Continue to approve*), Approve (*Remove now* disabled on the read-only server, the consent box), Back, Cancel restoring the deploy chrome; no page errors. A test pins that every removal step writes the footer, none leaves buttons in the body, and the deploy renderer restores the title. Full suite green.

### Removal order: rules → activate → engines → activate → dictionaries → activate, always
- **Before.** A pack removal, a picked-rules removal and an Undo already deleted in tiers (rules and application groups, then engines, then dictionaries) and activated between tiers, but the activation after a tier ran only when that tier had deleted something: after a tier whose deletes were all refused or already gone, the next tier started on an unsettled tenant. The Inline DLP Rule migration wizard's own rule deletion (Step 4) deleted rules and never activated.
- **Now** every removal path runs the same sequence, unconditionally: rules deleted, activate and wait for ZIA to report ACTIVE; engines deleted, activate and wait; dictionaries deleted, activate and wait. The activation after a tier runs whenever a delete in that tier reached ZIA, whatever ZIA answered, and the log says so (`Activating after the rules (N deleted)…`); the result's `activations` list names the tier each activation followed (`rules`, `engines`, `dictionaries`, a retry pass by its tier), and `activation` is the last one issued. A run that sent no delete (stopped before its first call) activates nothing. The wizard's Step 4 activates after its rule deletes, whatever ZIA answered, and shows whether the activation ran. An activation issued inside a retry pass is recorded with its tier too, so the list reads the same throughout; a run whose last tier deleted nothing but was answered by ZIA still ends with that tier's activation. The removal review and the run screen state the sequence in those words; `docs/SHARED_EXECUTION_PATH.md` rule 2 too.
- **Verified.** A test pins the exact interleaving on the fake tenant (delete rule, activate, wait, delete engine, activate, wait, delete dictionary, activate, wait) and the refused-tier case (activation still runs after the rules; the engine and dictionary the surviving rule uses are kept, nothing more is activated). Full suite green. No tenant contacted.

### Migrate from another DLP: target rules on every channel (web, endpoint, SaaS Security API, email)
- **Before.** The compile emitted ZIA inline web DLP rules only; a decision on any other channel was refused as unsupported, so a source policy on email, removable media or cloud storage at rest could be inventoried and scored but not carried.
- **Now** a decision names its target channel (`web`, `endpoint`, `saas`, `email`), one decision per channel, and a source policy is carried to several channels by copying its entry. Each channel is compiled with the same conventions the policy packs deploy with (pinned by a parity test against `pack_channels` and `pack_terraform`): every rule `DISABLED`; the channel's own action vocabulary (web BLOCK / CONFIRM / ALLOW; endpoint ALLOW / BLOCK / CONFIRM; email ALLOW / BLOCK / CUSTOMHEADERINSERTION with `custom_header`; SaaS Security API OFLCASB_DLP_REPORT_INCIDENT / OFLCASB_DLP_ALLOW, with the intended remediation recorded as `designed_action` and never emitted); endpoint rules need `data_transfer_method` (one of the four, one per rule) and carry the four device trust levels, a CONFIRM rule binding the tenant's Endpoint DLP user-confirmation template through a Terraform variable; SaaS rules need `saas_type` and a name of at most 31 characters; SaaS and email rules take `tenants` as onboarded tenant names (`zia_casb_tenant` data lookups) or the word `variable` (a `list(number)` variable filled in at plan); orders are unique and contiguous per channel; every missing or invalid field is a question that blocks the run. The Terraform JSON carries `zia_dlp_web_rules`, `zia_endpoint_dlp_rules`, `zia_casb_dlp_rules` and `zia_outbound_email_dlp` with the data sources and variables they need.
- **Efficacy mapping.** A source policy with several target rules is scored once over all of them: the channel facet is now the share of the source's channel mentions (web, email, endpoint, SaaS API; downloads are never inspected) that the approved targets cover, and the mentions not covered are named in the arithmetic; the response-action facet needs an approved action on every target. The template lists each channel's actions and required fields under `_channels`; the cross-reference rows for channels name the four rule families and their API calls; the report's converted table shows the channel, action and any designed action; the counts are per channel.
- **Verified.** New tests: parity with the pack conventions, one policy to all four channels with the exact Terraform (every rule DISABLED, the endpoint template binding, tenant lookups and the variable form, the SaaS name cap, the 49.0 % efficacy over four targets), each channel's fail-closed questions (transfer method, trust levels, action vocabulary, SaaS remediation, type, name, tenants, header, a disposition mixed with rules, duplicate decisions, per-channel orders, unknown channel, the uncovered-channel arithmetic), the API and the page. A browser pass on the four-channel decisions. Full suite green.

### Evaluate All Rules: the "Simulate a transaction" section removed
- **Before.** Under the Evaluate All Rules decision diagram (in the Inline DLP Rule migration wizard) sat a simulator: a table of rules to edit, three presets, a channel selector, the engines a transaction matches, and a step-by-step verdict; Step 2 of the wizard handed its downloaded rules to it.
- **Now** the section is gone, as asked. What stays is the decision diagram itself (Rule Order lane and Evaluate All Rules lane, the most-restrictive-action step, the tie broken by the lowest order number, the winner's settings, which channels use each model, the Zscaler source), in the wizard and on the *How Evaluate All Rules decides* reference tab. The SaaS lane now says on the diagram what the simulator used to say in words: no tie-breaker on that channel, a stricter rule further down is shadowed until it is moved up. The hand-off from Step 2, the simulator's script, styles and the sidebar / explainer copy that mentioned it are removed.
- **Verified.** The page tests pin the diagram and the absence of the simulator (no rule table, presets, result box, channel selector or hand-off); a browser pass of the reference tab and the wizard; full suite green.

### Enable write mode works again in browsers that refuse prompt()
- **Before.** Deploy policy packs' *Enable write mode* asked for the confirmation phrase with the browser's `prompt()` and reported refusals with `alert()`. An embedded browser (the desktop wrapper, a kiosk shell, some corporate browsers) returns nothing from `prompt()` and drops `alert()`, so the click did nothing at all: no box, no request, no message. The same failure had already moved the app's own sign-in to an in-app dialog; the write-mode switch was still on the old path.
- **Now** the switch asks in the app's own dialog (`ui_auth.js` gained `phrase`, a typed-confirmation box that pre-checks the phrase and explains a mismatch, and `ask`, a yes / no question used by *Back to read-only*). The outcome is written into the write-mode banner as well as announced: cancelled ("Still read-only: no confirmation phrase was entered"), refused by the server (its sentence, plus a notice with what to do), or enabled. The classic `prompt()` / `confirm()` survive only as guarded fallbacks for a page without the dialog script, so an unsupported prompt reads as cancelled instead of a silent return. The server contract is unchanged: the phrase must be `YES_I_UNDERSTAND`, the server must be bound to localhost, anything else is a 403 with a sentence, and nothing is persisted across a restart.
- **Verified.** A browser pass with every browser dialog dismissed (the embedded case) and the write-mode call intercepted so the server never left read-only: the in-app box opens, Escape cancels with the banner note, a wrong phrase is refused in the box without a request, the right phrase sends exactly one request with the phrase, *Back to read-only* asks and sends the disable, a faked 403 lands in the banner and a notice. A regression test pins the switch off `prompt()` / `alert()`, the dialog API, the script order and the server phrase. Full suite green.

### Migrate from another DLP: Forcepoint, Netskope and Skyhigh adapters, each vendor's export file types read, the APIs cross-referenced to ZIA
- **What was researched.** Forcepoint DLP's Security Manager exports policies as a zip of one `<policy name>.json` per policy plus `policy_names_metadata.txt` (optionally password-protected, optionally with the classifier and resource definitions), the same JSON its REST API returns (`/dlp/rest/v1`, refresh-token → access-token sign-in; policy_name, enabled, policy_level, rules with rule_name, severity, action_plan, parts_count_type, condition_relation_type, classifiers with classifier_name, predefined, threshold_type / threshold_value_from / threshold_calculate_type, sources, destinations, exceptions). Netskope exports Real-time Protection policies from the UI as JSON or CSV (policy name, status, source, destination, activities, DLP profile, action; there is no create or read API for them), DLP profiles link rules, rules link entities (3000+ predefined identifiers, or custom dictionaries that export as one CSV each, one keyword or regex per line), the REST API v2 (`Netskope-Api-Token`) serves incidents. Skyhigh exports Policy Templates as a zip (at most 50 rule groups and 64 KB; rule groups → rules → classifications: keywords with proximity, dictionaries with scores, advanced patterns, 82 pre-canned classifications, IDM, EDM; response actions by rule-group severity; dictionaries exported separately), and its SSE Policy API returns policies with a type (0 API DLP, 1 Proxy DLP).
- **Now** all four vendors are *adapter ready* on the page. Each accepts its own file types (`.zip` / `.json` for Forcepoint; `.zip` / `.json` / `.csv` for Netskope; `.zip` / `.json` / `.xml` for Skyhigh; the wrong extension is refused with a sentence), the zip is opened in memory (password-protected, oversized or over-populated archives refused, nothing written to disk), and the policies are read into the same inventory the Symantec adapter produces: id, name, status, the detection facets recognised from the JSON keys or XML element names (classifier items when the export carries the definitions, predefined classifiers as identifiers, database and file fingerprinting as EDM and IDM, thresholds, channels normalised to web / email / endpoint / SaaS-API / cloud-download so the web share is scored the same way for every vendor, sources, file conditions, exceptions, actions, severity) and the keys it did not recognise. Netskope's profiles, rules and dictionary CSVs are joined to the policies by name, the names that were not in the upload are listed so the missing file can be added. The decisions template, the fail-closed compile, the DISABLED web rules with the vendor's prefix (`fp_`, `nsk_`, `sky_`) and the efficacy mapping are unchanged and now apply to every vendor.
- **The API cross-reference.** Every vendor carries a table, shown on the page under the vendor picker and again in the report (and in `migration-report.json`), of each source object and field → the ZIA object and field it lands on → the ZIA API v1 call (`dlpDictionaries`, `dlpEngines`, `webDlpRules`, the predefined-dictionary, EDM schema, IDM profile, notification-template, identity, cloud-app and incident-receiver reads; activation named as never called) → the Terraform resource → how it is carried (*generated* by the compile, *reviewed* in the decisions file, *attested* after building it by hand, *manual* on the rule after apply, *dropped* with no equivalent), with the export file types and the source API's base, sign-in and calls above it. The command line gained `crossref --vendor …`, and `fetch` accepts JSON responses and a named token header (`--header Netskope-Api-Token`) beside the Bearer default.
- **Honesty note.** The field names follow each vendor's published export and API documentation as of this build (the vendor documentation sites were not reachable from this environment, so the schemas were assembled from their published summaries); the inventory lists every key it does not recognise so a newer or differently shaped export is mapped rather than misread, and nothing is claimed for a facet that was not read.
- **Verified.** New tests: the registry (four adapters, file types matching what the page accepts, every efficacy facet with a cross-reference row, activation never called), Forcepoint zip and REST JSON (items from the classifier definitions, identifiers, EDM, thresholds, channels, exceptions, the empty policy), password-protected / bad / over-populated archives refused, Netskope zip joining JSON + CSV dictionaries and the CSV export alone with the unresolved profile named, Skyhigh JSON and XML (policy type, classifications, thresholds, DTD refused), each adapter compiled end to end through the API with its prefix and the exact Forcepoint arithmetic (36.9 %), the CLI, the page. A browser pass of all three vendors on fixture zips (inventory, cross-reference, compile to ready). Full suite green.

### Findings & report folded into the Dashboard
- **Before.** The sidebar had *Dashboard* and *Findings & report* as two workflow items that both landed on the same results: the tiles, the findings list and the exports are one page.
- **Now** the workflow is Dashboard, Connect to the tenant, Scope assessment. The Dashboard holds the score tiles, every finding and the exports; it becomes the current item when a run finishes. The step strip's third step reads *③ Dashboard: findings + export* and lands there. Nothing on the page moved; only the duplicate entry is gone.
- **Verified.** The sidebar pins (nav order, no review item, the step-strip mapping), the full suite.

### Migrate from another DLP: an efficacy mapping with the arithmetic shown
- **Before.** The migration report said which policies were converted and which were blocked, nothing about how much of each policy's detection the translation actually carries.
- **Now** the inventory recognises the detection facets under each source policy from the export's element names (keywords and regexes with their items, predefined data identifiers, EDM, IDM, ML, match thresholds, protocols, sender / recipient / group scope, file conditions, exceptions, response rules, severity) and lists the element names it did not recognise. Compile scores every facet present in a policy against the decisions: keyword and regex items ÷ the phrases and patterns in the referenced approved dictionaries; thresholds present in the referenced engine expressions; the web share of the protocols named; the approved action. What this adapter never emits (scope, file conditions, exceptions, severity) is fixed at 0 and says so. What no computation can know (predefined identifiers, EDM, IDM, ML) is 0 unless a reviewer attests it in the decisions file (`attest`, true / false / a fraction with a note; the template lists exactly those facets). Fidelity = weighted points carried ÷ weighted points present, weights stated on the page; the overall number is the same ratio over every scored policy. Retired policies are listed, not scored; an export with no recognisable detection element gets *not computable*, never a percentage. The report page shows the overall number, the method, and per policy a table of every facet with its score, points, basis (computed / attested / fixed) and the arithmetic; `migration-report.json` carries the same `efficacy` object.
- **Verified.** New tests on a richer synthetic export (facet recognition, the exact arithmetic to 44.7 % and 64.2 % after an attestation, retired and bare policies unscored, the bare fixture not computable); the page in a browser; the full suite.

### Data Security Architect inside the tool: every diagram and table, offline
- **What arrived.** The Architect app's thirty-three diagrams (the DLP chain, dictionaries, engines, the inline web gauntlet, endpoint, email, SaaS API and SSPM, EDM and IDM, AI security, ten Workflow Automation flows) and eleven screenshots of its reference tables (dictionary and engine limits, what reaches DLP, the AI Guard journeys, per-channel gotchas, the setup sequences, the symptom walkbacks).
- **Now** `/reference/data-security-architect` (static/reference/data-security-architect.html, diagrams as WebP under static/reference/dsa, 3.7 MB for all thirty-three, the largest kept at 8000 px so their small type stays readable) carries them in the app's module order, each diagram in a figure that opens full size in a lightbox (fit or scroll), the tables transcribed as HTML, the setup sequences with their gates marked, the walkbacks as ordered lists. The Reference group's *Data Security Architect* button opens it like the other framework pages; the four flow pages link to its inline-web module as the architecture they are measured against; the live Google Sites source is linked from the page's header, never loaded. The external-link stopgap from earlier today is replaced.
- **Verified.** Every diagram served and once; the section ids, the transcribed limits, the lightbox; the page in a browser in light and dark with all images loaded, no failed requests, no page errors; the full suite.

### Migrate from another DLP: the Symantec adapter in the sidebar, Forcepoint / Netskope / Skyhigh to follow
- **What it is.** A new page under *Close the gaps* (`/report/vendor`, `vendor_migrate.py`) that takes another vendor's DLP policy export and, through decisions a person reviewed, produces disabled ZIA dictionaries, engines and web rules as Terraform for the zscaler/zia provider. It is the uploaded stand-alone Symantec converter folded into the app with its contract intact: the inventory reads only each policy's id, name and status (DTDs and entities refused); the decisions file is bound to the export's SHA-256 and needs an approved translation or an approved retirement for every policy and an approval for every dictionary and engine; compile is fail-closed (a report with the questions, no Terraform, until nothing is blocked); every generated rule is `DISABLED` and applies to Any user and location; nothing contacts or writes to a tenant. The command line keeps the same steps (`inventory`, `compile`) plus the export fetcher (`fetch`: one HTTPS GET, no redirects, size-capped, written 0600).
- **The page.** Pick the vendor (Symantec DLP ready; Forcepoint, Netskope and Skyhigh listed as planned and refused with a plain sentence until an export sample is mapped), upload the export, inventory it, download the decisions template (SHA-256 filled in, every policy listed with nothing approved), upload the reviewed decisions, compile, read the report (converted, questions that block, retired), download `migration-report.json` and, when ready, `main.tf.json` for the Terraform hand-off.
- **Verified.** The converter's own tests ported (ready output, stale SHA-256 refused, unknown channel blocks everything and removes stale Terraform, unapproved retirement blocks, XML entities refused) plus the registry, the two API calls (token-gated), the page and the explainer. Full suite green.

### One page to scope and run: "Scope assessment"; the tenant configuration keeps sign-in details only; Quick start removed
- **Before.** The sidebar had *Scope* (channels, scope, frameworks) and *Run assessment* as two items that covered the same ground, the tenant configuration card mixed sign-in details with the organisation and assessor names, and a separate *Quick start assessment* page repeated the sidebar workflow in prose.
- **Now** the workflow is Dashboard, Connect to the tenant, **Scope assessment**, Findings & report. *Scope assessment* holds the channels, the assessment scope, the compliance frameworks, a *Report details* card (organisation, assessor; moved there, not copied) and the **Run assessment** button; the run signs in first when needed and lands on the Dashboard. *Connect to the tenant* holds sign-in details only (cloud, sign-in method, vanity domain, API client or the classic ZIA sign-in, ZPA customer ID) plus the tenant profile picker that fills them. The step strip reads ① Connect → ② Scope assessment → ③ Review + export. A blank organisation or assessor sends you to Report details; a blank sign-in field sends you to Connect. The Quick start page, its route, its toolbar button, its help link and its explainer are gone; the help page's first-engagement list starts at Connect.
- **Verified.** Full suite (pins updated: nav order, step strip, the runner, the explainers, the page lists); a browser pass of the new page in both directions (missing report details, missing sign-in, a run on fixture data).

### Channel ownership drives the assessment
- **Before.** The Scope page's Channels list (Inline Web DLP, Endpoint DLP, Email DLP, CASB, GenAI, SSL inspection, ...) with its Zscaler / Alternative choices and vendor notes was kept in the browser and read by nothing: an assessor who marked Endpoint DLP as covered by another vendor still saw every Endpoint DLP check scored as a gap.
- **Now** the declaration goes with the assessment (`channel_scope.py`). A channel marked *Alternative* has its checks reported as *Not in scope*: the tenant is not read for them, each row says which channel and vendor, and they sit outside the score, next to *Not permitted* and *Could not be checked* in the same vocabulary (`permissions.CLASSES`, `docs/SCORING.md`). A channel marked *Zscaler* or left unticked (a gap) runs exactly as before, because a gap's findings are its evidence. The mapping from channel to checks is deliberately narrow (whole sections only where the section is the channel; the "5 — Endpoint DLP" section, which is really the general DLP section, contributes only its ZDP-install check). The choice is saved on the tenant profile and restored when the profile is picked; the report carries `channel_coverage` (the JSON export, a *Channel Coverage* section in the Word report, a Key insights line on the dashboard, the Completion tile and the coverage sentence).
- **Verified.** New tests: every mapped check and section exists; only *Alternative* skips, and the findings keep their section order; the stream reports the skipped checks first and the tenant is never called for them; the class is outside the score and the run stays *complete*; the profile round-trip; the Word report text; the page and dashboard pins. Full suite green.

### Sidebar walk-through: every page opened one at a time, four defects fixed
- **What was done.** Every item in the sidebar (the five workflow steps and the twenty tool pages, including the four framework reference pages) was opened individually in a browser in both themes, with the assessment run on fixture data and every tenant call answered locally, and each screen was inspected.
- **Fixed.** The in-app notice dialog (sign-in failure, unlock) sat in the top-left corner: the stylesheet's global `margin: 0` reset cancelled the native dialog's centring, so the dialog now sets `margin: auto` itself. The Scope step's *Select all* control rendered as an empty text field because the report stylesheet's text-input rule also matched checkboxes; the rule now excludes checkboxes and radios. The *Traffic flow & gaps* tab showed "[object Object]" when the tenant refused the read, because the data-flow, bypass and SSL-flow generators threw the server's `{cause, fix}` detail as-is; they now throw its text. The *Back to the assessment* link injected into the framework reference pages was white-on-white (those pages define none of the app's tokens) and covered the page's own brand; it has a literal colour fallback and sits bottom-left.
- **Left as is.** The four framework reference pages keep their own visual language (gradients, a light theme only): they are portable documents kept byte-for-byte, not app screens.
- **Verified.** The walk-through again after the fixes (no page errors, no leftover emoji, no horizontal scroll, no blur or gradient outside the reference pages), the dialog centred, the checkbox 18 px square, the error tab reading as a sentence; the full suite with four new pins.

### Human Interface Guidelines, stage 5: the report pages on the same tokens
- **Before.** The fifteen report pages, the help and quick-start pages and the deployment-flow page kept their own look: gradient title text, blurred translucent bars and cards, dot-grid diagram backgrounds, glow rings on selected steps and rules, about a hundred colour literals of their own (semantic tints, neutral fills, light-theme overrides), three monospace stacks, and page titles in Title Case split across markup ("Data Flow Map", "Path Analyzer", "Regulatory & Compliance Alignment").
- **Now** `reports.css` and every page's own stylesheet read the stage-1 tokens: semantic tints through `color-mix` on the pass / warn / fail / accent colours, neutral fills through the well tokens, one monospace stack, token radii, no gradient, glow, blur or dot grid; the light-theme colour overrides are gone because the tokens already carry accessible values; the report pages' theme toggle uses the card colour with no blur; the page titles are sentence case (*Data flow map*, *Path analyzer*, *Live DLP feed*, *Regulatory & compliance alignment*, *Bypass & non-inspected traffic*, *Quick start assessment*, *SSL inspection flow*, *FP/TP detection ratio*, *Deployment flow*).
- **Verified.** The full suite; every report page, help and quick start in a browser in both themes: no element with a blur or a gradient, the symbol set loaded, titles in sentence case, no page errors.

### Human Interface Guidelines, stage 4: labels in sentence case
- **Before.** Buttons, headings, tab titles, form labels and status pills mixed Title Case ("Run Assessment", "Download Word Report", "Detailed Findings", "Organization Name", "Action Required") with sentence case ("Run assessment", "Findings & report") from page to page.
- **Now** every UI label is sentence case, the HIG writing style: the toolbar and sidebar tools (*Quick start assessment*, *Traffic flow & gaps*, *Path analyzer*, *Live DLP feed*, *Deploy policy packs*, *Log analyzer* …), the step strip, the result cards (*Scores by section*, *Best practices & benchmarks*, *Regulatory & compliance alignment*, *Detailed findings*), the tenant form (*Organization name*, *Vanity domain*, *API client secret* …), the dialogs and the migration wizard, the dashboard tiles (*Overall maturity score*, *Key insights*), the status pills (*Action required*, *Needs review*, *Not configured*) and the report-page titles. Product and feature names keep their own capitals: Zscaler, Terraform, ZIA, ZPA, ZCC, ZIdentity, OneAPI, Client Connector, Workflow Automation, Evaluate All Rules, Secure by Design, and names of things in other products as those products show them (*API Key Management*, *Client Secret* in the ZIdentity steps). No wording changed, only casing; the Markdown and Word exports are untouched.
- **Verified.** The nine tests that pinned the old casing now pin the new; the full suite.

### Human Interface Guidelines, stage 3: one symbol set instead of emoji
- **Before.** About 150 emoji stood in for icons: the sidebar, every toolbar button, the dashboard tiles, insights and actions, the findings rows (drawn as CSS generated text that screen readers read aloud), dialog titles, step chips, the framework checkboxes, report-page buttons. They rendered differently on every platform and font, and a few were bare text inside a button's accessible name.
- **Now** `static/icons.js` is a bundled symbol set: 70 symbols drawn as inline SVG on a 24-point grid in `currentColor`, so each takes the colour and size of the text it sits in and looks the same on Windows, macOS and Linux, offline. The emoji stay in the markup as the readable fallback; at load, and whenever new content renders, each one is swapped for its symbol inside an `aria-hidden` span, and the findings rows get a real icon element instead of generated text. The sidebar, tiles, findings, dialogs, Deploy Studio and report pages all draw from the same set. SF Symbols themselves are licensed for Apple platforms only, so this is an SF-style set of the app's own.
- **Verified.** `tests/test_icons.py` (every emoji has a symbol, every symbol has a path, the script is first on the page and on every report page); the full suite; in a browser: no emoji left visible on the landing, Connect, results, findings, Studio or Digest pages.

### Human Interface Guidelines, stage 1: one token layer, flat surfaces, the system type scale
- **What you asked for.** "The UI should be aligned with Apple's Human Interface Guidelines", with the stack unchanged. This is the first of five stages; it replaces the look without touching a flow.
- **Before.** A dark navy glass theme: a radial page gradient, translucent blurred cards, cyan and purple glows on the rings, bands and buttons, gradient text in the title, gradient action buttons, hover lifts and shimmer animations; sizes, spacing and radii chosen per element (54 distinct font sizes, a quarter of the spacing on an 8px grid); every add-on stylesheet carrying its own colour literals.
- **Now** `static/index.html`'s first stylesheet (served as `/app.css`) is the one place colours, type, spacing and radii are defined, and the add-ons (`dashboard.css`, `guide.css`, `deploy_studio.css`, the dialog styles in `ui_auth.js`) read those tokens. Colours follow the system palette: grouped backgrounds (base, card, well) separated by hairlines, one accent (system blue, an accessible variant in light), semantic green / orange / red with their accessible light variants, secondary and tertiary labels; no gradient, glow, blur or lift anywhere on the main page. The system font (`-apple-system`, Segoe UI on Windows) at a fixed scale (large title, title 1–3, headline, body, callout, subheadline, footnote, caption), an 8pt spacing grid and one set of radii. Buttons are filled, tinted or bordered, one height each; destructive and write actions are a flat semantic fill; the findings filter is a segmented control; status pills are tinted capsules; section headers are grouped-list titles; the sidebar is a source list with the selected row filled in the accent; the rings and bars are a single accent stroke; the Deploy Studio sheet, steps, rows, switch and chips use the same tokens. Light and dark are the same layout on their own tokens.
- **Verified.** The full suite (three tests that pinned old colour literals now pin the new ones); the page in a browser in both themes: landing, Connect, results, findings, Deploy Studio. Report pages, icons and label casing follow in the next stages.

### The three branches are named the standard way: dev → qa → main
- **What you asked for.** "Standardise the branch names."
- **Before.** The promotion branches were `dev`, `DLP-CHECK-QA` and `main`: one of the three carried the project name in capitals, and the README had to explain that "`qa`" meant `DLP-CHECK-QA` on GitHub.
- **Now** the QA branch is **`qa`** on GitHub (created from the same commit as `dev` and `main`, which were already level), and every place that names it says `qa`: the Update app dropdown (*QA (qa)*), `UPDATE_Zscaler_Assessment.bat qa`, `python app_update.py --branch qa`, the README, SECURITY.md. An install still checked out on `DLP-CHECK-QA` is not stranded: `app_update` maps the old name to `qa` (`LEGACY_BRANCHES`), so its status and its next Update move it onto `qa`. The old branch is removed on GitHub once this change is on `main`, so the mapping is the only path left to it. Feature branches keep the `area/topic` convention (`ux/…`, `packs/…`, `saas/…`).
- Verified: `tests/test_app_update.py` — the allowlist is exactly `main, qa, dev`, the dropdown offers those three, the old name resolves to `qa`, and an origin without a `qa` branch is refused before anything moves.

### UI/UX stage 6: bars before the radar, nothing floating over the findings, focus you can see
- **What you asked for.** The brief's sixth priority: visual polish and accessibility — the radar replaced by bars or a table as the primary view, the Help button that overlapped buttons and cards, a layout that holds at 960px, keyboard focus, dialogs that give focus back, colour contrast.
- **Before.** The dashboard's third tile was a radar: unreadable under three sections, plotting an unmeasured section at 0 and printing *null%* in its labels and legend. The floating *? Help* button sat over the bottom-left of the findings and, at narrow widths, over the sidebar's own foot; the off-canvas sidebar stayed in the tab order with no backdrop and no Escape. A keyboard focus on a tenant field showed only a faint glow (`outline: none`); *Select all* / *Clear all* on the frameworks were anchors without `href`, unreachable by keyboard; the remediation wizard, the Deploy Studio and the migration wizard did not return focus on close. *Not configured* chips (`#64748b`), the amber→red buttons with white text and the light-theme *High Risk* chip were below 4.5:1; the ZCC diagnosis dialog stayed dark in the light theme; the report pages' theme toggle was unreadable in light. The results block and the dashboard had no landmark, the tile titles were plain divs, and the page root carried `role="document"`. Between 1001 and 1100px the three hero tiles shared a 750px column; six summary tiles at 960px wrapped four-then-two.
- **Now.** The tile is **Score by section**: one bar per section with its percentage (or *—* and "not measured"), readable at any width and by a screen reader; the ⤢ button opens the radar as the expanded view, disabled under three measured sections, and neither the radar nor its legend plots or prints an unmeasured section as a number. *? Help* is docked in the sidebar's brand row (always on screen, never over the content); the narrow-width sidebar is `visibility: hidden` until opened, gets a backdrop that closes it, closes on Escape and hands focus back to the menu button. Form fields show a 3px focus ring on keyboard focus; the two framework links are buttons; the wizard, the Studio and the migration wizard return focus to what opened them. `--info` is `#94a3b8`, the gradient buttons are `#b45309→#b91c1c`, the placeholder uses `--dim`, the *High Risk* chip uses `--warn`, the ZCC dialog follows the light theme, the report toggle uses the card colour. The results block is a labelled region, the dashboard is a labelled region with `h3` tile titles, `role="document"` is gone, and the three emoji-only prefixes are hidden from screen readers. The hero grid goes to two columns below 1250px (the sidebar takes 264px of the viewport) and the summary tiles to two columns below 1000px.
- **Verified.** `tests/test_polish.py`, the full suite; in a browser at 1280×900 and 960×800: the bars, the radar behind the button with a clean legend, focus back on the button after Escape, the Help button inside the sidebar and not over the footer, the focus ring on a field reached by keyboard, the Studio returning focus, and at 960px the sidebar hidden and out of the tab order, opened with a backdrop and closed with Escape, two-column tiles and no horizontal scroll.

### UI/UX stage 5: the deployment plan counts one way, conflicts are not "skipped", and write mode says what it is
- **What you asked for.** The brief's fifth priority: deployment planning and hand-off clarity — pack vs channel selection, a summary that adds up, overlaps and conflicts told apart from create/reuse/skip, write-mode copy that is true.
- **Before.** The plan's hero said *N changes* (create + modify + overwrite), the *Will create* tile counted creates only, the gate said *N objects to create* but counted modifies and overwrites too, and the screen-reader announcement used a fourth number. Conflicts (two packs defining a name differently, or a predefined Zscaler name) sat in the same *Skipped* tile and section as a missing prerequisite or an empty dictionary. Write mode was described as *for this server session* and *enabled from this window* although it is server-wide, for every browser, until the process restarts; the prompt and banner said *existing objects are never overwritten* although the default *Apply confidence tweaks* modifies the confidence of existing dictionaries (listed as *Will modify*); the approve step said in one card that *an admin must activate changes afterwards* and in the consent line that *changes are activated automatically*; the result page pointed to a button called *Export Terraform* that is labelled *Hand off as Terraform*; the review promised a first `terraform plan` that *is exactly this list* although gap mode also imports what exists.
- **Now.** One count vocabulary (`_dzCounts`) feeds the hero (*2 objects to create · 1 to modify · 1 kept as-is · 1 conflict · 2 skipped · 2 already in place*), the five tiles (*Will create*, *Will modify* / *overwrite*, *Kept as-is*, *Skipped / conflicts*, *Tenant rules*), the gates (*The plan can be deployed (2 objects to create, 1 to modify)*, *Nothing to write: every object is skipped or in conflict …*) and the announcement. Conflicts have their own section (*resolve before deploying: deselect a pack or rename*) and a warning gate; skips are *Skipped: needs a prerequisite* or *Skipped: empty*; *Already present* says its content matched where the tenant let it be compared. Write mode: *Enable write mode on this server? It applies to every browser and tab using this server, and stays on until the server restarts*; the banner, the announcement and the gate say the same; every "never overwritten" is now "not overwritten" with the confidence tweak named and counted (*1 confidence tweak on existing dictionaries, listed above as Will modify*), and the *How to read this plan* fold explains *Skipped vs conflict* and where the one kind of change to an existing object comes from. The approve checklist and the consent line agree: the deploy activates the changes when it completes; if activation cannot run, the result says so and an admin activates in the portal. The Select step's footer names the channels each pack will deploy to and says preflight writes nothing. The Terraform copy says the first plan creates what this list creates and imports what exists.
- **Verified.** `tests/test_deploy_clarity.py`, the full suite; in a browser with a mocked preflight (read-only server, no write mode, nothing deployed): the select footer, the read-only banner, the review's hero/tiles/sections/gates on a plan with every status, the approve step's checklist, consent text and gates, *Deploy to tenant* disabled.

### UI/UX stage 4: the Digest says what the loop covers, a compliance percentage says what it is, and CISA means this app
- **What you asked for.** The brief's fourth priority: monitoring and Digest clarity, and the "CISA daily check" — inspect before asserting, never invent compliance coverage.
- **What was found.** There is no daily evaluation of any tenant and nothing in the app scores a tenant against CISA. The Digest's loop runs every six hours *while the server process runs*, only for tenant profiles that remember their secret, and reads FP/TP Detection Ratio, the SaaS tenants and pack drift; it never runs the assessment checks or the score. "CISA Secure by Design score" in Advanced settings scores Zscaler Made Easy itself (the tool), and the Deploy Studio's Secure by Design fold scores a deployed pack's own objects. The only daily job is the upstream contract check in GitHub Actions (SDK, Terraform provider and API reference against what the app sends), which is not a CISA check and was not visible in the app. A framework percentage was the share of mapped checks that passed, and read 100% when nothing scoreable mapped to the framework; the page said "8 major regulatory frameworks" while listing 13.
- **Now.** The Digest is *the six-hour loop*: its hero, the sidebar tooltip and the help entry say when it runs (while the server runs; Task Scheduler or cron for a schedule that outlives it), what it reads, and that it does not re-run the assessment; the verdict says what the run covered (*No tenant was evaluated: no tenant profile remembers its secret …*) and a *Tenants evaluated* tile counts them; a manual *Run the loop now* keeps the same last-run bookkeeping as the scheduled run; the dead `_dh_refresh_once` and its stale comment are gone. A framework percentage is now *None / Not measured* when nothing scoreable maps to it, never 100 (the same rule now holds for the best-practice alignment and each programme stage in the Benchmarks card and its Markdown export, which used to print *100%* on nothing measured and *None%* for an unscored assessment); every consumer (results chips, benchmark chips, the compliance report, the Word report, the Markdown export) shows *Not measured* or *n/a* and carries the basis in words: *2 of 3 mapped checks configured (not a share of the framework's controls met)*; the Compliance Coverage and Regulatory Alignment copy say the percentage is evidence of alignment, not a certification, and no longer say "8 frameworks". Advanced settings says **CISA Secure by Design score — for this app, not the tenant**, and the Deploy Studio fold says **Secure by Design (this pack's objects in the tenant)**. A new **Daily upstream contract check** section explains the GitHub Actions job (06:23 UTC, `upstream-drift` issue on drift, not a CISA check, not a tenant check, its result not readable from this server) and runs the check's offline half on this server on demand through `GET /api/upstream-check` (installed SDK and committed Terraform against the shipped packs' payloads; under a second; cached an hour).
- **Verified.** `tests/test_monitoring_clarity.py`, `tests/test_benchmarks.py` (unmeasured frameworks sort last), the full suite; in a browser: the results and benchmark chips with their basis in the tooltip, the Advanced page's two headings and the offline check's result and re-run, the Digest hero, verdict, *Tenants evaluated* tile and schedule tile.

### UI/UX stage 3: findings say what class they are, and Remediate works on real findings
- **What you asked for.** The brief's third priority: findings and remediation clarity — which findings count, which are remediable, no pre-selection read as consent.
- **Before.** The row's 🔧 Remediate button, the wizard's criteria and Apply used a matcher that ignored the `checks` a recipe names, so on every real finding ("4.1", "A.1" …) the button answered *No remediation recipe registered* and the wizard's preview failed with *'Rule name' is required*. A refused read and a failed read were counted under the *Not Configured* tile and filter, and a refused INFO row's plain text said *is not configured or not in use*. Review & Remediate labelled rows *FAIL*/*REVIEW* and said *N remediable finding(s)* with no denominator; the dashboard tile counted every failure as "to fix". A field edited after the preview left *Apply now* on screen for values that were never previewed.
- **Now.** One matcher for every path, in the server's order (recipe id, the checks it names, prefix), so the row button, the wizard and Apply all resolve the same recipe; verified on "4.1" → *Add SSL Inspection Rule*. Each class has its own tile, filter (with counts: *Not permitted (1)*, shown only when non-zero) and pill: **Not Configured** is informational only, **Not permitted** and **Could not be checked** are their own, with a tooltip saying they sit outside the score. A refused read's plain text says *could not be read: the API client is not permitted to* with the role to grant, never "not configured". Review & Remediate says *2 of 4 open findings have a recipe*, labels rows *Action required*/*Needs review*, and its intro states that ticking only queues the walkthrough: nothing changes until each step is previewed, applied and confirmed, and only with write mode on. The dashboard tile reads *2 to fix · 2 with a one-click recipe*. Editing any field after a preview hides *Apply now* until the new values are previewed. The findings card explains which statuses count and what 🔧 means; the guide no longer promises "Critical findings first".
- **Verified.** `tests/test_findings.py`, `tests/test_remediation.py` (the matcher's order), `tests/test_plain_english.py` (refused vs failed reads), the full suite; in a browser with a fixture run: six tiles and seven filters with the right counts, the *Not permitted* filter shows only the refused row, the row button opens the recipe with no alert, the review count and labels, Apply hidden → shown after preview → hidden after an edit with the message.

### UI/UX stage 2: one way to run, one way back, results that survive a reload
- **What you asked for.** The brief's second priority: one navigation model — the same action behaves the same way wherever it is clicked, labels say what they open, context is not lost between pages.
- **Before.** "Run assessment" did two different things: the sidebar ran on the page, while the step strip and the guide's steps 2 and 7 opened a mirrored results tab. "Findings & report" with nothing run threw a browser `alert()` and then started a run. "Run Another Assessment" did not run anything (it cleared the form). A reload of the page, or a report tab's *Back* once the opening tab was gone, lost the whole result. Two buttons were both called "Evaluate All Rules" with the same icon (the migration wizard and the reference page); "Zscaler Updates" sat next to "Update app"; two icons were used twice. The Digest's *FP/TP Detection Ratio* link opened an empty report. Report pages opened on a 300–500-character paragraph before the content.
- **Now.** Every "Run assessment" (sidebar, step ③, guide steps 2 and 7) runs on this page through the same runner: sign in first if needed, then run, results below; *↗ Open in new tab* on the results is the explicit way to mirror them. "Findings & report" with no results shows an inline note and puts the focus on Run; nothing starts by itself. "Run Another Assessment" is **New assessment (clears results)** and the tile says *clears these results, then run again*. The last result is kept in this tab's `sessionStorage` (this tab only, gone when it closes) and comes back after a reload labelled **Restored from this tab · Assessed <time>**, with a status line saying so; a new run replaces it and *New assessment* clears it. **Rule Migration wizard** (🔀, opens on this page) and **How Evaluate All Rules decides** (📖, reference tab); **Zscaler SDK & docs updates**; no icon used twice. After a run the sidebar's current item is *Findings & report*. The Digest's FP/TP link opens the report from the assessment page (which holds the tenant sign-in) or says where to open it. Report-page intros fold to their first sentence behind a real *More* button (`aria-expanded`, keyboard).
- **Verified.** `tests/test_navigation.py`; the full suite; in a browser with a fixture stream: no-results note and focus, step ③ with empty fields lands on Connect with the sign-in note, the guide's Run runs on the page (no new tab), reload restores the result with its label and rows, *New assessment* then reload shows nothing, the Data Flow Map intro folds and unfolds, the Digest link opens no empty page.

### UI/UX stage 1: the app tells the truth about what it assessed, and Authenticate ends in the app's own words
- **What you asked for.** The UI/UX brief's first priority: no invented state, score or coverage; every score explains what it counts; authentication feedback that names the real cause, in an accessible in-app dialog rather than the browser's `alert()`/`prompt()` (which an embedded browser may not support).
- **Before.** A fresh page opened with *Score 0 — Ad Hoc* and *Completion 0%* although nothing had run. A tenant that refused the API client on some checks was scored as if those checks had failed (a *Not permitted* check looked identical to *Not Configured*), so the score dropped for reasons the customer could not fix by configuring anything. A section with no scoreable check showed *0%*. The score formula lived only in the code. Authenticate ended in a browser `alert()` starting *SUCCESS —* or *FAIL — you fat-fingered it*, the UI token was asked for by `prompt()` in a loop every time a background call hit a 401, and a missing token, a browser unable to show a dialog, an unreachable server and a tenant refusal all read as "could not connect".
- **Now — state and scores** (`docs/SCORING.md` is the one place the semantics are written down; `permissions.assessment_summary` is the one place they are computed, and the same numbers feed the page, the dashboard, the DOCX and the MCP summary): the score is *configured ÷ (configured + action required + needs review)*, INFO and ERROR checks are outside it, a check the tenant refused is *Not permitted* and outside it too, and an empty scoreable set is *Not assessed* rather than 0. The page opens as *Not assessed yet · no run in this tab yet*; during a run the completion figure follows the progress events; a finished run says *Partial · 10 of 12 assessed · 2 not permitted* with a coverage sentence, sections without a scoreable check say *Not measured*, refused rows say *Not permitted* and errored rows *Could not be checked*. The DOCX title page carries *Assessed: 3 of 4 (1 not permitted)* and *Overall Score: 50% — 1 configured of 2 scoreable* with the formula in the executive summary. `/api/assess`'s complete event carries the `summary`. Nothing about the checks themselves changed.
- **Now — Authenticate** (`static/ui_auth.js`, a native `<dialog>` with focus trapped, `Escape` to close, focus returned): the UI token is asked for once in the app's own dialog (password field, cancel respected for 30 s so background calls stop nagging; a deliberate Authenticate asks again). Success stays inline — announced, focus on the *Connected* card, the next step named. A failure opens one dialog with the cause, what to do and the technical detail, and the four causes are told apart: *This app needs its own sign-in first* (token missing — nothing reached Zscaler), *This browser could not show the sign-in dialog*, *The tool's own server did not answer*, and the tenant's own answer (*Zscaler rejected the client ID or client secret* or *Signed in, but not allowed to read …* for a 403). A double click sends one request; the button is disabled and marked busy while it runs. The helper copy for the client secret and the classic password now says what the code does (kept for the session unless *Remember* is ticked; keyring or an owner-only file).
- **Verified.** `tests/test_scoring.py` (the empty set, the division, a real run's numbers, states, the DOCX text, the complete event, the page strings), `tests/test_page_layout.py` (both Authenticate buttons end in the same function, the dialog files and copy, no `alert()` in the verdict), the full suite; in a browser: a fixture run from *Not assessed* through progress to *Partial · 10 of 12 assessed*, and the six sign-in paths (no token, invalid credentials, 403, double click, success, a browser whose `prompt()` throws) each ending in the right words with no native dialog shown.
- **Known limit.** In an embedded browser that can show neither `<dialog>` nor `prompt()`, the token cannot be typed at all; the app says so and points to the launcher, which carries the token in the URL.

## 2026-09-28

### Daily upstream contract check: the SDK, the Terraform provider and Zscaler's API reference against what the app sends
- **What you asked for.** "The SDK changed — look at the Terraform provider, the SDK and Zscaler automation and cross-check this with the current configurations. I need this to be an automated check, scheduled daily."
- **What the cross-check found today.** `zscaler-sdk-python` 1.10.0 (18 Sept) is newer than the installed 1.9.44 but changes only the AI Guard client — none of the 26 modules the app calls. The 2.0.0b2 prerelease is a new package (`zssdk`, httpx + pydantic, a data-driven resource layer) whose machine-readable ZIA service definition (apiVersion 2026-03-01) has operations for dictionaries, engines, web and email DLP rules and activation, with every field the app sends present, but **no operation at all for `/casbDlpRules` or `/endPointDlpRules`** — moving to 2.x as it stands would drop the SaaS and Endpoint channels. The Terraform provider `zscaler/zia` is at 4.8.9 (25 Sept), inside the pinned `~> 4.8`; its docs omit `rank` and `protocols` on `zia_dlp_web_rules` although the provider accepts them (CI's `terraform validate` proves it). Every SaaS payload already carries `rank`; every field the app sends on every channel is known to the installed SDK.
- **Now** `upstream_check.py` does that cross-check automatically. It computes what the app sends from the shipped packs through the real builders (every dictionary, engine and rule field, all four channels, extension rules included) and the attributes the Terraform generator emits from the committed workspaces, then checks them against: the installed SDK's signatures, documented keyword arguments and model wire fields (offline; `make check` and every CI push); PyPI's latest release, downloaded and diffed on the modules the app calls, so "a new version exists" is separated from "it touches what we send"; the 2.x prerelease's service definition (an operation per endpoint, every sent field a request-body member); the provider's latest version against the pin, its real schema (`terraform providers schema -json` on the runner) and its docs; and whether automate.zscaler.com answers. Findings are *drift* (exit 1) or *note*, in words, with the full field lists in the report.
- **Scheduled daily** (`.github/workflows/upstream_check.yml`, 06:23 UTC, also on demand): drift opens or updates one issue labelled `upstream-drift` with the report and fails the job; a clean day closes the issue. The report is attached to every run and shown in the job summary. `make upstream` runs it by hand.
- Verified: `tests/test_upstream_check.py` — the payload keys come from the real builders for every channel; every key is known to the installed SDK and the offline run is clean; a made-up field is drift; the service-definition check passes on a definition shaped like 2.0.0b2's and flags a missing `/casbDlpRules` operation and an unknown field; version ordering and PyPI parsing; the provider constraint parser (the provider's pin, not Terraform's `required_version`); the doc parser reads from the schema section on; the committed workspaces parse; docs alone only note, the schema settles it; the workflow, the CI step, the Makefile target and the host allowlist. The online run was exercised from this checkout against PyPI and raw GitHub (the sandbox proxy blocks the registry and automate.zscaler.com; those paths report the refusal as a note).

### A rule refused on rank is created at the other rank, so it always deploys
- **What you asked for.** "The rule rank for SaaS must be set to 0 so it always deploys." Every SaaS rule the app sends already carries rank 0 (shipped packs and the channel-selection rules alike; the SDK passes it verbatim), and the real tenant still answered `403 RBA_LIMITED — Rule rank is higher than the admin rank.` — then the app mistook that for an ordering problem and tried orders 3, 2 and 1 against the same answer.
- **Now** a rank refusal is its own case (`pack_deploy._rank_refusal`): the rule is retried once at the other rank (7 when 0 was refused, 0 when 7 was) at the same order, on SaaS and on every other channel, and the log says *ZIA refused rank 0 (…); trying rank 7 — the API client's admin rank does not allow rank 0 on SaaS rules* and then *created at rank 7 instead of 0*; the result carries `rank_note`. Rank 0 stays the default, so a tenant whose API client can use it gets it; one that cannot gets the rule at rank 7 rather than not at all. The order retry no longer fires on a rank refusal.
- The strict ZIA test fake models the tenant's answer (`strict_tenant(saas_admin_rank=7)` refuses rank 0 on SaaS rules the way the real one did).
- Verified: `tests/test_run_control.py` — against the plain strict tenant every SaaS rule of the enterprise pack lands at rank 0 with nothing retried; against the rank-refusing one all nine land at rank 7, nine *trying rank 7* and nine *created at rank 7 instead of 0* lines, no *refused every order tried*, no failures, and no rank note on web or endpoint rules.

### Every refusal says whether it is a 403 or a 429, in words
- **What you asked for.** "Can this app let me know if it is a 403 or 429?" — after a run in which ZIA answered `Rule rank is higher than the admin rank` and then `Resource Access Blocked` on everything, and the log showed raw JSON for the rules but only *could not read: dictionaries* for the read-back.
- **Now** every refused line names the number and what it means: *failed — Resource Access Blocked. What this means: HTTP 403 — ZIA blocked the API client's access to this resource (not a rate limit) …*; *HTTP 429 — ZIA's rate limit: too many requests in the window (not a permission problem); the app waits and retries*; *HTTP 403 — the rule's rank is above the API client's admin rank*. The status is pulled out of whatever the SDK hands back — a JSON body as text, a dict, or its repr — so it is there even when ZIA only gave a blob (`pack_ops._error_parts`, `http_label`, `describe_error`; the per-object events carry `http_status`).
- **The tenant read says which list was refused and why**: *could not read: dictionaries (HTTP 403: Resource Access Blocked)*, in the log and in the plan's warning. **The read-back no longer calls unreadable objects missing**: *93 object(s) could not be verified — the tenant refused to be read: dictionaries (HTTP 403: Resource Access Blocked); …*, each such object marked `unverified` with the reason, and *not found* kept for objects the tenant did list without.
- **Authenticate's FAIL popup** names them too: *HTTP 429 — ZIA is rate-limiting this API client …* (wait a minute, try again), *HTTP 403 — ZIA blocked the API client's access (Resource Access Blocked). Not a rate limit …* (wait, then check the role), and *HTTP 403 — … a permission, not a rate limit* for a plain forbidden.
- Verified: `tests/test_run_control.py` — the three error shapes yield the status; the labels for 403 (blocked, rank, plain), 429, 5xx; the log line and event for a 403 and a 429; a read-back with dictionaries refused 403 names it in the read line, marks the dictionary unverified with the status, and says *could not be verified*, not *not found*; the Authenticate explainer for a 429, a blocked 403 and a plain 403.

### Authenticate ends with a SUCCESS or FAIL popup
- **What you asked for.** "When I click Authenticate I need to see a success popup as an alert so I know it's good, or a Fail — you fat fingered it — if the authentication fails."
- **Now** both Authenticate buttons (Start here step 1 and the Tenant details form, which funnel into the same check) end with a popup: *SUCCESS — authenticated to <tenant>*, with how many dictionaries, engines and web DLP rules the app can now see and a reminder that nothing was changed; or *FAIL — you fat-fingered it (or the tenant said no)*, with the cause, the fix and the tenant's own words. The card underneath still shows the same result. Only a click reaches the popup — never a page load or a profile load (`static/guide.js`, `_gdAuthPopup`).

### Channel selection: one disabled rule per engine on every channel, Endpoint on removable media
- **What you asked for.** The old Fed pack's deploy ended with `[policy] Old Fed Policy Pack Endpoint DLP Monitor: failed — Exceeded maximum number of DLP engines: 4`: with Endpoint DLP ticked and no endpoint rule in the pack, the channel selection built one endpoint rule carrying all 17 engines, and ZIA allows at most four. "The endpoint DLP should deploy 1 engine per rule and use the removable media channel and be disabled" — and "ensure this same methodology is used in every pack and also for Email, SaaS as well."
- **Now** the rules the channel selection adds for a channel the pack has no rule on are one per engine on every channel — *US PII [Endpoint]*, *US PII [SaaS]*, *US PII [Email]*, *Private Keys [Endpoint]*, and so on, `<prefix><engine> [<Channel>]`, fitted to the SaaS 31-character limit and unique within the pack — each DISABLED, monitor-only (ALLOW, or report-incident on SaaS), INFO severity; Endpoint rules on the removable-media channel (`REMOVABLE_DRIVE_TRANSFER`). That is the shape every shipped pack already has on all four channels (one rule per engine, equal counts; `tests/test_pack_conventions.py`), so an uploaded pack now deploys the same way (`pack_channels.extension_policies`, `ENDPOINT_MAX_ENGINES`).
- A packed endpoint rule with more than four engines is now set aside at preflight with the reason (*carries 5 engines; ZIA allows at most 4 on an Endpoint DLP rule … split it one engine per rule*) instead of failing at the tenant, and the strict ZIA test fake refuses it the way the tenant does.
- Verified: `tests/test_channel_targets_undo.py` — six engines become six one-engine rules on Endpoint, SaaS and Email alike, disabled, Endpoint on removable media; SaaS names stay within 31 characters and unique; a five-engine packed rule is refused before anything is written; Terraform hand-off carries the same rules. `tests/test_zia_contract.py` carries the refusal. `GETTING_STARTED.md` §5d says so.

### Update app can follow `dev` or `qa`, not only `main`
- **What you asked for.** "I want the app to have options to update to specific branches and make that selectable for now" — so a fix can be tried from an install before it is promoted through `dev → qa → main`.
- **Now** the **Update app** button on the Start here card has a **Branch** dropdown — *Stable (main)*, *QA (DLP-CHECK-QA)*, *Development (dev)*, the installed one marked — that re-checks GitHub for the chosen branch as you pick it and is posted with the update. `UPDATE_Zscaler_Made_Easy.bat dev` and `python app_update.py --branch dev` do the same from a prompt; `--status` reports which branch updates come from.
- **What it does underneath** (`app_update.py`): the names are a strict allowlist (`BRANCHES`) validated before anything reaches git — the API answers 400 to anything else, the CLI refuses it.
