An update switches to a local branch, creates a tracking branch for one only GitHub has, and refuses one GitHub does not have before anything moves; the pull is still fast-forward only, hand edits are still set aside and re-applied, and the GitHub REST cross-check now compares against the same branch. With no branch named, an update stays on the branch the install is on — it never silently moves an install back to `main`. `/api/health` reports the branch and GitHub's tip of *that* branch, so "update available" is honest on `dev` too, and the Advanced settings page's Update button follows the same rule.
- `SECURITY.md` (*Supported versions*), `README.md` (*Branching and releases*) and `INSTALL.md` say so, including that `qa`/`dev` carry work that has not been through QA.
- Verified: `tests/test_app_update.py` — switching to a `dev` branch one commit ahead, staying on it when no branch is named, switching back; the allowlist refused by `valid_branch`, `update`, the CLI and both endpoints; `/api/health` and `/api/app/update/status` reporting the branch and the choices; the card's markup and the posted body. Full suite green. Also run live from a checkout against the real repository: `/api/health` and `/api/app/update/status` answered for all three branches, an unknown branch got 400, and in a headless browser the dropdown rendered the three choices, picking *Development* re-checked `dev` and the Update button posted `{"branch":"dev"}` (the update itself intercepted, nothing moved). The *Update app* entry in the in-app help (*Every function*) describes the dropdown.

### A pack dictionary named like a predefined dictionary heals itself when the pack loads
- **What you asked for.** "So can you make the fix for me" — auto-heal in the app, chosen over hand-editing `old_fed_policy_pack`, after its deploy still failed on `Duplicate DLP dictionary names are not allowed. [Private Key].` and the `Private Keys` engine and every rule behind it were skipped.
- **Now**, the moment any pack is loaded — built-in or uploaded, for the plan, the deploy, a removal, the journey check and the Terraform hand-off alike — a dictionary named like one of the names the Admin Portal shows Zscaler's predefined dictionaries under (`Private Key` for `CRED_LEAKAGE`, `IBAN`, `Credit Cards`, `Medical Information`, …; `dlp_importer.PREDEFINED_DISPLAY_NAMES`) is renamed `Z-<name>`, and every `D:<name>` reference in the pack's engines follows (`dlp_importer.heal_predefined_names`, applied by the loaders next to `ship_monitor`). The plan warns *dictionary 'Private Key' will be deployed as 'Z-Private Key'*, the deploy log says the same, and the pack file is never modified. So `old_fed_policy_pack` deploys as it is next time: the dictionary is created, the engine is built on it, the rules follow.
- A display name the list does not know is still reported as a conflict by the plan, exactly as before — the tenant-driven check stays as the backstop. The built-in packs carry no such name; a test keeps it that way and keeps the strict test tenant's display names in step with the list, so the two cannot drift apart.
- Verified: `tests/test_run_control.py` deploys a verbatim copy of the failing upload against the strict tenant (which refuses duplicates exactly as ZIA does) and checks the rename, the plan warning, the log line, the created dictionary and engine, and that the file on disk is untouched; `tests/test_pack_conventions.py` covers the helper itself (whole-name rewrite that leaves `Private Keys-Plaintext Regex` alone, idempotence, case-insensitivity, inputs untouched) and that all three loaders agree; `tests/test_zia_contract.py` keeps the display-name list and the strict tenant in step. Full suite green; the committed Terraform is unchanged, since no shipped pack is renamed.

### The Fed pack's private-key dictionary is now `Z-Private keys`
- **What you asked for.** "In the old and new fed pack there is a dictionary called private keys, I want you to change the name of it to Z-Private keys" — after a deploy log still showed `[dictionary] Private Key: failed — Duplicate DLP dictionary names are not allowed. [Private Key].` and, behind it, `[engine] Private Keys: skipped — dependency failed`.
- **Now** the Fed pack's clone of ZIA's predefined `CRED_LEAKAGE` dictionary is `Z-Private keys` (it was `Fed Pack Private Key` since the previous entry), in both places it exists in this repository: the pack source (`fed_policy_pack/configs/dlp_dictionaries.json`, with the `Private Keys` engine's expression updated to match) and the generated Terraform hand-off (`terraform/fed_policy_pack/`, regenerated). The `Z-` prefix matches the pack's own rule names (`Z-Private Keys-Monitor`).
- **The "old" Fed pack is not in this repository.** The deploy log that prompted this names the pack `old_fed_policy_pack` — an upload in the local pack library (`user_packs/`, git-ignored, never committed), not the built-in `fed_policy_pack`. It still carries the original, un-renamed `Private Key` dictionary, which is exactly why it fails. It has to be fixed where it lives: open it in Advanced settings → pack library and rename that dictionary the same way, or deploy the built-in `fed_policy_pack` instead.

### The Fed pack's private-key dictionary is now unmistakably named
- **What you asked for.** "In the fed pack (both versions) rename the private key dictionary to Fed Pack Private Key" — sent alongside a real tenant error, `Duplicate DLP dictionary names are not allowed. [Private Key]`, from ZIA refusing a dictionary named the same as its own predefined `CRED_LEAKAGE` dictionary (shown in the Admin Portal as "Private Key").
- **Now** the Fed pack's clone of that predefined dictionary is `Fed Pack Private Key`, in both places it exists: the pack source (`fed_policy_pack/configs/dlp_dictionaries.json`, with the `Private Keys` engine's expression updated to match) and the generated Terraform hand-off (`terraform/fed_policy_pack/`, regenerated with `python pack_terraform.py --out terraform`). It was already `Private Key - Medium Confidence` — disambiguated once before, for the same reason — but that name is still close enough to the taken one to invite exactly this confusion; the new name isn't.

### Deployed rules say DISABLED in their own description, not just the plan
- **What you asked for.** "In the deploy policy pack all deployed rules are set to disabled and the description should say that as well," then "in the descriptions don't say deployed by Zscaler Made Easy."
- Every rule Zscaler Made Easy deploys already shipped `DISABLED` at the point the payload is built, whatever the pack says. What wasn't true: the rule's own `description` field in the tenant didn't say so, and the plan-preview card said "monitor-only" but not "DISABLED".
- **`pack_deploy.py`**: `baseline_description()` and `pack_description()` now append "Deployed DISABLED; enable once reviewed." to every rule's description (dictionaries and engines have no disabled state, so they're untouched), idempotently. The plan-preview's "monitor-only" impact card says DISABLED too now.
- Then dropped the plain "Deployed by Zscaler Made Easy" attribution sentence from both helpers' provenance tags — the tag itself (`[DLP-Check baseline ...]`/`[DLP-Check pack ...]`) is what removal matches on and stays; `baseline_description()` keeps its substantive "measured on every journey check, never overwritten" claim, `pack_description()` now leaves just the bracketed tag.
- Verified: `tests/test_baselines.py` and `tests/test_engine_pruning.py` assert the actual deployed rule's `state` and `description`, not just the plan text; a new unit test on both helpers directly (rule vs. dictionary/engine inclusion, idempotent re-tagging); `tests/test_pack_removal.py`'s fixtures updated for consistency. Full suite green.

### CISA Secure by Design gap-close: a real vulnerability disclosure policy, and two more startup fail-closed checks
- **What you asked for.** "Implement the CISA Secure by Design gap-close on Zscaler Made Easy" — a CISA-complete vulnerability disclosure policy, tighter `disclosure`/`cves` scoring, a written record of which vulnerability classes are designed out, a fail-closed startup check for a non-loopback bind, a cross-channel integrity check on the app updater, hash-verified CI tests, and a one-line public-repository note — without signing the CISA Pledge, adding WebAuthn, removing CSP's `unsafe-inline`, becoming a CNA, or touching packs/Terraform.
- **`SECURITY.md` rewritten as a real VDP.** Authorizes good-faith testing of *this product* only (never a customer's Zscaler tenant), a safe-harbor commitment for testers who stay in scope, the existing private reporting channel (3-business-day acknowledgement, 30-day fix window for credential/tenant/unapproved-write issues) kept as-is, a coordinated-disclosure window (90 days, or 7 days after the fix reaches `main`, whichever is first), and a commitment to GitHub Security Advisories with a CWE id on every High/Critical one. The existing defaults table and Known limitations section are kept and extended, not replaced.
- **`secure_by_design.py`.** The `disclosure` control now passes only when SECURITY.md carries all four of: a reporting channel, the authorization language, safe harbor, and the disclosure window — `fail`, not silently `pass`, if any is missing. `cves` now passes only when both GitHub Security Advisories and CWE are promised. A new `classes_doc` control reads `docs/VULNERABILITY_CLASSES.md` (new) and fails if it does not cover memory-safety, SQL, XSS/CSP, and the hash-pinned/pip-audit/Trivy supply chain.
- **`docs/VULNERABILITY_CLASSES.md` (new).** Class by class: memory-unsafe bugs, SQL injection and command injection (designed out — no C extension code, no SQL, no `shell=True`), XSS (mitigated by escaping at every render site, with the inline-CSP limitation named rather than hidden), SSRF, supply-chain compromise, path traversal and denial of service.
- **A server bound beyond loopback now refuses to start** unless SSO is fully configured, or the operator sets `ZSCALER_ALLOW_NONLOCAL_BIND=YES_I_UNDERSTAND` because they already have their own authenticating reverse proxy in front of it (`security.require_safe_bind()`, called at startup next to the existing SSO fail-closed check). Binding beyond loopback with the UI token gate itself off (`ZSCALER_UI_AUTH=0`) is refused the same way. `SECURITY.md` and `INSTALL.md` both now say plainly that the UI token (and an SSO sign-in) is not multi-factor authentication by itself.
- **`app_update.py` cross-checks GitHub.** When `origin` is a github.com remote, the commit just pulled by `git` is compared against what GitHub's REST API reports for the same branch — a channel separate from the git pull itself. A mismatch rolls the pull back with `git reset --hard` and refuses the update rather than running an unverified tree. This does not add commit/tag signature verification (`main` carries no signed tags); that limitation is now written down in `SECURITY.md`, not left implicit.
- **CI's `test` job now installs from `requirements.lock`, hash-verified** (`pip install --require-hashes --no-deps -r requirements.lock`, same as the installer/updater/container) instead of a loose `requirements.txt` resolution, then adds `pytest`/`httpx` on top. The `supply-chain` job's `pip-audit` and the `container` job's Trivy scan are untouched.
- **`LICENSE` and `README.md`** each gained one line: being public so the code can be reviewed and vulnerabilities disclosed responsibly does not change the license terms — still not for redistribution, sublicensing or third-party use.
- Verified: `tests/test_secure_by_design.py` (the new `classes_doc` control, the stricter `disclosure`/`cves` scoring, both the pass and fail paths), `tests/test_secure_defaults.py` (`require_safe_bind`'s loopback/override/SSO/UI-auth-off branches, the doc/LICENSE/README content, the server wiring), `tests/test_app_update.py` (the GitHub cross-check matching, mismatching-with-rollback, and skipped-when-not-github.com paths). Full suite green (one pre-existing, unrelated failure in this sandbox — `test_legacy_api.py`'s `jwcrypto` import — reproduces identically on `dev` before this change and is fixed by installing from `requirements.lock`, which now happens in CI's `test` job too).

### Optional SSO: an OpenID Connect login in front of the UI token
- **What you asked for.** "Make sso integration optional for now" — a real opt-in config flag and the supporting code path, off by default.
- **`sso.py` (new).** `ZSCALER_SSO_ENABLED=1` requires a sign-in at any standards-compliant OpenID Connect identity provider (Okta, Entra ID/Azure AD, Google Workspace, Auth0, Keycloak, ...) — Authorization Code flow with PKCE, state and nonce checked, the ID token's signature verified against the provider's own published keys (not just decoded), issuer and audience checked — before the same UI token cookie every request is already gated on gets issued. This isn't a second, parallel security mechanism: `security.ui_request_ok()` is unchanged; SSO only changes how that one cookie is minted. Off by default, nothing about the app changes until you set it.
- **Fails closed, on purpose.** Turn it on without every `ZSCALER_SSO_ISSUER` / `_CLIENT_ID` / `_CLIENT_SECRET` / `_REDIRECT_URL` set (or with a non-HTTPS issuer that isn't loopback) and the server refuses to start, rather than silently leaving the static-token shortcuts (`?token=...`, the paste-in unlock box) as the only real gate while claiming SSO is required. Once it's on, both of those shortcuts stop working — the cookie is only ever minted by a completed login.
- **Where it fits.** This is for the container/server deployment, run by more than one person behind a shared URL — not the single-operator desktop launcher, which doesn't need it. The Advanced settings page's CISA Secure by Design "Multi-factor authentication" control, previously marked not-applicable for a single-operator tool, now passes once SSO is configured (enforcing a second factor is then the identity provider's own policy).
- Verified: `tests/test_sso.py` — off by default; fails closed when misconfigured; a real signed-JWT round trip against a fake identity provider (genuine RSA keypair, JWKS, discovery document); every attack tried and refused (unknown state, a token signed by the wrong key, wrong audience, wrong issuer, mismatched nonce, replaying a consumed state); the static-token shortcuts refused once SSO is on; the IdP's `error`/`error_description` are HTML-escaped, not reflected raw. Full suite green.

### A dev → qa → main branch structure
- **What you asked for.** "Structure the repo to make initial changes to the dev branch then promote to qa then to main."
- **Now** there are three long-lived branches, promoted in one direction only: `dev` (where new work lands and is merged by pull request), `qa` (`DLP-CHECK-QA` on GitHub — a fast-forward of `dev`'s tip once it's ready for end-to-end testing), and `main` (a fast-forward of `qa` once QA signs off, what customers run). `dev` was created fresh from `main`'s current tip; `qa` was already there and brought level with `main` first (all 12 changes it was missing, from the Path Analyzer through the install guide). See the new "Branching and releases" section in `README.md`.
- All three branches are identical right now (`main` = `qa` = `dev`), so this is a pure structural change: nothing about the app itself moved.

### Score Distribution radar: never clips, and an exploded full-size view
- **What you asked for.** "Score distribution image needs to be able to show an exploded view or be centered."
- **Root cause.** The radar's canvas had a fixed size (`viewBox="0 0 220 190"`); a section name long enough, or a set with enough sections, ran past the edge of that box on whichever side had the longest label, and SVG silently clips anything drawn outside its own viewBox — so the chart looked lopsided or cut off depending on how many sections an assessment scored and how long their names were.
- **Fix.** The viewBox is now computed from the actual label set every time it draws, sized just wide and tall enough to hold the longest label on every side — so the circle is always centred and nothing is ever clipped, regardless of how many sections there are or how long their names run (real assessments commonly score 12 sections). A new expand button (⤢) on the Score Distribution tile opens a full-size view: untruncated section names, the percentage at each point, and a legend table pairing every section with its score. Closes on Escape, the backdrop, or the Close button, and returns keyboard focus to the expand button.
- Verified: `tests/test_dashboard.py` (the viewBox is computed, not a fixed constant; the expand button, modal, close paths and legend are wired; the exploded view uses the same theme colours as the tile rather than SVG's black default). Browser run against a fake 6-section assessment: zero clipped labels and symmetric margins on the tile, full labels and a correctly coloured chart in the exploded view, Escape closes it and returns focus, no console errors.

### Channel Selection page, and FP/TP Detection Ratio checks the receiver behind each delivery path
- **Channel Selection (new).** The sidebar gets a **Channels** page, matched to the reference screens you sent: 11 Zscaler data-security capabilities (Inline Web DLP, Endpoint DLP, Email DLP, SaaS Security/CASB, GenAI Security, Shadow IT Discovery, Endpoint Data Scan, SSL/TLS Inspection, Cloud App Control, Device Control, Workflow Automation), each with its risk level, category and a plain description. For each one, mark it **Zscaler**-owned or covered by an **Alternative** (with a note on which vendor); tick "Select all"; add a custom channel Zscaler Made Easy doesn't list. Three live tallies — Owned Channels, Alternatives, Coverage Gaps — and a sticky footer ("N of 11 channels addressed · M coverage gaps remaining") with *Continue to Assessment*, which jumps straight to Connect to the tenant. This is a real input, not decoration: there's no Zscaler API that reports what a customer already owns, so the choice is kept in this browser (and persists across reloads) the same way the theme preference does.
- **What it isn't yet:** the reference screens' *Gap Analysis*, *Assessment* and *Report* as separate pages, and the sidebar's *Data Identifiers / App Control / File Type Control / API Calls / Config Compare* sub-views, are a materially bigger, separate change to the app's information architecture — not done in this pass. Say if you want those scoped next.
- **FP/TP Detection Ratio now checks the receiver, not just the rule's switch.** Zscaler has three configuration points for incident delivery, and the report reads all three instead of assuming a rule's switch alone means delivery works: the rule's own "Send to Incident Receiver" setting (cloud-to-cloud, or an on-premises Incident Receiver over ICAP), **and** whether an actual Incident Receiver object or ICAP server object is configured and enabled behind it. A rule can have cloud-to-cloud turned on with no receiver ever created, or every receiver disabled — that still shows as "configured" today but delivers nothing; the report now catches it, names it "Configured but not delivering," and adds a high-severity finding independent of whether old incidents happen to exist.
- Verified: `tests/test_dashboard.py` (the Channels page: 11 real channels, persistence, the guide panel and results footer hidden while it's open, styling); `tests/test_detection_health.py` (the rule-switch vs. receiver-object cross-check for both cloud-to-cloud and ICAP, a disabled receiver still counted as missing, the finding's severity). Browser runs against fake tenants for both.

### Live DLP Feed: transactions landing live, with success/error and the reason
- **What you asked for.** "I want the ability to view the traffic as it traverses all policies in Zscaler in real time — vital to troubleshoot, must be interactive, and provide success or error rates and reasoning." You confirmed the API client has Insights access.
- **What Zscaler's API actually exposes, checked before building anything.** There is no pull API for the full ZIA transaction log (every URL Filtering, Cloud App Control and Firewall & DNS Control decision) — that data is Web Insights / Cloud NSS, a *push* feed to your own log store (Splunk, S3, a syslog receiver) that this tool cannot read. What the API does expose live is Workflow Automation's own DLP incident search, the same one the FP/TP Detection Ratio report already reads. **Find the gaps → Live DLP Feed** (`/report/live`) is built on that, honestly, with the gap stated on the page itself, not hidden.
- **What it does.** Start the feed and it polls every 15 seconds: the first poll looks back two minutes (never dumps history), every poll after only asks for what landed since the last one. Each row shows **who** was transacting, **what triggered** (the rule and dictionaries, with match counts), **where the data was headed** (URL host, application, or the recipients for mail), the channel, and — read from the rule's actual configured action, never guessed — whether it was **enforced** (blocked/confirmed) or only **monitored**. A running tally across the session: seen, enforced, monitor-only, still open, and the **FP:TP ratio** among the ones already verdicted. Stop any time; Reset session clears the tally and the rows.
- **Interactive and live**, per the ask: no page reload, a growing table with a soft highlight on new rows (skipped under `prefers-reduced-motion`), an accessible live region announcing new arrivals in a batch instead of one-by-one.
- Verified: `tests/test_detection_health.py` (the windowed fetch behind the poll, the moving `since` and the clamp on a stale one, the rule-action lookup used instead of guessing, the session tally rolling up across polls, the endpoint end to end, the page wiring). Browser run against a fake tenant that adds a fresh incident mid-session: the first poll shows the seeded incidents with correct enforced/monitor tags, the second real poll (15 s later) picks up the new one and flashes it in, Stop/Reset/Back all work, no console errors.
- Not built: the wider ZIA transaction log (URL Filtering/Cloud App Control/Firewall decisions with no DLP match). That needs your tenant's Cloud NSS pointed at a log store this app can read — a separate, larger integration; ask if you want it scoped.

### Path Analyzer: what to do about it, reasoned across every DLP channel
- **What you asked for.** "Take into account app profile bypasses heavily, and what should you choose to mitigate the risk — this accounts for Email, Endpoint DLP, SaaS CASB DLP and inline."
- **Now** every Path Analyzer result carries a **What to do about it, across every channel** section (`path_analyzer.recommend_mitigations`): a ranked, reasoned list, not a checklist. Whenever the network path itself sees nothing — bypassed by the app profile, or left encrypted by an SSL exemption — Endpoint DLP is weighed first, because it is the one control that runs on the device regardless of where the traffic goes: if it is off, that is the highest-severity fix; if it is already on, the page says so instead of suggesting nothing. When the path is decrypted and inspected but only monitor-only, the recommendation is to promote the exact rule that matched, by name. When it is decrypted with no rule at all, the fix is to deploy one. Email DLP and SaaS Security API are recommended only when the destination is actually shaped like webmail (its ZIA category) or a sanctioned cloud application (ZIA's own lookup) — never as a blanket suggestion for an arbitrary site. A path that is already blocked, or already covered end to end, says plainly that there is nothing to mitigate.
- Verified: `tests/test_path_analyzer.py` (the ranked list for a bypassed, blind, monitor-only, enforced and blocked destination; the webmail and sanctioned-app cases; the page wiring and stylesheet). Browser run against a fake tenant confirms the section renders correctly for a webmail destination (Endpoint informational + Email DLP medium) and a blocked one (nothing to mitigate).

### FP/TP Detection Ratio (was Detection Health): who is transacting, what triggered, where it was going, and the telemetry destinations to exclude
- **What you asked for.** The Detection Health page should analyse who is transacting, the triggers, and where the data was intended to be sent, so that publicly posted telemetry sites and URLs that never carry sensitive data can be excluded automatically; and the page should be the FP/TP detection ratio.
- **Now** the page is **FP/TP Detection Ratio** (same route, `/report/health`, same API). It opens with the ratio: every incident an analyst closed counts once, a resolution of *false positive / business justified / not an incident / not a violation / benign* is a false positive, any other closed incident a true positive, open incidents wait; shown as FP:TP in lowest terms (for example 6:1) with the share of verdicts that were not real and a one-line reading of what it means.
- **Every incident now carries who, what and where**: the user (as Workflow Automation records them, with department), the trigger (rule and the dictionaries with their match counts), the action (upload, post, send), the file, and the destination (the URL's host, the application, or for mail the recipients' domain). Three new sections: *Where it was going* (per destination: incidents, users, real, not real, actions, rules), *Who is transacting* (per user: incidents, real, not real, open, top destinations, rules) and *Latest transactions* (when, who, trigger, sent to, channel and action, verdict).
- **The exclusion algorithm.** A destination is marked **Exclude** when it has at least 3 incidents, none closed as real, and either its host or path matches a telemetry / public-content pattern (telemetry, metrics, analytics, beacon, crash and diagnostics collectors, Sentry / Datadog / New Relic / Segment / Mixpanel and the like, tag managers and ad beacons, update / OCSP / CRL services, `/v1/track`-style paths) or 80% or more of its closed incidents were not real. Each one becomes a finding and an **Exclude destination** proposal in the approval queue naming the rules it hits and the fix (a URL category or domain exclusion in the ZIA portal); nothing is written by itself, a person approves. The thresholds and the pattern list are settings (`dest_min_incidents`, `dest_fp_pct`, `telemetry_patterns`).
- The Markdown export carries the ratio and the two new tables. The report keeps user identities as the incidents carry them; it lives in the server's memory and is never written to disk.
- Verified: `tests/test_detection_health.py` (the new fields, the telemetry match, the exclusion decision, the users' and destinations' tables, the ratio, the proposal, the export). Not verified here: a real tenant's Workflow Automation feed.

### Path Analyzer: where a user's traffic to one destination goes, live, and what blocks or impacts it
- **What you asked for.** A built-in analyzer that takes an IP address or a URL and shows the path traffic takes through the tenant as if a user were browsing there, live, and clearly shows what blocks or impacts it.
- **Now** *Find the gaps → Path Analyzer* (`/report/path`) takes what a user would type (a full URL, a hostname or an IP address; the page says in words when the input is none of those), the network the device is on and the platform. The first run reads the tenant as it is right now (Client Connector app and forwarding profiles, trusted networks, PAC files, then SSL inspection, URL filtering, Cloud App Control, File Type Control, the DLP rules on every channel and the URL categories) with each step shown live, an elapsed clock and a Stop button; later runs reuse that read and only look the destination up, so they take seconds; *Fresh read* forces a new read. ZIA's own URL lookup (`POST /urlLookup`) places the destination in its categories and cloud application, and a security alert on the destination is reported first.
- **The answer is one line**, for example "Blocked by URL filtering rule 'Block gambling'.", "Never reaches Zscaler: forwarding profile sends mail.example.com direct.", "Reaches mail.example.com encrypted: Zscaler sees the hostname, never the content.", "Reaches chat.openai.com decrypted; 2 DLP rule(s) inspect the content and can block it." Under it: where it stops, which object decides that, whether it reaches Zscaler, is decrypted and content-inspected, the ZIA categories; then **What blocks this traffic** and **What impacts it on the way** (each with the rule or profile by name, the severity and what to do), the path hop by hop with the rule that decided each hop, the other channels for the same data, the assumptions (any user, group and location; browser traffic over HTTPS from a managed device; the configuration now, not a log) and a JSON export.
- **How it signs in.** Opened from the assessment page, the tab uses that page's credentials when you click Analyze; opened on its own, it offers the saved tenant profiles or what the server has in its environment / `tenant_config.json`. Read-only throughout.
- **API:** `POST /api/path-analyzer/start` (a job followed at `GET /api/dlp-packs/jobs/{id}`, stoppable at `/cancel`) and `POST /api/path-analyzer` (waited, for scripts); the body is the usual credentials plus `destination`, `network` (off / on / vpn), `platform` and an optional `report_id` of an earlier read to reuse. Module `path_analyzer.py` on top of the Data Flow Map's tracer.
- Verified: `tests/test_path_analyzer.py` (parsing, the lookup and its refusals, the verdicts for a blocked, blind, enforced, bypassed, security-alerted and IP destination, reuse of a read across destinations and networks, Stop, the job end to end, the page wiring); a browser run of the page against a fake tenant (the live log, three verdicts, a bad input, Fresh read and Stop). Not verified here: a real tenant (no credentials in this environment).

They are self-contained: nothing is fetched from the network, and their language choice is kept in the browser only.
