### Removal only through the rule picker, with a usage check right before anything goes
- **The whole-pack removal buttons are gone** from Deploy Policy Packs (*Remove from tenant* on each pack card, *Remove all…* in the options). The one way to remove is **Remove rules…**: every DLP rule in the tenant on every channel, ticked by you (the filters select a pack's rules or every Zscaler Made Easy rule in one click). The API's whole-pack plans stay for scripts; *Undo this deployment* stays for a deployment recorded by this app.
- **The check before anything goes.** The preview reads every rule on every channel and every engine: an engine is removed only when no rule outside the selection uses it, a dictionary only when no engine that stays uses it, predefined Zscaler objects never. The approve step lists the exact rules, engines and dictionaries with why each one goes, a consent box, and a final confirmation that names the rules. Then, **right before the engine tier and again before the dictionary tier, the tenant is read again**: anything a rule or a surviving engine now uses (added since the preview, by anyone) is left alone and named in the log and the result ("checked again just before removal: still used by rule 'X' (Endpoint DLP); left alone").
- **No inline flow diagram** on the deploy review, the deploy result or the removal preview: those screens show the lists only. The dependency diagram lives on the *How a deployment works* page.
- Verified: tests for the live re-check (a rule added after the preview keeps its engine and dictionary), the plan's keep rules, and the page (no whole-pack buttons, no inline diagram, the rules named in the confirmation); the whole suite passes.

## 2026-09-15

### The Terraform hand-off no longer looks stuck, and every tenant read says what it is reading
- **What was wrong.** "The download and hand-over for Terraform is taking too long." Building the workspace takes milliseconds; in gap mode the server first reads the whole tenant (dictionaries, engines, the rules on every channel, SaaS tenants, templates), a minute or two of API calls behind one silent request with a button that said *Generating…*.
- **Now** the hand-off runs as a background job like a deploy (`POST /api/dlp-packs/terraform/start`, `GET /api/dlp-packs/jobs/{id}`, the zip from `GET /api/dlp-packs/jobs/{id}/download`; the waiting endpoint stays for scripts). The hand-off page shows what is happening as it happens ("Reading the tenant: Endpoint DLP rules… 14 found", "Plan: 12 reuse, 5 create", "Generating the Terraform workspace for Fed Policy Pack…"), an elapsed clock and a Stop button, then a card with what was downloaded and the gap it covers; gap mode says up front that it reads the tenant first.
- **Every tenant read is logged step by step** (`fetch_tenant`, `fetch_channel_state`): a deploy's log now opens with "Checking the approved plan against the tenant as it is now…" and the reads, instead of showing nothing until Phase 1, and the read-back at the end shows its reads too.
- Verified: `tests/test_terraform_job.py` (the read lines, their place in a deploy's log, the job end to end in static and gap mode with the zip content, the page wiring); browser run of the hand-off page against a slow fake tenant.

### Tenant configuration: the classic ZIA API sign-in beside the ZIdentity API client
- **What was missing.** Every sign-in went through OneAPI: a ZIdentity API client (client ID, client secret, vanity domain). A tenant that has not enabled ZIdentity had no way in.
- **Now** Tenant Configuration has a *Sign-in method*: **OneAPI** (as before, the default) or **Classic ZIA API** (admin username, password and the tenant's API key from Administration → API Key Management, signed in at `zsapi.<cloud>.net` with the obfuscated-key session the classic API expects). The same nine credential fields travel in every request (`tenant_auth.py`), so the connection test, the assessment, Deploy Policy Packs, removals, Detection Health, the Terraform hand-off, the continuous evaluation, the MCP server and the command line all take either sign-in. Saved profiles keep the method and the username and remember the password and API key the way they remember a client secret (the OS credential store, else the owner-only file; never returned by the API, masked in every error, redacted in the audit log). The environment takes `ZSCALER_AUTH_METHOD=legacy` with `ZSCALER_USERNAME`, `ZSCALER_PASSWORD`, `ZSCALER_API_KEY`; `tenant_config.json` the same keys.
- **What the classic API cannot do is said, not hidden.** It covers ZIA only: the connection test says so, the assessment reports ZPA, Client Connector and ZDX checks as not available with a plain reason, and the cloud must be the tenant's actual cloud (Commercial means zscaler.net). Connection failures are explained for this sign-in ("Zscaler rejected the admin username, password or API key…", the API key's location, the host to allow).
- Under the hood the policy packs use the Zscaler SDK's legacy ZIA client; the Endpoint and Email DLP services the SDK only ships for OneAPI are built on the same session, so every channel deploys the same way.
- **Found on the way: a remembered secret was never used from the browser.** The page and Deploy Policy Packs looked for the selected profile at `window.GD`, but the guide kept it in a top-level `const`, which is not a window property; so every request went out without the profile id and a saved profile with *remember* ticked still asked for the secret. The guide now publishes it, and a profile's remembered secret (or password and API key) signs in with the form's secret fields blank, as always intended.
- Verified: `tests/test_legacy_api.py` (the credential rules, the host per cloud, the API key obfuscation matched against the SDK's own, the classic session in the assessment client, profiles with two remembered secrets, the server's resolution from form / profile / environment, the SDK client construction, the connection test and assessment endpoints, the page wiring). Not verified here: a real tenant on the classic API (no credentials in this environment).

### A deploy never looks hung, and a Fed pack deploy no longer drops 51 rules or fails on "Private Key"
- **What was wrong (from a real deploy log).** (1) *"[channels] Fed Policy Pack: Endpoint DLP, SaaS Security API, Email DLP NOT deployed: unticked in the pack card (51 rule(s) skipped)"*: the Deploy Studio remembered a channel choice in the browser across sessions, and a choice made long ago (Web only) silently left three channels out. (2) *"[dictionary] Private Key: failed — Duplicate DLP dictionary names are not allowed"*: the tenant shows Zscaler's predefined CRED_LEAKAGE dictionary under the display name "Private Key", ZIA refuses a second dictionary with that name, and the plan did not know; the Private Keys engine and its four rules were skipped behind it. (3) A deploy or removal ran as one silent HTTP request: the browser showed a spinner and nothing else for minutes. Underneath, the Zscaler SDK was built with no request timeout at all and slept for as long as ZIA's Retry-After said (up to an hour when the hourly request limit is reached), and it waited twice per write for the portal's edit lock when someone was signed in to the ZIA Admin Portal — 76 objects × 15 seconds of waiting, all of it invisible.
- **Channels.** The channel choice now lives for the page only: every time Deploy Policy Packs opens, every channel the pack ships rules for is ticked, and the old remembered choice is cleared. Unticking is a per-deploy decision, and the approve step now carries a warning line naming the pack, the unticked channels and the number of rules that would be left out, before the approval box.
- **Private Key.** The Fed pack's copy of the credential-leakage dictionary is now *Private Key - Medium Confidence* (the engine follows; Terraform regenerated). The plan matches names the way ZIA compares them (case and spacing ignored) and marks a pack dictionary that carries a predefined display name as a conflict with the fix in words. If ZIA still refuses a create as a duplicate at write time, the existing object is looked up by name and reused, so what depends on it is built instead of skipped. The strict test tenant now refuses duplicates exactly as ZIA does, display names included.
- **Live progress.** A deploy and a removal run as background jobs (`pack_jobs.py`; `POST /api/dlp-packs/apply/start`, `POST /api/dlp-packs/removal/apply/start`, `GET /api/dlp-packs/jobs/{id}`, `POST …/cancel`). The Deploy step shows the tenant's answer to every write as it arrives, the step it is at ("Step 1 of 5 — dictionaries", "activating in ZIA and waiting for ACTIVE"), an elapsed clock and a **Stop** button; the removal wizard the same. Stop sends nothing more after the write in flight; what was written is activated, read back and recorded for undo. The waiting endpoints stay for scripts.
- **Bounded waits and a plain reason.** The SDK client is built with a 90-second request timeout and never sleeps more than 30 seconds on a rate-limit answer. In the shared execution path, the first refusal no later write can get past — the portal's edit lock, the exhausted hourly limit, refused credentials, a client without the DLP write permission — stops the run at once: every later write is reported as *skipped — not attempted*, the log carries one `STOPPED — nothing more is written. …` line, and the result screen says why in words ("Someone is signed in to the ZIA Admin Portal for this tenant and holds its edit lock … Sign out of the portal, or wait for the session to time out, then run this again"; "ZIA's request limit for this hour is reached; it accepts requests again in about 57 minutes"). A locked tenant is known after one write instead of twenty minutes.
- Verified: 16 new tests (`tests/test_run_control.py`) cover the stop rules, the cancel, a locked-tenant deploy and removal, the duplicate reuse, the display-name conflict, the jobs module, the job endpoints end to end and the SDK client's caps; the whole suite passes; a browser run against a slow fake tenant showed the live log, the elapsed clock, the warning gate for an unticked channel, a Stop mid-deploy, a deploy stopped by the edit lock and a removal with the live log. Not verified here: a real tenant (no credentials in this environment).

### Every Zscaler cloud, including zscalerten.net and the government clouds, from one table
- **What was wrong.** The cloud picker offered six commercial clouds and no way to choose zscalerten.net, zscalergov.net or zscalergov.us, while its help text told government customers to "use gov" with no such option. Worse, the assessment engine built the API address from the cloud name (`api.zscloud.zsapi.net`, `api.zscalerthree.zsapi.net`), which does not exist for commercial clouds, and always signed in at zslogin.net, which the FedRAMP clouds do not use; the SDK wrapper passed the cloud name straight through with the same effect. Only Deploy Policy Packs mapped beta and gov correctly.
- **Now** `zscaler_clouds.py` is the one table: every cloud a tenant can live on (zscaler.net, zscalerone.net, zscalertwo.net, zscalerthree.net, zscloud.net, zscalerten.net, zscalerbeta.net, zscalergov.net, zscalergov.us), its admin host and its OneAPI environment. Every commercial cloud signs in at `<vanity>.zslogin.net` and calls `api.zsapi.net`; beta uses the beta hosts; zscalergov.net and zscalergov.us use their own FedRAMP identity provider and gateway (`zidentitygov.net` / `api.zscalergov.net`, `zidentitygov.us` / `api.zscalergov.us`). The GUI picker lists them by admin URL, the server validator accepts every spelling people use (`gov`, `govus`, `zscalerten.net`, `admin.zscalergov.net`, upper case) and names the accepted values otherwise, and the assessment engine, the SDK wrapper, the MCP server and the discovery tool all resolve hosts through the table. Saved profiles with the old values keep working.
- Tests cover every cloud's login and gateway from each entry point and that the picker offers them all.
- **CI's lock check no longer fails on other people's release days.** It compiled `requirements.txt` from scratch and compared, so any dependency publishing a new version (tzdata, pyjwt, urllib3, uvicorn in the last three days) turned every pull request red. It now compiles on top of the committed lock, which fails only when `requirements.txt` changed without a re-lock; new versions come in through `make lock`, and known vulnerabilities in the pinned set are still caught by the pip-audit step. The lock is refreshed to today's versions.

## 2026-09-13

### Refactor pass and two packaging targets from one tree
- **Static cleanup, no behaviour change.** Every finding of a whole-tree lint run is fixed: 60 unused imports and variables removed, 24 f-strings without placeholders made plain strings, three copies of the same helper folded into one (`_nx` now lives in `dlp_importer`, `_field` in `pack_channels`, with the old names kept for callers), the legacy pack page's remaining dead code gone. The seven `import x  # noqa` lines that probe whether an optional module can load are deliberate and stay.
- **Entry points fail in words, not tracebacks.** The web server checks its address and port before starting: a taken port, an address the machine does not own or a privileged port is reported in one sentence with what to do, exit code 2 (uvicorn used to print a bare errno and exit 3). `ZSCALER_PORT` outside 1–65535 falls back to 8000 with a warning. The command-line assessment reports a missing, unreadable, malformed or incomplete configuration file in one sentence (exit 2) instead of a traceback or, worse, silently falling into the interactive prompt; an unwritable report path is reported the same way.
- **Documentation.** Docstrings on every public function and class of the server, the security helpers, the channel helpers and all 170 registered checks (each says its id, section and name); README rewritten to describe what the project does, how it is structured, every configuration variable, and how to install, run and test both delivery modes.
- **Local installer for Linux and macOS** (`install.sh`, `start.sh`, `stop.sh`, `local_settings.env.example`) beside the Windows `.bat` files: a private `.venv`, the same hash-pinned `requirements.lock` the container installs, an editable install that provides the `dlp-check`, `dlp-check-assess` and `dlp-check-evaluate` commands (`pyproject.toml`). A `Makefile` gives one set of targets for both modes (`install`, `dev`, `run`, `test`, `check`, `docker`, `docker-run`, `lock`).
- **Container unchanged in substance**, now provably built from the same source: `tests/test_packaging.py` checks that the installer and the Dockerfile install from the same pinned file and start the same entry point, that every module is listed for the package, that the dependency lists agree, that the shell scripts parse, and that secrets and state files never reach the image or the repository. `tests/test_entrypoints.py` covers the new error paths.

## 2026-09-12

### One road into the tenant and one road out, for every pack
- **What was wrong.** Packs reached the tenant by two different roads. Deploy Policy Packs planned, validated, reused by name, mirrored every channel, tagged every object and recorded the deployment; the pack page's *Import to Tenant* button ran an older importer (`dlp_importer.run_full_import`) with none of that: web rules only, no plan to approve, no provenance tag, its own verification that could disagree with the plan ("Verification found issues" next to "all 76 objects present"). Deletes had two executors as well: the removal wizard's (tiers, activation between tiers, retries, read-back) and an older `undo` that deleted in one pass with no activation. Direct SDK calls were spread over four modules, each shaping errors its own way, and nothing retried a rate limit during a deploy.
- **Now: `pack_ops.py` is the shared execution path** (docs/SHARED_EXECUTION_PATH.md). It is the only module that names an SDK write method. One validator for every kind of object runs before any request; dependencies are created first (`CREATE_ORDER`) and removed last (`DELETE_TIERS`); every write goes through one call that turns every SDK answer into the same shape, retries rate limits, busy tenants and timeouts with backoff, and never lets one object's failure stop a run; every write leaves one structured event (`events` in every result, and the `dlp_check.packs` log) with the object, the outcome, ZIA's message, its code and HTTP status and the number of attempts. Deploy Policy Packs, the removal wizard, *Undo*, the pack page and the continuous-evaluation proposals all use it. The old importer and the old undo executor are gone; the Fed pack's original stand-alone importer stays in its folder as the reference the path is measured against, marked as never run by the app.
- **Failures say what and why.** Every refusal a real tenant has given carries a plain-language explanation next to ZIA's own words, in the log (`What this means: …`), in the deploy and removal screens and in the events: what the message means and what to do about it.
- **Verification has one source of truth.** After a deploy, the same planner reads the tenant back; the per-pack verification is derived from that read, so it can never contradict the plan. *Check what is in the tenant* on the pack page answers from the same planner and says what cannot be created on this tenant and why, separately from what is missing.
- **Deploying twice is safe and says so.** A second run finds everything by name, creates nothing and stops with "Nothing to deploy: every policy pack object already exists". A test deploys every shipped pack twice on the strict tenant and checks that no channel gains a second rule of one name; another deploys every pack and undoes each deployment, and the tenant must be back to exactly what it held before.
- **The pack page deploys through the wizard.** *Deploy this pack* opens Deploy Policy Packs with the pack selected (plan, review, approve, deploy, verify); the single-pack `/import` endpoint returns the plan first and deploys only with that plan's hash, records the deployment for undo, and refuses to write in read-only mode.
- **How a deployment works** (`/report/deploy-flow`, linked from Deploy Policy Packs): a step-by-step, keyboard-navigable walk-through of the deploy flow and the removal flow for someone who has never seen the code: what happens at each step, what can stop it, and what you see on screen.
- **CISA Secure by Design, applied to what is deployed.** Every journey check (*Where is this tenant?* and the assessment's journey) now reports, per pack, deviations from the three principles with what to do: objects missing or blocked and rules never enabled (ownership of outcomes), objects changed in the tenant without the pack recording it (transparency), rules weakened against the pack and enforcing rules that no longer match it (secure defaults). The Advanced settings score gains two controls: that deployed packs are measured against the principles, and that one execution path exists.
- **Guards.** `tests/test_shared_path.py` fails the build if any module outside `pack_ops.py` and its channel adapter calls an SDK write method, or if anything imports the Fed reference script; it also locks in validation-before-write, transient retry, plain explanations, structured events, idempotency and full teardown for every shipped pack, and the endpoints. Two banking dictionaries whose names carried commas were renamed to the character set every proven name uses.

### What the CJIS deploy taught: five more things ZIA refuses, now stated in every pack and enforced before any write
A real tenant refused the CJIS pack five ways. Every shipped pack now states each of these the way ZIA wants (CJIS 1.2, Fed 2.1, Global Banking 1.1, Healthcare 3.3, Enterprise 1.1, Source Code 4.16), the deploy corrects an uploaded pack that does not, preflight refuses what cannot be corrected with the tenant's own words, and the test tenant every pack is deployed on in CI refuses all five exactly as the real one did.
- **"Confidence score value is not supported in custom dictionary"** (14 CJIS dictionaries). A custom dictionary has no confidence score in ZIA; the deploy no longer sends one, and the CJIS pack no longer carries them. The plan compares custom dictionaries without it, so a tenant copy is not reported as "differs" over a field ZIA never stores.
- **"Dlp dictionary's confidenceThreshold can't be null"** (the CJIS SSN clone). A clone of a predefined dictionary must carry a confidence score; a clone that names none deploys with `CONFIDENCE_LEVEL_HIGH`, and the pack validator says so.
- **"Atleast 1 hierarchical identifier should be selected for hierarchical dictionaries"** (the CJIS driver's-licence clone). A clone of a hierarchical template (driver's licence, credentials) now defaults to the full identifier set the Fed pack proved; a clone of a hierarchical template the app has no list for (EU IBAN, EU passport, adult content) is blocked in preflight with the reason instead of failing at the tenant.
- **"Request body is invalid." on webDlpRules** (every CJIS, Banking and Healthcare web rule, and four Fed rules). Those rules named `NATIVE_FTP_RULE`, a URL Filtering protocol ZIA does not accept on a DLP rule. Every web rule now uses the set the Fed pack's other thirteen rules were created with on the same tenant (`WEBSOCKETSSL_RULE`, `WEBSOCKET_RULE`, `FTP_RULE`, `HTTPS_RULE`, `HTTP_RULE`); any other protocol is refused in preflight.
- **"Channel is mandatory" on endPointDlpRules** (every Endpoint mirror in every pack). An Endpoint DLP rule applies to exactly one transfer method and there is no "any": the zscaler/zia provider accepts `REMOVABLE_DRIVE_TRANSFER`, `NETWORK_DRIVE_TRANSFER`, `APPLICATION_FILE_ACCESS` and `PRINTING` only. Every pack's Endpoint mirror now watches removable media (USB), the channel selection's Endpoint extension rule does the same instead of the unaccepted "ANY", an endpoint rule that names no method deploys as removable media, and the Terraform states it. Copy a rule with another method to watch network shares, printing or application file access.
- Terraform regenerated for every pack. Documentation in POLICY_PACKS.md corrected (protocols, transfer methods).

### Every pack has the Fed pack's logic, and the tests say so
- The Fed pack is the one deployed in production, so its rule shape is now the only shape: every web rule in every pack carries the Fed protocol set, **admin rank 0** (the Fed's production rules do; the SaaS refusal above named rank 7), monitor action, INFO severity, DISABLED, one engine and nothing else (the Source Code pack's cloud-application scope is the one addition, created on the same tenant). The OCR flag that only the unproven rules carried is gone. Endpoint, SaaS and Email mirrors come from one generator and are the same shape in every pack; the channel-selection extension rules match. A test compares every pack against the Fed pack's own rules and fails on any drift.
- **Every naming and description limit the provider validates is now enforced before a write**, with the tenant's words: dictionary name and description 255, pattern 256 bytes and 8 per dictionary, phrase 3 to 128 bytes and 256 per dictionary, engine name characters and description 255, SaaS rule name 31, Web and Email rule description 10240, admin rank 0 to 7, the Web protocol set, one of four endpoint transfer methods. The test tenant refuses each the same way.
- **Smoke-checked in a browser**: all six packs selected in Deploy Policy Packs, preflight, approve, deploy (461 objects, 0 failed, every channel the same count), verified by a fresh read, then Remove all (561 objects, verified clean), with no page error; every toolbar button opened; the API smoke and the full suite (319 tests) pass.

### And three more from the Source Code pack on the same tenant
- **"Name exceeds the max length 31 characters" on casbDlpRules.** The SaaS Security API caps a rule name at 31; Web, Endpoint and Email took longer names on the same tenant. Every pack's `[SaaS]` mirror now fits (the name is shortened at a word boundary, the `[SaaS]` suffix stays, and names stay unique in the pack: `SRC-JavaScript Code to [SaaS]`, `Z-CJIS Markings Medium [SaaS]`, `HC-US PHI High [SaaS]`), the channel selection's SaaS extension rule fits too, and preflight refuses a longer SaaS name with the reason.
- **"Rule with rank 7 is not allowed at order 12" on casbDlpRules.** After four Source Code SaaS rules were created at the next free order, ZIA refused the next free order for every rule after them, and the single retry one order further on was refused the same way. A refused order is now answered by re-reading the type's rules from the tenant and trying the orders ZIA can still accept, from the last one down (a bounded number of tries), and the log says which orders were refused and where the rule landed. The rules of the next pack start from where the last one was accepted. The test tenant keeps a customer-made SaaS rule last, as the real one appears to, and every pack's SaaS mirrors are still all created around it.
- **406 "Request Rejected" on dlpDictionaries for `SRC-HTML Signal`.** Not the API but its edge: a request body containing `<script`, `<html`, `<meta` and the like is rejected before the API sees it (thirty other dictionaries in the same run went through). The seven tag-opening patterns are now written with the hex escape (`\x3cscript\b`), the same regex to PCRE and to the local evaluator; a pack that still carries a literal tag opening is refused in preflight with the escaped form to use.

### Removal waits for ZIA and tries again, so engines and dictionaries go too
- **What happened on a real tenant**: the rules on Web, Endpoint and Email were deleted, some SaaS deletes were refused, and every engine and dictionary delete was refused, because ZIA still counted the engines as in use by rules that were only pending-deleted (the activation had not completed) and answered other deletes with transient errors while it was busy.
- **Now**: after each tier (rules, then engines, then dictionaries) the removal activates and **waits for ZIA to report ACTIVE** before starting the next tier, instead of activating and carrying straight on. A delete refused for a reason that time or an activation clears (still in use or referenced, activation in progress, edit lock, rate limit, a SaaS delete missing its rule type) is tried again after the tenant settles, up to three passes per tier, with a fresh rule type for SaaS rules; anything else fails once and stays failed. The log says which deletes will be retried, when it is waiting, and the activation status it saw.
- **Leftovers with no rule can be picked.** *Pick rules…* now also lists the orphans: engines no rule on any channel uses and custom dictionaries no engine uses, each marked Zscaler Made Easy or "not created by Zscaler Made Easy". A picked orphan is planned and removed like anything else, with the dictionaries only it uses; a picked id that is gone is reported. Removing one pack now also catches Zscaler Made Easy's own historical names (an object left under a name a pack used to use), flagged as a name-only match to confirm.

## 2026-09-11

### A channel left out of a deploy is never silent
- **Why the Fed pack's Endpoint and Email rules did not deploy, with no line in the log.** Deploy Policy Packs remembers each pack's channel choice in the browser. A choice remembered from before a pack gained its Endpoint, SaaS and Email mirrors kept only Inline Web, and the deploy dropped the mirrors before the log, so nothing said so. A remembered choice is now trusted only while the pack still ships rules on the same channels it did when the choice was made; a pack that gained mirrors since goes back to the default, every packed channel.
- **And when a packed channel really is unticked, it says so three times**: a warning on the pack card ("Endpoint DLP is unticked: the 17 rules this pack ships there will NOT be deployed. Tick the channel to deploy them."), a warning in the plan, and a `[channels]` line at the top of the deploy log with the rule count.

### Verified end to end: every button in a browser, every pack against a tenant that refuses what ZIA refuses
- **The test tenant now enforces the ZIA API contract** (`tests/test_zia_contract.py`): every rule a real tenant has answered with a 400, plus the documented limits. Dictionaries: description at most 255, proximity only on the any-pattern-with-any-phrase type (which needs a pattern and a phrase), at most 8 patterns of 256 bytes without lookaround, at most 256 phrases of 3 to 128 bytes, a clone needs a predefined template this tenant has, unique names. Engines: the allowed name characters, description at most 255, every referenced dictionary id must exist, match counts under 1000. Rules: unique names per channel, engines that exist, the channel's action set, state, severity, rank, a type and a tenant for SaaS rules, a tenant for email rules. Every shipped pack is deployed on it in CI (nothing may fail, be blocked or skipped, and every channel must receive the same number of rules) and then removed with the tenant read back empty.
- **What that found and fixed**: the Fed pack's clones carried `dict_template_id` values from the tenant they were exported from; on any other tenant those ids are not the predefined dictionaries, so six clones and the five engines behind them would have failed. A clone's template is now resolved by name in the tenant being deployed to, first; an id from a pack is only a fallback and is refused with the reason when it is not a predefined dictionary there. The shipped packs carry template names only.
- **Every toolbar button, in a real browser.** All fourteen tools open and render without a script error, the assessment runs to results with the plain-English block on every finding, the results, compliance and remediation views open, and the Word export returns a document. Found and fixed: guided mode (the default) hid the whole Reference tool group, so Advanced settings, Best Practices, Zscaler Updates and the Evaluate All Rules reference were invisible until the mode was switched off. Every tool now stays in the toolbar in both modes; guided mode only folds the Compliance Coverage card.

### CISA Secure by Design score in Advanced settings
- **The app measures itself, live.** A new section in the Advanced settings tab scores this install against CISA's Secure by Design principles (take ownership of customer security outcomes; radical transparency and accountability; lead from the top) and the seven pledge goals (MFA, default passwords, reducing classes of vulnerability, security patches, vulnerability disclosure, CVEs, evidence of intrusions). Each control is read from the running server or the app folder (UI token gate, write mode, bind host, debug, the security middleware, TLS verification, the outbound allowlist, dangerous constructs in the code, SECURITY.md's disclosure policy and known limitations, the audit log, build identity, the changelog, hash-pinned dependencies, the CI scans and SBOM, Dependabot, the tests, container hardening, secrets kept out of the repository, memory-safe language) and says what was found and what to do when it falls short. MFA is shown as not applicable to a local single-operator tool and not scored. `GET /api/secure-by-design`; a Re-check button re-measures.

### The Fed pack's coverage, wherever that data must be protected too
The Fed pack's production-proven engines now sit in every pack whose customers hold that data. Enterprise and banking already carried US PII, Financial Information and Private Keys (their ZBP- engines); this batch closes the remaining gaps, one web rule per engine mirrored onto Endpoint, SaaS and Email, every rule DISABLED and monitor-only.
- **CJIS 1.1**: `CJIS US PII` (identity data of subjects, victims, witnesses and officers that state breach law reaches outside any CJI marking; reuses the pack's own CJIS Prox 500 clones and adds CJIS- copies of the rest) and `CJIS TLP Red`, `CJIS TLP Amber Strict`, `CJIS TLP Amber` (the Traffic Light Protocol markings law-enforcement information sharing runs under). 14 rules per channel.
- **Source Code to AI 4.15**: `SRC-Private Keys` (plaintext, JWK, PGP and Bouncy Castle keys and Zscaler's credential-leakage clone, the secrets that travel with code), scoped to the same AI/ML applications as the language rules. Rebuilding the pack from the tenant's own dictionaries now keeps any non-language engine and the dictionaries only it uses instead of dropping them.
- **Healthcare 3.2**: Engine 10, `HIPAA Financial Information` (payment cards and magnetic tracks, ABA routing numbers, W-2 / 1099 / K-1 / 1040 forms and Zscaler's financial classifiers), the PCI DSS scope a provider or payer carries on claims, billing and payroll. 12 rules per channel.
- Terraform regenerated for the three packs; the pack notes and PHI_HIPAA_BUILD.md name where each engine came from.

### Removal preview: "nothing to remove" now says what it read
- **A partial read is never "nothing".** When the removal preview could not list a whole kind of object (dictionaries, engines, a channel's rules), it says so in the preview, and when nothing matched at all it blocks with the reason ("Nothing matched, but the tenant could not be fully read: dictionaries: …") instead of reporting an empty plan.
- **The "Nothing to remove" card shows what the preview saw**: how many dictionaries (custom, Zscaler Made Easy-tagged), engines and rules per channel were read, and what could not be listed, so an empty result can be traced to its cause in one look.
- **A listing that omits the custom flag no longer hides the app's own dictionaries**: a dictionary carrying the Zscaler Made Easy tag whose name is not one of Zscaler's localised predefined names is treated as ours. A dictionary the tenant flags as predefined is still never touched.

### Engines the tenant refused: names and description length
- **Names.** A real tenant answered `INVALID_NAME: DLP Engine name can only have alphanumeric, underscore, hyphen, dot, paranthesis and space` for `HIPAA Narrative / Low Volume`, `HIPAA Pharmacy / Claims / Billing` and `HIPAA Critical (Strong Signature + Clinical Document)`. They are now `HIPAA Narrative - Low Volume`, `HIPAA Pharmacy - Claims - Billing` and `HIPAA Critical (Strong Signature and Clinical Document)`; the CJIS pack's three engines and six dictionaries with a slash are renamed the same way. Preflight now refuses an engine name outside that character set with the reason, before any write, and a convention test holds every shipped pack to it.
- **Descriptions.** The same tenant answered `DLP engine description can not be more than 255 characters` for every other engine: the packs' engine descriptions are documentation and run long. The deploy now clamps what it sends to 255 bytes (the provenance tag is kept whole, the text is shortened with an ellipsis), as it already did for dictionaries; the pack files keep the full text. The fake tenant in the tests refuses both, exactly as the real one does.

### Healthcare pack 3.1: the Fed pack's PII and PHI coverage
- **US PII.** The Fed pack's US PII build joins the healthcare pack under an `HC-` prefix so the two packs can share a tenant: twelve dictionaries (Social Security Numbers, Tax Identification Number and Drivers License clones, each at high confidence and at 500-byte proximity; Alien Registration Number, Date of Birth and US Passport as custom pairs) and a `HIPAA US PII` engine (the Fed expression plus passports) behind `HC-US PII-Monitor`. It catches the identity data a covered entity holds on patients, members and staff that state breach law reaches even when no diagnosis travels with it.
- **US PHI, two confidences.** The Fed pack's `US PHI - Medium Confidence` and `US PHI - High Confidence` engines come across as Engines 8 and 9 (`HIPAA US PHI - Medium Confidence`, `HIPAA US PHI - High Confidence`) with their own rules: an independent PHI opinion built on Zscaler's Medical Document, Medical Information, Drugs and Diseases dictionaries beside the customer's Engine 1, for the tuning phase to compare.
- Eleven inline rules now, each mirrored onto Endpoint, SaaS and Email (44 rules, equal on every channel), all DISABLED and monitor-only. Terraform regenerated; PHI_HIPAA_BUILD.md carries the new engines, rules and dictionaries.
- **A clone whose template the tenant lacks no longer blocks its engine.** The deploy planner treats a pack clone with no predefined template on this tenant like a missing predefined dictionary: the clone is skipped, the engine is created without that term, and the plan names it. A tenant that exposes only the SSN dictionary still gets all eleven healthcare engines and every rule.

### Healthcare and CJIS dictionaries the tenant refused
- **Proximity needs the right match type.** A real tenant answered `400 Proximity can be enabled only for 'Match any Patterns and any Phrases' or 'Match Near or Not Near Phrases' match types` for the four healthcare identifier dictionaries (MRN, date of birth, member id, address) that carried a 50-byte window on the *Match All* type, which blocked all 8 engines and every rule behind them. They now use `MATCH_CUSTOM_ANY_PATTERN_WITH_ANY_PHRASE` (a number counts when a label sits within the window, which is what the design meant). The CJIS pack had the same defect on eleven dictionaries: the five with patterns and phrases move to that type, and the six phrase-only ones drop a window that could not apply. Clones keep their template's proximity as before.
- **It cannot reach a tenant again.** Preflight and pack validation name the problem before any write (`proximity 50 needs match type MATCH_CUSTOM_ANY_PATTERN_WITH_ANY_PHRASE…`), a convention test checks every shipped dictionary, the fake tenant in the tests refuses the combination exactly as the real one does, and a native Zscaler export uploaded with the combination is corrected on transcription with the change named in the pack's warnings. POLICY_PACKS.md documents the rule.

### Every finding in four plain sentences, the same in every output
- **What we found, what it means for the business, what to do, once fixed.** Every check result now carries the same four sentences (`plain_english.py`), written for a first-week engineer to read to a customer: what the tenant does today, why it matters to the business, the next step with the portal path, and the business outcome once it is fixed (or, for a configured control, what it gives the business). The outcome comes from one table per area of the product, so the same control reads the same everywhere: the results tab (the technical detail folds under *Technical detail*), the JSON the assessment saves, the Word report (critical findings, the review table's *Once fixed* line, and every detailed finding) and the digest, where each mapped article names the outcome once acted on.

### Every pack monitors, and says so
- **No rule in any pack is named or targeted as blocking.** The healthcare tiers (`HC-HIPAA Critical-Monitor`, `Identity Trifecta-Monitor`, `Sensitive Subcategory-Monitor`, `Bulk PHI Major-Monitor`, `Bulk PHI Significant-Monitor`), the banking bulk rules, the Fed CUI marked-documents and weak-markings rules and the 13 source-code rules are renamed `-Monitor`; the shipped packs carry no `target_action`, so the plan shows no *target BLOCK* chip. Enforcing a rule is the customer's decision in the portal once incidents have been reviewed; the journey records it as progress. `ship_monitor` still turns an uploaded pack's BLOCK / CONFIRM into monitor with the designed action kept aside.
- **Why can't I deploy?** The plan and the approval step list every gate a deployment needs, each with its state and what opens it: the plan itself (blocked reasons named), something to create, write mode (with the Enable button or the start script to use), and the approval box.
- **Healthcare (and any pack naming a predefined dictionary with parentheses) deploys on tenants that lack it.** When a tenant did not expose "Names (US)", "Driver's License (United States)", "Tax Identification Number (US)" or an NDC dictionary, the missing name was reported cut at the parenthesis, the pruning step could not find it, the engine was blocked and every rule behind it was skipped: the healthcare pack created its 7 dictionaries and nothing else. Missing names are now read whole, the engine is created without those terms (the plan says which), and all 8 rules land on every channel. A regression test deploys the pack against a tenant with only the SSN dictionary.
- **The app works at whatever address you open it.** The CSRF check now also trusts the page the server itself served (a same-origin request), so a spare port, the machine's name or a container port mapping no longer answers every write with "Origin not allowed"; a page on another origin, another local port included, is still refused.

### Continuous evaluation, phases 4 and 5: guidance keeps itself current, and the loop runs without you
- **Every article is mapped, not just listed** (`guidance.py`). A new library article is matched to topics (Email DLP, SaaS Security API, Workflow Automation, NSS/SIEM, Client Connector, Endpoint DLP, SSL, GenAI, identity, DLP engines, containment, automation, and the packs' verticals), and each topic names the checks, the packs and the DLP channels it can affect. A mapped article becomes an *Update check* proposal (or *Extend coverage* when it announces a new capability) in the app-wide queue with the article, source and tier as evidence; an unmapped one is recorded as no action, so nothing is raised twice. The first run baselines the existing library. Approving a guidance proposal records the article as a reference for its checks in `checks_config.json`, the versioned file that ships with the app. `GET /api/guidance/sources`.
- **Partner sources, Proofpoint first.** The source registry gains Proofpoint (help centre and release notes), Google Cloud and Workspace docs, ServiceNow docs, Atlassian support (Jira, Bitbucket) and SentinelOne (login-walled: Zscaler's guide is the signal), each with the topics its articles can change; CrowdStrike, Okta and Ping carry theirs too. **Stale sources are flagged, never silently kept**: a watched source that has not answered for seven days shows as stale in the Digest tab and the digest.
- **The SaaS tenant capability matrix is data** (`saas_apps.json`, `saas_matrix.py`): application, SaaS type and capabilities (DLP, malware, App Governance, SSPM, Workflow Automation), refreshed from Zscaler's two help pages on the schedule (a refreshed copy goes to `saas_apps.local.json`). The loop compares it with the customer's onboarded tenants: tenants that are not ACTIVE raise *Re-authorise tenant* proposals (resolved when they recover), DLP-capable applications not onboarded and applications at the 16-tenant limit are listed, and a pack whose SaaS or Email mirrors have no tenant of that type is named. `GET /api/saas-apps`.
- **Always on** (`continuous_eval.py`). Every six hours while the app runs, and from `RUN_Continuous_Evaluation.bat` (Task Scheduler) or `python continuous_eval.py --once` (cron, container) without the browser: for every tenant profile that remembers its secret the loop runs Detection Health and refreshes its proposals, reads the SaaS tenants, measures the journey against the profile's packs and compares it with the last record (drift), and writes one digest (JSON and Markdown, kept under `digests/`). The 6-hour Detection Health refresh is now this loop.
- **It tells you.** A digest that has something to decide (new proposals, new guidance, a stale source, a tenant that lost its authorisation, a pack that moved, an error) is sent by e-mail (`DLP_CHECK_SMTP_*`, `DLP_CHECK_DIGEST_TO`) and/or to a Teams or Slack incoming webhook (`DLP_CHECK_DIGEST_WEBHOOK`, https only); `DLP_CHECK_NOTIFY` = noteworthy (default), always or never. Secrets never appear in a digest. The new **Digest** tab (`/report/digest`, *Find the gaps*) shows the latest digest, runs the loop now, and lists the watched sources, the SaaS matrix, the schedule and the notification settings. `GET/POST /api/digests`.

### Start here and Deploy Studio
- **Every step is labelled "Step N of 7"** in words before its title, and the number stays visible on narrow screens.
- **The step strip at the top is buttons, not labels.** ① Configure opens the tenant details, ② Select Scope jumps to the scope choices, ③ Run Assessment runs it (results open in their tab), ④ Review + Export jumps to the results or, when there are none yet, says so and hands over to Run. It still lights up as the assessment moves through the steps.
- **Removing a pack prompts for write mode.** The removal review now carries an *Enable write mode* button beside *Remove now* and says why the button is locked; once write mode is on, the review refreshes in place.

### One rule per engine, in every pack
- **Every Web DLP rule carries exactly one engine, every engine has a rule, and every channel carries the same rules.** The source-code pack's single rule with 13 engines is now 13 rules, one per language (`SRC-<Language> Code to AI-Block`), mirrored onto Endpoint, SaaS and Email (13 x 4). The multi-engine monitor rules in the Fed pack (FOUO, TLP, US PHI, CUI-Monitor-All), Global Banking (Customer PII, Customer Records, MNPI) and the Enterprise pack (TLP, US PHI) are split one rule per engine, named after the engine (`Z-TLP Red-Monitor`, `Bank-KYC and AML Files-Monitor`, `CUI-Monitor-Contextual`, and so on); the CJIS pack gains the rule its `CJIS Markings Medium Confidence` engine lacked. Fed 17 x 4, Banking 13 x 4, Enterprise 9 x 4, CJIS 10 x 4, Healthcare 8 x 4, Source code 13 x 4. The source-code generator (`tools/pack_from_pattern_doc.py`) emits one rule per engine and rebuilds the mirrors; a test enforces the convention on every pack; Terraform regenerated.

### Continuous evaluation, phase 3: approved proposals are applied from the app
- **Preview, apply, read back, roll back.** An approved proposal in Detection Health (promote a monitor rule, retire a dead rule, forward silent rules to the Incident Receiver) gets a *Preview and apply to the tenant* button. The app re-reads every rule the proposal touches and shows exactly what would change; if the tenant moved since the evidence was gathered (the field no longer holds the value it had) the change is refused with the reason. After an explicit confirmation the rule is updated through the same channel services as a deployment, read back, and only counted when the tenant holds the new value. The value before the change is recorded on the proposal, so *Roll back* replays the same steps in reverse. Write mode is required; ZIA keeps the change pending until an admin activates. Every step goes to audit.log and the proposal's history (`proposal_apply.py`; `POST /api/proposals/{tenant}/{id}/preview|apply|rollback`). Dictionary and engine tuning proposals stay manual (made in the portal) and say so.

### Log Analyzer (new)
- **Drop in Client Connector logs or a PAC file, read what happened in plain English.** A new *Troubleshoot › Log Analyzer* tab (`/report/logs`, `POST /api/logs/analyze`) takes the ZIP that Client Connector's *Export Logs* produces (or single `.log` files, `.tar`/`.tgz`/`.gz` bundles, or a PAC file), analyses it in memory on the local server and answers with a one-line verdict, the facts (client version, OS, user, cloud, tunnel version and last state, period covered), findings ordered by how much they hurt the user (each with *What happened*, *What it means for the user*, numbered *What to do next*, where it was seen, and redacted evidence lines), cross-finding context (errors behind a captive portal are a symptom, not a fault; a certificate rejection explains the sign-in failure that follows), and the most repeated unrecognised error lines for a senior engineer. Twenty-four situations are recognised: captive portal, trusted network, third-party VPN, sign-in failure, PAC download failure, certificate / TLS rejection, DNS failure, tunnel down, Z-Tunnel 2.0 fallback, gateway or service edge unreachable, proxy authentication (407), ZPA failure, posture failure, network changes and sleep/resume, update failure, policy download failure, client turned off or uninstalled, driver / system extension failure, crash, strict enforcement, fail-open, fail-close, MTU, and time-outs.
- **PAC files are checked too**: not a PAC, unbalanced braces, no return, a hard-coded gateway instead of `${GATEWAY}`, a DIRECT default that bypasses Zscaler, fail-open vs fail-closed of the final proxy string, a single gateway with no failover, `isInNet(host, …)`, `myIpAddress()`, `isResolvable()`, missing private-range / plain-host bypasses, Client Connector local ports, and an inventory of every DIRECT bypass with its risk (data-loss channel, very broad, public range).
- **Safe by design**: nothing is written to disk or sent anywhere; archive caps (members, size per member, total size, one level of nesting), member names never used as paths, binary members skipped, passwords / tokens / cookies / JWTs blanked out of every evidence line; the page is keyboard-operable with a labelled file input, a live region and column-headed tables. Findings download as Markdown.

### Plain-English layer for a first-week engineer
- **Every page explains itself.** A *What is this page, and why it matters* box at the top of every report tab (open by default; a remembered *Hide these boxes* for people who no longer need it) says what the page is, why the customer cares, when to use it, what you get and what to do next.
- **What does everything do?** (`/help`, a *Start* toolbar button and the help drawer): every function of the app as one card each, in the order of a first engagement, with an eight-step first-engagement list, the glossary (now including Client Connector, Z-Tunnel, PAC file, captive portal, trusted network) and the *when something goes wrong* list. One catalogue (`static/explain.js`) feeds the page boxes, the help page and the assessment page's help drawer; a test fails if a toolbar button ships without an entry.

### Every pack ships monitor-only
- **Every rule in every pack is DISABLED and monitor-only.** The ten Web rules that shipped with BLOCK or CONFIRM (Fed CUI, Global Banking bulk rules, the Healthcare PHI Block tiers, the source-code upload rule) now ship `ALLOW`; the designed action is kept as `target_action`, and the data-governance journey names it as the hardening step once incidents are reviewed. The same convention is applied at load time to every pack, uploaded ones included (`pack_channels.ship_monitor`): Deploy Policy Packs, the Terraform hand-off and the journey all see monitor-only, disabled rules; the validator warns when an upload carries an enforcing action. Terraform regenerated with the target action as a comment.

### Advanced settings: Terraform on upload
- **Every pack you add is transcribed to Terraform on the spot.** The upload saves the pack and renders its static Terraform workspace beside it (`<pack>/terraform/`); the success message reports resources, data sources and files with an *Export Terraform* button, and the library table has a *Terraform* download for every pack (`GET /api/pack-library/{id}/terraform.zip`), built-in ones included.

### Prompt feature removed
- **The Prompt sidebar, the natural-language command parser and the LLM integration are gone.** The right-hand *Prompt* panel and its edge rail, the command catalogue and dispatch map, the `/api/commands`, `/api/commands/parse`, `/api/llm/status` and `/api/llm/validate` routes, `commands.py`, `llm_parser.py` and `LLM_SETUP.md` are removed. The app makes no AI or LLM calls of any kind; every function is a button with a plain-English explanation. The page's main column now uses the full width.

### Evaluate All Rules reference
- **The reference tab shows the decision only.** The simulator is removed from *How Evaluate All Rules decides* (`/report/evalall`); it stays in the Inline DLP Rule Migration wizard, where the tenant's own rules are loaded into it.

### Engines: the Sum operator
- **Sum is supported end to end.** The Engine Builder's Sum operator is written in the API's form, `((D1.S + D2.S + …) > n)`, and every part of the app now understands it: pack expressions (`D:<name> + D:<name>`), the deploy's resolver and the pruning of missing predefined terms, the pack validator, the Terraform renderer and the local corpus test's engine evaluator (a Sum with an unsimulated dictionary is "possible"). Names with parentheses ("Names (US)") are read paren-aware, so a Sum that ends with such a name renders correctly.
- **Healthcare PHI Policy Pack 3.0, Engine 6 is a real Sum** as the build document specifies: the identifier matches of SSN, MRN, member id, NPI, TIN, driver's licence and enhanced driver's licence are totalled, in three engines at > 1, > 50 and > 500 behind the three Bulk rules (a rule has no match-count condition of its own).

### Assessment scoped to the profile's vertical
- **The tenant profile's industry decides what the app suggests.** Selecting a profile ticks the compliance frameworks for its industry (Healthcare: HIPAA, NIST CSF 2.0, SOC 2, NIST 800-53; Federal: NIST 800-53 / 800-171, CMMC L2, NIST CSF; Financial services: PCI DSS, GLBA, SOC 2, GDPR, ISO 27001; and so on) unless the profile saved its own choice, so the results page and the Regulatory & Compliance tab report deficiencies for those frameworks only. The data-governance journey (pack deficiencies) is measured against the packs ticked on the profile, or the industry's packs when none are ticked. Deploy Policy Packs lists the vertical's packs first with a *For Healthcare* chip and folds the others away behind *Show all packs*. The scope note under Tenant profiles says what was narrowed and why (`GET /api/vertical-scope`; the assessment result carries `scope`).

### Packs
- **Healthcare PHI Policy Pack 3.0: the PHI / HIPAA six-engine build.** Rebuilt from the customer's build document (predefined-first): seven custom dictionaries for the HIPAA identifiers no predefined dictionary covers (Custom-Medical-Record-Number, Custom-Date-of-Birth, Custom-Health-Plan-Member-Identifier with the Medicare MBI, Custom-Address-ZIP) plus Custom-Sensitive-Condition-Markers (SUD, mental health, HIV, genetic incl. gene names, reproductive), Custom-Insurance-Billing and Custom-Clinical-Narrative; everything else from Zscaler's predefined dictionaries, referenced by their portal display names. Six engines calibrated so one PHI record fires: HIPAA Critical (three pathways), Narrative / Low Volume, Identity Trifecta (strict and expanded branch), Sensitive Subcategory, Pharmacy / Claims / Billing, and Bulk PHI as three tiers (> 1, > 50, > 500) because the API has no Sum. Eight inline rules mirrored one-for-one onto every channel; `PHI_HIPAA_BUILD.md` carries the 18-identifier coverage map, the reporting tiers, the incident-to-breach decision path (45 CFR §§ 164.402–414, 42 CFR Part 2) and the tuning notes.
- **Predefined dictionaries resolve by display name.** A pack may reference a predefined dictionary as the portal shows it ("Medical Document") or as the API exports it (`ML_MEDICAL_DOC_LEAKAGE`): the deploy resolves both, plus the description the API carries, and Terraform data sources use the API code where one is known.
- **Healthcare PHI Policy Pack 2.0** (superseded the same day by 3.0). The pack now carries the healthcare-relevant detections from the other packs and six inline topics, each mirrored one-for-one onto Endpoint, SaaS and Email (6 × 4 rules): (1) MRN / patient account / DOB with clinical context and their bulk export; (2) PHI with clinical context at medium confidence and (3) at high confidence with Zscaler's PHI engines, shared by name with the Enterprise pack (Medical Document ML, Medical Information with drug and disease terms, beside SSN, passport, alien-number, DOB and driver-licence clones); (4) patient demographics without clinical context (US PII engine); (5) patient payment-card data, the banking pack's card logic under `HC-` names; (6) payer identifiers: new `HC-Medicare Beneficiary Identifier (MBI)`, `HC-National Provider Identifier (NPI)` and `HC-Health Plan Member ID` dictionaries behind `HC-Health Insurance Identifiers`. 24 dictionaries, 7 engines. Every rule ships DISABLED at INFO; the two Block-grade rules carry BLOCK. Terraform regenerated.

### Evaluate All Rules
- **The decision diagram has its own tab** (`/report/evalall`, toolbar tile *How Evaluate All Rules decides* under Reference, and a link from the migration wizard): a decision flow with the tie drawn as a branch (more than one matching rule with the most restrictive action → rule order breaks the tie, lowest order number wins → the winner's template, auditor, Incident Receiver and severity apply), the two lanes side by side, and the simulator.
- **Channel scope stated everywhere.** Inline Web DLP (with the switch on), Endpoint DLP and Outbound Email DLP evaluate all rules; SaaS Security API DLP rules are the exception: rule order, first match wins, per rule type, no tie-breaker. The simulator has a channel selector and, on SaaS, shows the first-match walk and the shadowing it causes.

### Pack library
- **Native Zscaler JSON uploads.** The Advanced settings upload now accepts the API's own export shapes (`dlpDictionaries`, `dlpEngines`, `webDlpRules`, `endpointDlpRules`, `casbDlpRules`, `emailDlpRules`: camelCase fields, numeric ids, `D<id>.S` expressions): ids become names, expressions become `D:<name>`, sub-rules are kept, predefined dictionaries and engines are left out (every tenant has them), tenant-specific scope (locations, groups, departments, users, SaaS and email tenant ids, auditor/ICAP) is dropped with a warning that says so. The result is an ordinary pack, and a **Download as Terraform (static)** button appears right after the upload.

### Updater
- **"Fetch from GitHub" behind TLS inspection.** git does not read `REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE`, so on a laptop where the proxy re-signs github.com the update failed at the fetch with an SSL certificate problem while the app's own HTTPS calls worked. The updater and the GitHub build check now hand git the same bundle (`GIT_SSL_CAINFO`), and an SSL, sign-in or connectivity failure says exactly what to set in `local_settings.bat`.

### Terraform hand-off
- **Static or gap, your choice.** The mode is a radio choice instead of being implied by the credentials: static sends no credentials and reads nothing (no `imports.tf`); gap needs the tenant details and is refused without them.

### Removal
- **Every removal goes through the same guided path.** The history's *Undo* and the Done step's *Undo this deployment* used to delete straight away after a browser confirm; they now open the removal wizard (Preview → Approve with consent → Remove in tiers with activation → Verify by a fresh read) on exactly what that deployment created, with a plan hash, the keep rule for engines and dictionaries something else still uses, and the deployment record marked undone from the read-back (`deployment_id` on `/api/dlp-packs/removal/preflight` and `/apply`).

### Packs
- **Equal counts on every channel, Web DLP as the cue.** Each pack now carries exactly one Endpoint, one SaaS and one Email rule per inline Web DLP rule (CJIS 9 × 4, Fed 12 × 4, Global Banking 9 × 4, Enterprise 6 × 4, Healthcare 1 × 4, Source Code 1 × 4). The separate per-engine `[Endpoint Removable Media]` rules are gone: the Endpoint mirror carries no `data_transfer_method`, so it already covers removable media (USB) along with every other transfer method, and incidents name the engine either way. `tools/fan_out_channels.py` regenerates the mirrors and reports UNEQUAL when a pack drifts; Terraform regenerated.

### Deploy
- **Every SaaS mirror of a pack is created.** SaaS Security API rules are ordered per rule type and the API does not shuffle the others down for a new order 1 the way Web DLP does, so a pack with nine `[SaaS]` mirrors landed one and the rest were refused. Each new SaaS rule now takes the next free order of its type (an order conflict is retried once), and the Done step shows, per channel, how many rules were created, reused, skipped or failed and the first errors (`channels` in the apply result).

### Continuous evaluation, phase 2: proposals and the approval queue
- Detection Health findings become **typed proposals** with evidence, expected effect and rollback: promote a monitor rule (confirm phrase required), retire a dead rule, tune a noisy dictionary (exclude the application behind the noise or raise the match count), forward the matches of rules that reach no Incident Receiver. Ids are stable, so the next evaluation refreshes an open proposal instead of duplicating it.
- The **queue** lives in `proposals.json` next to the app (never committed) and shows at the top of the Detection Health tab: approve, snooze (14 days) or dismiss (remembered 30 days) each one, with your name for the audit log; a proposal whose signal disappears resolves itself. `GET /api/proposals?tenant=`, `POST /api/proposals/{tenant}/{id}/decide`, Markdown/JSON export. The 6-hour refresh feeds the queue for every profile that remembers its secret. Nothing is written to the tenant by a decision; approved proposals wait for the apply step (phase 3).

### Page layout
- **Quick Start Assessment** is a button at the top of the page (toolbar, group *Start*) that opens the four steps and the credential instructions in their own tab (`/quickstart`); the card at the bottom of the page is gone.
- **Tenant details** (organisation, assessor, cloud, vanity domain, API client, scope, tenant profiles) are an expandable section inside step 1, *Connect to the tenant*, of the Start-here panel. The form is the same one (same fields, same ids); it opens by itself until the four connection fields are filled in.
- **Regulatory & Compliance Alignment opens in its own tab** (`/report/compliance`): framework cards, the control-by-control drilldown with the findings behind each control, filters, and Markdown / CSV / JSON export. The results page keeps one chip per framework and a button to open the tab; the inline drilldown is gone.

### Evaluate All Rules
- The Inline DLP Rule Migration wizard opens with **how Evaluate All Rules decides**: a two-lane flow (Rule Order versus Evaluate All Rules) and a **simulator**. Type or load rules (Step 2's download feeds it), name the engines a transaction matches, and read the decision step by step: every enabled rule evaluated, the matching set, the most restrictive action (Block › Isolate › Confirm › Allow), **the tie on the action broken by rule order (lowest order number wins)**, whose template, auditor, Incident Receiver and severity apply, and what Rule Order would have done instead. Presets cover a tie between two Block rules, a monitor rule shadowing a block rule, and Confirm versus Allow.

### Local corpus test (DLP Efficacy)
- Besides the custom dictionaries, the corpus test now reports the **predefined dictionaries that would fire** (local approximations of the public pattern shapes, check digits validated: card numbers, SSN, ITIN, ABA routing, IBAN, SWIFT, NPI, Medicare MBI, e-mail, phone, IPv4, AWS / Google / GitHub / Slack keys, private keys, JWT, passport and driver-licence shapes, dates of birth, ICD-10, VIN, MAC, UK NINO and NHS, Canadian SIN, Australian TFN), with masked samples; machine-learning and content-based dictionaries are listed as *not simulated*, never guessed.
- **Which engines would fire**: every engine's expression (`D<id>.S > n` with AND / OR / NOT and parentheses) is evaluated on those counts — fires, does not fire, or *possible* when it hinges on a dictionary that cannot be simulated — and the Web DLP rules each firing engine drives are named.

### Detection Health
- A noisy dictionary now says **why**: whether it carries the noise itself or only co-fires with another dictionary, how thin its matches are compared with the real incidents, which applications and departments the not-real incidents come from, what is known about the pattern (nine-digit numbers, dates, names, Luhn-valid numbers, clinical or financial vocabulary …), and the dictionary's own settings (custom or predefined, confidence, phrases, patterns, proximity). Each comes with a concrete fix, in the findings, the table and the Markdown export.

### Strict Client Connector evaluation
- Seventeen new checks read the app profiles field by field (anti-tampering, every service-disable / uninstall / exit password, log level, QUIC dropped, fail-close and app fail-open, revert password, intercept on all adapters and VPN gateways, the disable window, bypass hygiene including exclusions wider than a /16), the forwarding profiles (Z-Tunnel 2.0 per network type, DTLS with TLS fallback, filter driver, IPv6, RFC1918, PAC use, trusted-network criteria), the company settings (Endpoint DLP entitlement, phased rollout, telemetry, inactive-device cleanup), the privacy settings, the device fleet (strict enforcement, anti-tampering, tunnel version, Endpoint DLP, version spread) and the **DLP notification templates** (TLS, content attachment, placeholders, plain-text body, enforcing rules that notify nobody). Each names the profile or object at fault and cites the Zscaler guidance it applies. Numeric platform codes and the `policyExtension` blob are read.

### Zscaler Update Check
- A source whose page is gone (HTTP 404, a "page not found" page, or a page with no readable text) is shown as **NO PAGE** with the reason, and its link opens the section index instead of an empty page. The Deployment & Operations root uses the right slug.

### Fixes
- **Traffic Flow map no longer fails with "Internal error".** When no Client Connector app profile matched the platform (the profile list unreadable, or the tenant's profiles carrying the numeric platform codes of the Client Connector API), the forwarding hop dereferenced a missing profile and the whole map died. The tracer now degrades hop by hop, the numeric codes (1 iOS, 2 Android, 3 Windows, 4 macOS, 5 Linux) are read, the ZPA action list of a forwarding profile no longer overwrites the ZIA tunnel mode, one destination that cannot be traced is reported in the diagnostics instead of taking the map down, and a failure names the exception and points to `server.log` (which now carries the traceback for every internal error).
- **Client Connector reads use the right endpoint.** App profiles are read from `web/policy/listByCompany` (the earlier `webPolicies/listByCompany` answered 404 on every tenant, so the ZCC findings said "not readable" while the portal was fine); the client tries each known path and caches the one that works, and a failure names the path and status it tried.
- **Update app restarts reliably.** The relaunch used `cmd /c timeout`, which refuses to run without a console, so the new server never started; it is now the silent launcher itself in batch mode (`wscript //B … /restart`), which waits for the old server to release the port, never shows a Windows Script Host dialog (problems go to `launcher.log`) and keeps the UI token so the page stays unlocked after the restart. If the relaunch cannot be scheduled the page says what to do by hand.
- **Review & Remediate works.** The recipes named ids the assessment never produces (`SSL-01`, `DLP-01` …), so no finding was ever remediable; each recipe now lists the assessment checks it answers (`4.1`, `A.2`, `E.2`, `5.3`, `A.1`, `E.1` …) and the Remediate buttons and the review list match on them.

### Packs
- Fed Policy Pack 2.0 absorbs the CUI Marking Baseline: the 28 tiered marking dictionaries and their block/caution/monitor rules now ship inside the Fed pack, enhanced with the V1.1 contextual phrases, Registry category names and declassify-date patterns as tier 7 (`CUI-Detect-Contextual`, monitor only). The V1.1 `CUI Engine (High/Medium/Low Confidence)` engines, their eight dictionaries and the orphan `AEA Medium Confidence` engine are gone. `cui_marking_baseline` is removed; a tenant that deployed it keeps its objects and the Fed pack adopts them by name.
- `tools/pack_from_design.py` can regenerate a design that lives inside a hand-authored pack (`"designs"` in `pack.json`).

### Packs
- `zbp_endpoint_dlp_baseline` removed: every pack mirrors its rules onto Endpoint DLP, so the PCI-only endpoint pack no longer adds coverage.
- Wording: everything is a **policy pack** now (the word "baseline" is gone from the UI, docs and pack names; ids, JSON keys and the provenance tag are unchanged). `Global Banking Policy Pack`, `Healthcare PHI Policy Pack`, `ZBP Enterprise Policy Pack`, `Source Code to AI Policy Pack`.
- Preflight no longer skips an engine (and every rule behind it) because a predefined dictionary the pack references is not exposed by the tenant: the term is dropped, the engine is created without it, and the plan and warnings say which names were left out (`pruned`). This is what stopped the Global Banking pack on tenants without the EU/APAC passport and IBAN predefined dictionaries.

### Updater
- A half-applied earlier update (re-applying the edits conflicted, leaving "unmerged paths") no longer blocks every later update: the conflicted files are copied to `updater_backup\<timestamp>\`, the folder is reset to the last build and the update continues; the edits from that update remain in `git stash`.
- "Could not set aside the hand-edited files: error: could not write index" is handled: a stale `.git\index.lock` (left by an interrupted git) is removed automatically before the update, a lock that is only seconds old is left alone and explained, and the error now says what to check (editor/IDE, OneDrive or antivirus holding the folder, read-only `.git`).

### Detection Health (continuous evaluation, phase 1)
- New **Detection Health** report (toolbar tile, `/report/health`, `POST /api/detection-health`): what the tenant's DLP rules actually do, measured from Workflow Automation incidents and the analysts' verdicts. Per rule, engine, dictionary, channel, application and department: incidents, closed, not real and the false-positive rate; rules classified **noisy** (≥ 40 % not real over 14 days with ≥ 10 closed), **dead** (enabled, no incident in 30 days), **ready to promote** (monitor-only, ≤ 5 % not real over 30 days with ≥ 20 closed), healthy, disabled or not in the inventory; findings worst first. "Not real" defaults to False Positive / Business Justified / Not an incident / Not a violation / Benign; every threshold can be overridden per request (`settings`).
- The report says **how incidents reach Workflow Automation**: cloud-to-cloud forwarding (rules with Zscaler Incident Receiver), an on-premises Incident Receiver over ICAP, both, or neither — and names the enabled rules that forward nowhere, which is why a tenant can have matches but no incidents.
- Every 6 hours, for tenant profiles that remember their secret, the report runs on its own and a compact entry lands in the profile history (`GET /api/detection-health/schedule` shows the state). Read-only; no user identities are kept (departments and counts only). Markdown and JSON export.
- Traffic Flow's incident hop and reference diagram name both delivery paths.

### Deploy
- **Read back after writing.** After the objects are created and ZIA is activated, the deploy waits for ZIA to report ACTIVE and runs the same preflight again: every dictionary, engine and rule it created, overwrote, modified or reused must be found. The Done step shows "Verified by a fresh read: all N objects are in the tenant" or lists exactly what a fresh read could not find (`read_check` in the API result).
- **Endpoint DLP: one removable-media rule per engine.** Every pack now ships, besides the Endpoint mirror of each inline rule, one Endpoint DLP rule per engine scoped to removable media (`REMOVABLE_DRIVE_TRANSFER`, monitor-only, disabled, named `<engine> [Endpoint Removable Media]`), so incidents on USB and other removable drives name the engine and the control can be enabled engine by engine. `tools/fan_out_channels.py` generates them; Terraform regenerated.

### Removal
- **Verified by reading.** After the deletes and the activation, the removal polls ZIA until it reports ACTIVE (up to 45 s), then runs the same discovery the preview ran against the tenant. The wizard's new **Verify** step says "nothing of the pack remains" or lists exactly what is still there, with *Verify again* (a fresh read on demand) and *Remove leftovers* (runs the removal again on exactly those objects). A removal is only called complete when the read comes back clean.
- **Remove every deployed pack at once** now means everything Zscaler Made Easy ever put in the tenant: any object carrying a Zscaler Made Easy tag (whatever pack id or version wrote it — the retired CUI Marking and Endpoint packs included), plus the names of every pack in the library and of objects earlier pack versions shipped under other names (Fed 1.1's CUI engines and dictionaries, the endpoint pack's PCI engines and rules, the pre-v4.14 source-code objects, extension rules named after the old "Baseline" pack names). Same per-object rules as a single pack: anything a customer rule or engine still uses is kept and listed.
- **Pick rules to remove**: a third way in, for leftovers no tag or name can identify. The wizard lists every DLP rule in the tenant on every channel (state, engines, whether Zscaler Made Easy created it, with a filter and select-by helpers); tick the ones to remove and the plan adds the engines and dictionaries only they use, keeping anything an unticked rule or a surviving engine still uses, then preview → approve → remove → verify as usual. `POST /api/dlp-packs/removal/inventory` lists the rules; `rules: [{channel, id}]` on the preflight/apply endpoints selects them.
- Packs without a baseline section (Fed, CJIS) now tag every object they create (`[DLP-Check pack <id> v<version>]`), so a later removal finds them by tag instead of by name alone.
- SaaS Security API rules are now actually deleted: the delete call carries the rule's `ruleType`, which the API requires; without it every SaaS row failed.
- After the final activation the removal re-reads the tenant and reports any object it still lists as **still present** (the removal is not called complete until nothing is left), instead of trusting the delete calls.

### Traffic Flow & Gaps
- No more "Could not read" boxes. Traced paths draw only the data security controls (Cloud App Control, URL Filtering, SSL, File Type Control, DLP, then logs & incidents); Browser/FTP Control, threat protection, Bandwidth Control and Firewall & DNS are not data-leak controls and are left out of the path (the reference diagram still shows ZIA's full order). A Client Connector hop the tool cannot interpret says what it assumed and why ("no app profile for macos", "no forwarding profile linked", "mode 'X' not recognised for off network").
- The path now follows ZIA's policy enforcement order for a web request (Zscaler, *Understanding Policy Enforcement*): Browser & FTP Control, Cloud App Control before URL Filtering (URL Filtering is marked not evaluated when an app-control rule matched), SSL inspection, Malware Protection / Advanced Threat Protection, upload File Type Control, upload Bandwidth Control, Web DLP, Firewall & DNS Control last, then a "Logs & incidents" hop showing which rules forward to the Zscaler Incident Receiver / ICAP and notify an auditor, with a weak point when none do.
- A reference diagram at the top of the report, "How ZIA processes a policy: device → incident", in the same style as the traced paths, with an explanation per stage through Nanolog/NSS, the Incident Receiver and Workflow Automation.

## 2026-09-10

### Security
- Web UI and API require a per-process UI token (cookie set by the launcher's URL, or `X-DLP-Check-Token` / Bearer header). `ZSCALER_UI_AUTH=0` disables it.
- Audit log (`audit.log`, owner-only) of every non-GET API call with a redacted body, plus write-mode toggles.
- MCP server binds 127.0.0.1 by default; pack upload is localhost-only; Word exports use private temp files removed after download.
- Hash-pinned `requirements.lock`; installer and updater install from it with `--require-hashes`.
- Container image (non-root, read-only filesystem, loopback publish) with `docker-compose.yml`; CI adds pip-audit, an SBOM and an image scan; Dependabot for pip, actions and Docker.
- `SECURITY.md` (disclosure policy), `LICENSE`, this changelog.

### Features
- Advanced settings tab: add your own policy packs from a JSON bundle, the three pack files, Terraform for the zscaler/zia provider or a ZIP; they are validated, kept in `user_packs/` (the `/data` volume in the container) and offered everywhere the app offers a pack. Download any pack as one JSON file.
- Detection validation harness (`tools/validate_detection.py`) and reviewer report; predefined dictionaries resolve by API or display name.
- Assessment runs checks in parallel, streams results first, reports a stuck check instead of hanging, and measures the journey afterwards.
- Partial scans: checks, report sections and plan items the API client cannot read are marked as not permitted; results say what a full scan or a configuration change needs.
- Best Practices & Benchmarks card: best-practice alignment grade per area, DLP programme stages, frameworks incl. NIST CSF 2.0 and GLBA.
- Data Flow Map: the exact path a transaction takes through Client Connector app and forwarding profiles, PAC, SSL, URL filtering, app control, file type control and DLP, per policy pack.
- Source Code to AI pack rebuilt from the v4.14 pattern library (13 languages, Anchor AND Signal); rebuild from the tenant's own dictionaries.
- Global Banking Baseline pack; Deploy Policy Packs flow diagram in its own tab; assessment results in their own tab; SSL Inspection Flow shows Client Connector bypasses.
- Every report section opens by default; the compliance card shows all frameworks when none is ticked.
